\mathcal{D}={\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}\mathsf{Alg}_{\infty}
structured spaces, called
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
-ringed spaces, and we denote it
\mathsf{Top}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}}
.

Definition 8.7

(Locally
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
-ringed spaces)
.

Define the category of
Locally
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
-ringed spaces

\mathsf{Top}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}}^{loc}\subset\mathsf{Top}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}}
by

(1)

the objects of
\mathsf{Top}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}}^{loc}
are structured spaces
(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
such that each stalk of the zeroth homotopy sheaf
\pi_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})_{x}
is a local (discrete)
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
-rings with residual field
{\mathbb{R}}
.

(2)

morphisms are morphisms
(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})\to(Y,{\mathcal{O}}_{Y})
such that the map of stalks
\pi_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{X,x})\to\pi_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{Y,f(x)})
is a map of local rings.

Proposition 8.8
.

The global section functor
\Gamma
fits into an adjunction with a right adjoint
\operatorname{Spec}

\Gamma:\mathsf{Top}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}}^{loc}\ \begin{subarray}{c}\longrightarrow\\[-10.00002pt]
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}\ {\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}\mathsf{Alg}:\operatorname{Spec}

Now we define the essential image of the functor
\operatorname{Spec}
to be the (
\infty
-) category of affine derived manifolds, denoted by
\mathsf{dM}^{\mathsf{Aff}}
. We call a locally
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
-ringed space
(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
a
derived manifold
if there exists an open cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i}
of
X
such that each
(U_{i},{\mathcal{O}}_{X}|_{U_{i}})\in\mathsf{dM}^{\mathsf{Aff}}
. Denote the (
\infty
-) category of derived manifolds by
\mathsf{dM}
.

Clearly,
\mathsf{Mfd}
is a full subcategory of
\mathsf{dM}
, since for
M\in\mathsf{Mfd}
,
M\simeq\operatorname{Spec}\big(C^{\infty}(M)\big)
. In particular, we see that all smooth manifolds as derived manifolds are affine.

Example 8.9
.

The derived critical locus introduced before is a derived manifold. We have seen that derived critical locus is a derived enhancement of the classical critical locus.

Example 8.10
.

Another large class of derived manifolds are given by
differential graded manifolds
.
A
graded manifold
is defined to be a locally ringed space
\mathcal{M}=(M,{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}})
where
M
is a smooth manifold, and the structure sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}}
of
\mathcal{M}
is locally isomorphic to
{\mathcal{O}}(U)\otimes\operatorname{Sym}(V^{*})
for an open set
U\subset M
and
V
a vector space. Here
{\mathcal{O}}
denotes the structure sheaf of
M
as a smooth manifold and
\operatorname{Sym}
denotes the supercommutative tensor product. By a result of Batcher, any graded manifold
\mathcal{M}
can be realized by a graded vector bundle
E\to M
such that
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}}\simeq\Gamma(\operatorname{Sym}E^{*})
. We say a graded manifold is a
differential graded manifold
if it is equipped with a degree
+1
vector field
Q
with
Q^{2}=0
.

Example 8.11
.

Joyce showed that many constructions in producing moduli spaces, for example, moduli spaces of
J
-holomorphic curves, yields derived manifolds.

Let’s go back to our motivating example of Pontryagin-Thom construction. We want to see whether
\mathsf{dM}
solves the transversality problem in
\mathsf{Mfd}
.

Proposition 8.12

(
[
Spi08
]
)
.

The
\infty
-category
\mathsf{dM}
has the following properties:

(1)

Let
X\in\mathsf{Mfd}
and
A,B
be submanifolds of
X
, then the homotopy pull back
A\times_{X}^{h}B\in\mathsf{dM}
. We call
A\times_{X}^{h}B\in\mathsf{dM}
the
derived intersection
of
A
and
B
in
X
.

(2)

There exist an equivalence relation on the compact objects of
\mathsf{dM}
which extend cobordism relation in
\mathsf{Mfd}
, i.e. for any
X\in\mathsf{Mfd}
, there is a ring
\Omega^{der}
, which is called the
derived cobordism ring
over
X
, and a functor
i:\mathsf{Mfd}\to\mathsf{dM}
which induces a homomorphism
i_{*}:\Omega(T)\to\Omega^{der}(T)
.

(3)

we have a derived cup product formula. Let
A,B
be compact submanifolds of
X
, then we have

[A]\smile[B]=[A\cap B]

in
\Omega^{der}(X)
.

Definition 8.13
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in
\mathsf{dMfd}
. We say
f
is

(1)

a
closed (open) immersion
if the underlying map between topological spaces is a closed (open) embedding,
and
\pi_{0}
component of the morphism of sheaves
f^{-1}{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
is a surjection(equivalence).

(2)

\acute{e}
tale
if the underlying map between topological spaces is a local homeomorphism and the map
f^{-1}{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
is an equivalence of sheaves.

(3)

smooth
if for any
x\in X
, there are affine open neighborhood
U\ni x
,
V\ni f(x)
such that the restricted map
f:U\to V
is equivalent to a projection
V\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Note that this corresponds to the submersion in the classical differential geometry. In fact,
f:X\to Y
in
\mathsf{Mfd}
is smooth as morphism in
\mathsf{dM}
iff
f
is a submersion.

(4)

locally finitely presented
if, for any point
x\in X
, there are affine open neighborhood
U\ni x
,
V\ni f(x)
such that the restricted map
f:U\to V
belongs to the smallest subcategory of
\mathsf{Aff}/V
containing
V\times{\mathbb{R}}\to V
and is closed under finite limits.

Lemma 8.14
.

Let
P
be one of the properties of maps above. then

(1)

The compositions of maps with property
P
also has property
P
.

(2)

Let
f:X\to Y
have property
P
, then the base change of
f
under any morphism still has property
P
.

(3)

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in
\mathsf{dM}
, and
\{U_{i}\to Y\}
be an open cover of
Y
. Suppose that each base change
U_{i}\times_{Y}X\to U_{i}
has property
P
, then
f
has property
P
.

(4)

Let
f:X\to Y
be a smooth(
\acute{e}tale
) surjection, and
g
is any morphism. If
g\circ f
is locally finitely presented or smooth(
\acute{e}tale
), then
g
is also locally finitely presented or smooth(
\acute{e}tale
).

9.
Differential geometric
L_{\infty}
algebroids

9.1.
L_{\infty}
algebroids

Let
M
be a smooth manifold and
E=(E_{-i})_{0\leq i\leq\infty}
be a graded vector bundle over
M
. Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{M}
be the sheaf of
C^{\infty}
functions on
M
.

Definition 9.1
.

An
L_{\infty}
-algebroid
structure on
E
is a sheaf of
L_{\infty}
algebra structures on the sheaf of sections of
E
with an anchor map
\rho:E_{0}\to TM
such that

(1)

For
n=2
and one of the entry having order 1, we have the Leibniz rule

\{x,fy\}_{2}=f\{x,y\}_{2}+\rho(x)[f]y

where
x\in\Gamma(E_{0})
,
y\in\Gamma(E)
,
f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{M}
. For
n\geq 3
, all brackets
\{\cdots\}_{n}
is
{\mathcal{O}}_{M}
-linear.

(2)

E
is a dg
{\mathcal{O}}_{M}
module. In addition,
\rho\circ d^{(1)}=0
.

9.2.
dg manifolds

Definition 9.2
.

A
graded manifold
is defined to be a locally ringed space
\mathcal{M}=(M,{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}})
where
M
is a smooth manifold, and the structure sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}}
of
\mathcal{M}
is locally isomorphic to
{\mathcal{O}}(U)\otimes\operatorname{Sym}(V^{*})
for an open set
U\subset M
and
V
a vector space.

Here
{\mathcal{O}}_{M}
denotes the sheaf of
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
-functions on
M
. We have the following identification for positively graded manifolds,

Theorem 9.3

(
[
Bat79
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(M,{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}})
A positively graded manifold can be realized by a graded vector bundle
E\to M
such that
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}}\simeq\Gamma(\operatorname{Sym}E^{*})
.

Definition 9.4
.

A
dg manifold
is a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded manifolds
E=\bigoplus_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}E_{i}
with a degree 1 odd homological vector field
Q
, i.e.
Q^{2}=0
.

dg manifolds are introduced in
[
Ale+97
]
, which is called
Q manifolds
.

If we can reduced the grading from
{\mathbb{Z}}
to
{\mathbb{N}}
, then we call
E
is a
positively graded dg manifold
or
NQ
-manifold. The structure sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{E}
of
E
, i.e. functions on
E
, is isomorphic to
\Gamma(\operatorname{Sym}E^{*})
, where
\operatorname{Sym}E^{*}
is the graded symmetric algebra of
E^{*}
, i.e. if we have
e_{i_{1}},e_{i_{2}}\in E^{*}
then

e_{i_{1}}\odot e_{i_{2}}=(-1)^{|e_{i_{2}}||e_{i_{2}}|}e_{i_{2}}\odot e_{i_{1}}\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}E^{*}

Given a function
f\in\Gamma(\operatorname{Sym}E^{*})
, we say
f
is of
arity

k
and
degree

n
is a section of
\sum_{(\sum_{m=1}^{k}i_{m})=n}E^{*}_{-i_{1}}\odot\cdots E^{*}_{-i_{k}}
. Then we define vector fields to be derivations on
\Gamma(\operatorname{Sym}E^{*})
. We say a vector field
X
is of arity
n
if it maps a function
f
of arity
k
to a function
X[f]
of arity
n+k
.

Given an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid, we could construct an
NQ
-manifold by a ’dualizing’ process. Note that any functions on
E
have arity greater than or equal to 0, and it is easy to verified that any vector fields on
E
, i.e. graded derivations of
{\mathcal{O}}_{E}
, have arities
\geq-1
. Given a vector field
Q
, we can decomposed it into different arities uniquely
Q=\sum_{i\geq-1}Q^{(i)}
. To see this more clearly, let’s start with the case of Lie algebroids:

Example 9.5

(Lie algebroid)
.

First, let us consider the case of ordinary Lie algebroid. Let
E
be a Lie algebroid over
M
with anchor map
\rho:E\to TM
. On
\Gamma(E)
, we have the skew-symmetric bracket
[,]
. By shifting 1 degree, we can consider a symmetric bracket on
\Gamma(E[1])
by

\{x,y\}=[\tilde{x},\tilde{y}]

where
\tilde{x},\tilde{y}
are corresponding sections in
\Gamma(E)
if
x,y\in\Gamma(E[1])
.
Now the functions on
E[1]
are identified with
\Gamma(\operatorname{Sym}E[1]^{*})
. In order to construct
Q
, it suffices to define it on
C^{\infty}(M)
and
\Gamma(A[1]^{*})
.
First,
Q[f]\in E[1]^{*}
, we define

<Q[f],\xi>=\rho(\xi)[f]

for
\xi\in\Gamma(E)
. Next,
Q[f]\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(E[1]^{*})=\bigwedge^{2}(E[1]^{*})
. Define

<Q[\alpha],\eta\wedge\xi>=\rho(\eta)<\alpha,\xi>-\rho(\xi)<\alpha,\eta>-<\alpha,\{\eta,\xi\}>

where
\alpha,\eta,\xi\in E[1]^{*}
. Next, we extend
Q
to all
\operatorname{Sym}E[1]^{*}
by derivations. For example, let
\beta\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}E[1]^{*}
, then

\displaystyle<Q[\beta],x\wedge y\wedge z>=

\displaystyle\rho(x)<\beta,y\wedge z>-\rho(y)<\beta,x\wedge z>+\rho(z)<\beta,x\wedge y>

\displaystyle-<\beta,\{y,z\}\wedge x>+<\beta,\{x,z\}\wedge y>-<\beta,\{x,y\}\wedge z>

Let
\eta\in\operatorname{Sym}^{m}E[1]^{*},\xi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{n}E[1]^{*}
, then we define

Q[\eta\wedge\xi]=Q[\eta]\wedge\xi+(-1)^{|\eta|}\eta\wedge Q[\xi]

Let us calculate
Q^{2}
. On functions, we have,

\displaystyle<Q^{2}[f],\eta\ \wedge\xi>=

\displaystyle\rho(\eta)<Q[f],\xi>-\rho(\xi)<Q[f],\eta>-<Q[f],\{\eta,\xi\}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho(\eta)\rho(\xi)[f]-\rho(\xi)\rho(\eta)[f]-\rho(\{\eta,\xi\})[f]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(\rho(\eta)\rho(\xi)-\rho(\xi)\rho(\eta)-\rho(\{\eta,\xi\})\bigg)[f]

which vanishes due to the property of the anchor map
\rho
.
On
\operatorname{Sym}^{2}E[1]^{*}
, we have

\displaystyle<Q^{2}[\alpha],x\wedge y\wedge z>=

\displaystyle\rho(x)<Q[\alpha],y\wedge z>-\rho(y)<Q[\alpha],x\wedge z>+\rho(z)<Q[\alpha],x\wedge y>

\displaystyle-<Q[\alpha],\{y,z\}\wedge x>+<Q[\alpha],\{x,z\}\wedge y>-<Q[\alpha],\{x,y\}\wedge z>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho(x)\bigg(\rho(y)<\alpha,z>-\rho(z)<\alpha,y>-<\alpha,\{y,z\}>\bigg)

\displaystyle-\rho(y)\bigg(\rho(x)<\alpha,z>-\rho(z)<\alpha,x>-<\alpha,\{x,z\}>\bigg)

\displaystyle+\rho(z)\bigg(\rho(x)<\alpha,y>-\rho(y)<\alpha,x>-<\alpha,\{x,y\}>\bigg)

\displaystyle-\bigg(\rho(\{y,z\})<\alpha,x>-\rho(x)<\alpha,\{y,z\}>-<\alpha,\{\{y,z\},x\}>\bigg)

\displaystyle+\bigg(\rho(\{x,z\})<\alpha,y>-\rho(y)<\alpha,\{x,z\}>-<\alpha,\{\{x,z\},y\}>\bigg)

\displaystyle-\bigg(\rho(\{x,y\})<\alpha,z>-\rho(z)<\alpha,\{x,y\}>-<\alpha,\{\{x,y\},z\}>\bigg)

Using the property of anchor map and cancellations, we get

\displaystyle<Q^{2}[\alpha],x\wedge y\wedge z>=

\displaystyle<\alpha,\{\{y,z\},x\}>-<\alpha,\{\{x,z\},y\}>+<\alpha,\{\{x,y\},z\}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle<\alpha,\{\{y,z\},x\}>+\{\{z,x\},y\}+\{\{x,y\},z\}>

Hence the Jacobi identity is exactly equivalent to
Q^{2}=0
on
\operatorname{Sym}^{2}E[1]^{*}
. Since
Q
on higher arity terms are defined from its action on lower arity terms, we conclude that
Q^{2}=0
. Note that we have constructed a dga
(A^{\bullet},d)
, where
A^{\bullet}=\Gamma(M,\operatorname{Sym}^{\bullet}E[1]^{*})
and
d=Q
.

Example 9.6

(Poisson manifolds)
.

Recall a Poisson manifold is a smooth manifold
M
equipped with a Poisson bracket
\{-,-\}
satisfies Leibniz rule
\{fg,h\}=f\{g,h\}+g\{f,h\}
and puts a Lie algebra structure on
C^{\infty}(M)
. Note that
\{f,-\}:C^{\infty}(M)\to C^{\infty}(M)
is a derivation, then we can find a
\pi\in\wedge^{2}TM
such that
\{f,g\}=\pi(df,dg)
.

Next, we shall look at the equivalence between
L_{\infty}
algebroids and NQ-manifolds over
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
-manifolds, which is given by Voronov
[
Vor10
]
.

Theorem I

(
[
Vor10
]
)
.

Let
M
be a
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
manifolds. There is an one-to-one correspondence between
L_{\infty}
-algebroids and NQ-manifolds over
M
.

Proof.

(1) Constructing a
NQ
-manifold from an
L_{\infty}
algebroid.

Suppose now we are given an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid structure on a dg vector bundle
\{E_{-i},d\}_{i\geq 0}
. First notice that for any vector
X
we can decompose
X
into components of different arities
X=\sum_{i=-1}^{\infty}X^{(i)}
, where each
X^{(i)}
is of the homogeneous arity
i
. Since
Q
is of degree 1, the
-1
arity part which is the contraction with
\Gamma(E_{-1})
vanishes. Hence
Q=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}Q^{(i)}
.

First, the arity 0 part is given by the dual of differential, i.e.
<Q^{(0)}[\alpha],x>=(-1)^{|\alpha|}<\alpha,d^{(i)}(x)>
, where
\alpha\in\Gamma(E^{*}_{-i+1}),x\in\Gamma(E_{-i})
.

By analogue of formula for ordinary Lie algebroid, we define

<Q^{(1)}[f],\xi>=\rho(\xi)[f]

<Q^{(1)}[\alpha],\eta\odot\xi>=\rho(\eta)<\alpha,\xi>-\rho(\xi)<\alpha,\eta>-<\alpha,\{\eta,\xi\}_{2}>

and extend the action to higher order terms by derivation. (see previous example)

For arities
i\geq 2
, since all
\{\cdots\}_{i}
’s are
\mathcal{O}_{M}
-linear, we define

Q^{(i)}=\{\cdots\}_{i}^{*}=E^{*}\to\operatorname{Sym}^{i+1}(E^{*})
for
i\geq 2
. It follows directly that

Q^{(i)}
’s are
\mathcal{O}_{M}
linear for
i\geq 2
.

Next, we want to verify that
Q
is homological. Clearly
Q
is of degree 1 by our construction. Expanding
Q^{2}
gives

Q^{2}=Q^{(0)}\circ Q^{(0)}+\big(Q^{(0)}\circ Q^{(1)}+Q^{(1)}\circ Q^{(0)}\big)+Q^{{(1)}}\circ Q^{{(1)}}+\cdots=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{i+j=k}Q^{(i)}\circ Q^{(j)}

Let us look at first few terms. First, we have
Q^{(0)}\circ Q^{(0)}=0
since
d^{2}=0
. Next, let us consider
\big(Q^{(0)}\circ Q^{(1)}+Q^{(1)}\circ Q^{(0)}\big)
.j

\displaystyle<Q^{(0)}\circ Q^{(1)}(\alpha),x\odot y>=

\displaystyle<Q^{(1)}(\alpha),(-1)^{|\alpha|}d(x\odot y)>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle<Q^{(1)}(\alpha),(-1)^{|\alpha|}(dx\odot y+(-1)^{|x|}x\odot dy)>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{|\alpha|}\Big(\rho(dx)<\alpha,y>-\rho(y)<\alpha,dx>-<\alpha,\{dx,y\}>\Big)

\displaystyle+(-1)^{|\alpha|+|x|}\Big(\rho(x)<\alpha,dy>-\rho(dy)<\alpha,x>-<\alpha,\{x,dy\}>\Big)

+Q^{(1)}\circ Q^{(0)}\big)
exact means
On the other hand,

\displaystyle<Q^{(1)}\circ Q^{(0)}(\alpha),x\odot y>=

\displaystyle\rho(x)<Q^{(0)}(\alpha),y>-\rho(y)<Q^{(0)}(\alpha),x>-<Q^{(0)}(\alpha),\{x,y\}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{|\alpha|}\Big(\rho(x)<\alpha,dy>-\rho(y)<\alpha,dx>-<\alpha,d\{x,y\}>\Big)

Note that
|\alpha|=|x|+|y|-1
. Since the anchor map is nontrivial only on
\Gamma(E_{-1})
. Combined with the fact that
\rho\circ d=0
, we have,

\displaystyle<\big(Q^{(0)}\circ Q^{(1)}+Q^{(1)}\circ Q^{(0)}\big)(\alpha),x\odot y>=

\displaystyle(-1)^{|x|+|y|}<\alpha,\{x,dy\}>+(-1)^{|y|}<\alpha,\{x,dy\}>

\displaystyle+(-1)^{|x|+|y|}<\alpha,\{x,y\}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{|x|+|y|}<\alpha,d\{x,y\}+(-1)^{|x|}\{dx,y\}+\{x,dy\}>

Hence
(Q^{(0)}\circ Q^{(1)}+Q^{(1)}\circ Q^{(0)}=0
follows from the Leibniz rule
d\{x,y\}+(-1)^{|x|}\{dx,y\}+\{x,dy\}=0
. It follows that all higher arities term of
Q^{2}
are 0 due to general Jacobi identities.

(2) Constructing an
L_{\infty}
algebroid from a
NQ
-manifold.

Let
E=(E_{-i})_{i\geq 1}
be an
NQ
-manifold over
M
. We want to construct an
L_{\infty}
algebroid structure on
E
. First, notice that given any section
e\in\Gamma(E)
, then we can identify it as a constant vector field
\partial_{e}
on
E
by letting
\partial_{e}(\epsilon)=<\epsilon,e>
for
\epsilon\in\Gamma(E^{*})
. Note that here we mean
\partial_{\alpha}
is a derivation on
\Gamma\big(\operatorname{Sym}E^{*}\big)
. We denote this map by
i:\Gamma(E)\to\mathfrak{X}_{const}(E)
, where
\mathfrak{X}_{const}(E)
denotes the vector fields on
E
which is constant on the fiber. Let
(x_{i})
,
(\phi_{j}^{k})_{j}
be local coordinates of
M
and
E_{-k}^{*}
’s. Then locally we can write any vector field
X
as

X=\sum_{i=1}^{n}v^{i}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{\dim E_{-k}}f_{j}^{k}(x,\phi)\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi_{j}^{k}}

Let
\pi
be the operator which projects any vector field
X
to
X^{\prime}
which is constant on fiber and equals to
X
on the zero locus of the fibers of
E^{*}
. Hence locally,
\pi
looks like

\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}v^{i}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{\dim E_{-k}}f_{j}^{k}(x,\phi)\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi_{j}^{k}}\bigg)\mapsto\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}v^{i}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{\dim E_{-k}}\tilde{f}_{j}^{k}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi_{j}^{k}}\bigg)

where
\tilde{f}_{j}^{k}(x)={f}_{j}^{k}(x,0)
.
By previous identification, we can regard the image of
\pi
as sections of
\Gamma(E)
. In fact,
i^{-1}\circ\pi:\mathfrak{X}(E)\to\Gamma(E)
gives the desired map.

Hence, we define the anchor map
\rho:\Gamma(E_{-1})\to\Gamma(TM)
by
<Q[f],x>=\rho(x)[f]
. Denote the one bracket
\{-\}_{1}
by
d
. Define
d\alpha=i^{-1}\pi([Q,\partial_{\alpha}])
for
\alpha\in\Gamma(E)
. For higher brackets, we use Voronov’s higher derived bracket formula and define

\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}_{n}=i^{-1}\circ\pi\Big([\cdots,[[Q,\partial_{\alpha_{1}}],\partial_{\alpha_{2}}],\cdots]\Big)

By the property of derived bracket, we have
J^{n}_{Q}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})=\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\}_{n,Q^{2}}
where
\{-\}_{\cdots,Q^{2}}
is the
n
-th derived bracket induced by
Q^{2}
. Since
Q^{2}=0
, all Jacobiator vanish and hence we get a
L_{\infty}
algebroid structure.

∎

Remark 9.7
.

Note that there exists a map
\rho^{*}\circ d_{dR}:C^{\infty}(M)\to\Gamma(T^{*}M)\to\Gamma(E^{*}_{-1})
. Hence, we have the following complex

\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Q^{(0)}}}{{\longleftarrow}}\Gamma(E^{*}_{(-2)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Q^{(0)}}}{{\longleftarrow}}\Gamma(E^{*}_{(-1)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho^{*}\circ d_{dR}}}{{\longleftarrow}}C^{\infty}(M)

9.3.
Lie algebroid representations

Definition 9.8
.

Let
A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho}}{{\to}}TM
be a Lie algebroid over
M
. A representation of
A
is a pair
(E,\nabla)
such that
E
is a vector bundle over
M
and
\nabla:\Gamma(A)\times\Gamma(E)\to\Gamma(E)
is a flat
A-
connection on
E
.
Let
\Omega(A,E)
be the space of
E
-valued differential forms over
\Omega(A)
, the representation
(E,\nabla)
is equivalent to a square zero differential
d_{\nabla}
.

Note that the differential
d_{\nabla}
is given by the usual Koszul formula

\displaystyle d_{\nabla}(\omega)(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n+1})=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}(-1)^{i+1}\nabla_{\alpha_{i}}\omega(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\hat{\alpha}_{i},\cdots\alpha_{n+1})

\displaystyle+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n+1}(-1)^{i+j}\omega([\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}],\alpha_{1},\cdots,\hat{\alpha}_{i},\cdots,\hat{\alpha}_{j},\cdots,\alpha_{n+1})

9.4.
Lie algebroid cohomology

Definition 9.9
.

The Lie algebroid cohomology groups
H^{\bullet}(A,E)
with values in representation
(E,\nabla)
of the cohomology groups associated to the complex
\big(\Omega(A,E),d_{\nabla}\big)
.

Proposition 9.10
.

H^{1}(A,E)=E^{A}=\{x\in E\ |\ \nabla_{a}x=0\ \forall a\in A\}
.

Proposition 9.11
.

H^{2}(A,M)=\operatorname{Der}(A,E)/\operatorname{InnDer}(A,E)
.

Proposition 9.12
.

Given a Lie algebroid
A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho}}{{\to}}TM
with a representation
(E,\nabla)
, with an
(n+2)
-cocycle
\omega_{n+2}\in\Omega^{n+2}(A,E)
where
n\geq 1
, then we can associate them a
L_{\infty}
-algebroid with only nontrivial terms concentrated in degree
0
and
-n
with zero differential. Conversely, for any
L_{\infty}
-algebroid with previous properties, we can construct a Lie algebroid with representations, i.e. a quadrupole
(A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho}}{{\to}}TM,E,\nabla,\omega_{n+2}).

Proof.

(
\Rightarrow
) Suppose we are given an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid
\big((A_{-i})_{i\geq 1},\rho\big)
with only nontrivial terms
A_{-n}
and
A_{-1}
. Define
A=A_{-1}
with anchor
\rho:A\to TM
. Jacobi identity holds since
d
is trivial, hence
A\to TM
forms a Lie algebroid. Next, we define
E=A_{-n}
as a vector bundle over
M
. We can construct a representation
\nabla:\Gamma(A)\otimes\Gamma(E)\to\Gamma(E)
through the Lie bracket. In fact, define
\nabla_{a}s=[a,s]
, where
[-,-]
is the 2-bracket in the
L_{\infty}
structure. From the Leibniz rule of the anchor map, we get

\nabla_{a}(fs)=[a,fs]=f[a,s]+\rho(a)(f)s=f\nabla_{a}(s)+L_{\rho(a)}(f)(s)

for
s\in\Gamma(E),a\in\Gamma(a),f\in C^{\infty}(M)
,
and similarly

\nabla_{fa}=[fa,s]=f[a,s]=f\nabla_{a}(s)

Hence
(E,\nabla)
gives a representation of
A
. Next, let us look at the
(n+2)
-bracket
l_{n+2}:\Gamma(A)^{\otimes(n+2)}\to\Gamma(E)
. We want to construct a
(n+2)
cycle from
l_{n+2}

The homotopy Jacobi identity reads

\sum_{{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}\atop{i+j=n+3}}\sum_{\sigma\in UnShuff(i,j)}\chi(\sigma,v_{1},\cdots,v_{n})(-1)^{i(j-1)}l_{j}\left(l_{i}\left(v_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,v_{\sigma(i)}\right),v_{\sigma(i+1)},\cdots,v_{\sigma(n+2)}\right)=0

∎

where the only nontrivial
l_{i}
’s are
i=2,n+2
. Note that all terms
v_{i}
’s are of degree 0. Hence, we can break the summation into
(n+2,1)
-unshuffle
\sigma
and
(2,n+1)
-unshuffle
\tau
.

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau}\chi(\tau)l_{n+2}([v_{\tau(1)},v_{\tau(2)}],v_{\tau(3)},\cdots,v_{\tau(n+3)})

\displaystyle+\sum_{\sigma}\chi(\sigma)[l_{n+2}(v_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,v_{\sigma(n+2)}),v_{\sigma(n+3)}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i<j}(-1)^{i+j+1}l_{n+2}([v_{i},v_{j}],v_{1},\cdots,\hat{v}_{i},\cdots,\hat{v}_{j},\cdots,v_{n+3})

\displaystyle+\sum_{i}(-1)^{n+3-i}(-1)^{n+2}[l_{n+2}(v_{1},\cdots,v_{i-1},v_{i+1},\cdots,v_{n+3}),v_{i}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{i<j}(-1)^{i+j}l_{n+2}([v_{i},v_{j}],v_{1},\cdots,\hat{v}_{i},\cdots,\hat{v}_{j},\cdots,v_{n+3})

\displaystyle-\sum_{i}(-1)^{i+1}[l_{n+2}(v_{1},\cdots,v_{i-1},v_{i+1},\cdots,v_{n+3}),v_{i}]

\displaystyle=-d_{\nabla}l_{n+2}(v_{1},\cdots,v_{n+3})

Here we used the fact that there are
(n+3)

(n+2,1)
unshuffles each of which has sign
(-1)^{n+3-i}
. Similarly, there are
n+3

(2,n+1)
unshuffles each of which has sign
(-1)^{i+j+1}
. Hence, we have shown that
l_{n+2}
is an
(n+2)
cocycle.

(\Leftarrow)

Suppose now we are given
(A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho}}{{\to}}TM,E,\nabla,\omega_{n+2})
. We construct a dg
O_{M}
-module
F=\bigoplus_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}F_{i}
with only two nontrivial terms
F_{0}=A
and
F_{n}=E
with zero differential. For brackets, we extend the 2-bracket comes from the Lie algebroid
A
and the cocycle
\omega_{n+2}
. In fact, we can extend
[-,-]:\Gamma(F_{i})\otimes\Gamma(F_{j})\to\Gamma(F_{i+j})
by

[a,x]=\nabla_{a}(x)=-[x,a]

and

[x,y]=0

for
a\in\Gamma(a)
,
x,y\in\Gamma(E)
. Define
l_{n+2}=\omega_{n+2}
and
l_{i}=0
for
i\not=n+2,2
. Thus, we get an
L_{\infty}
structure on
F
.

Proposition 9.13
.

Homotopy equivalent
L_{\infty}
-algebroids of the forms in the previous proposition give cohomologous cocycles.

9.5.
Deformations and obstructions

We want to study the deformation of a Lie algebroid
A=(A,\rho,[-,-])
.

Definition 9.14
.

A multiderivation of degree
n
on a vector bundle
E\to M
is defined to be a skew-symmetric multilinear map
D:\Gamma(E)^{\otimes(n+1)}\to\Gamma(E)
which is a derivation in each entry. Hence, for any
D\in\operatorname{Der}^{n}(A)
, we have an associated map
\sigma_{D}:\Gamma^{\otimes n}\to\Gamma(TM)
which is called the symbol of
D
and satisfies

D(s_{1},\cdots,fs_{n})=fD(s_{1},\cdots,s_{n})+\sigma_{D}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{n-1})(f)s_{n}

Consider the space of multiderivations
\operatorname{Der}^{n}(A)
of degree
n
on
A
, we can form a cochain complex
\big(\operatorname{Der}^{\bullet}(A),\delta\big)
, where the differential
\delta
is given by the usual Koszul formula. Note that
\operatorname{Der}^{\bullet-1}(A)\simeq C^{\bullet}_{def}(A)
, where
C^{\bullet}_{def}(A)
is the deformation complex associated to
A
.

On
\operatorname{Der}^{\bullet}(A)
, we can define the Gerstenhaber bracket
[D_{1},D_{2}]=(-1)^{pq}D_{1}\circ D_{2}-D_{2}\circ D_{1}
where

D_{2}\circ D_{1}(s_{0},\cdots,s_{p+q})=\sum_{\tau}(-1)^{\tau}D_{2}\big(D_{1}(s_{\tau(0)},\cdots,s_{\tau(p)}),s_{\tau(p+1)},\cdots,s_{\tau(p+q)}\big)

where the sum is over all
(p+1,q)
shuffles for
D_{1}\in\operatorname{Der}^{p}(E),D_{2}\in\operatorname{Der}^{q}(E),s_{i}\in\Gamma(E)
.

Proposition 9.15
.

The Gerstenhaber bracket makes the cochain complex
\big(\operatorname{Der}^{\bullet}(A),\delta\big)
a differential graded Lie algebra.

Note that the Lie bracket
m\in\operatorname{Der}^{1}(A)
, hence we can write the differential
\delta
as
\delta=[m,-]
where the bracket is the Gerstenhaber bracket. Since
\operatorname{Der}^{\bullet-1}(A)\simeq C^{\bullet}_{def}(A)
, we have
H^{\bullet}\big(\operatorname{Der}^{\bullet}(A)\big)\simeq H^{\bullet+1}_{def}(A)
as a differential graded Lie algebra with zero differential.

Given a Lie algebroid
A=(A,\rho,m)
, a deformation of
A
is a one parameter family of Lie algebroid over an interval
I
, denoted
A_{t}=(A,\rho_{t},m_{t})
varying smoothly with respect to
t
such that
A_{0}=(A,\rho,m)
. By Crainic and Moerdijk
[
CM04
]
, any deformation gives a cocycle
c_{0}\in C^{2}_{def}(A)
, whose cohomology class only depends on the equivalent class of deformations.

Recall, the Jacobi identity reads
[m,m]=0
. First, let us consider
m^{\prime}=m+\phi
, where
\psi:\Gamma(E)^{\otimes 2}\to\Gamma E
is a skew-symmetric bilinear map. Since we require
\rho^{\prime}=\rho+\psi
satisfies the Leibniz rule, we have

\displaystyle m^{\prime}(\alpha,f\beta)=

\displaystyle fm^{\prime}(\alpha,\beta)+\rho^{\prime}(\alpha)(f)\beta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f\big(m+\phi\big)(\alpha,\beta)+\big((\rho+\psi)(\alpha)f\big)\beta

by deleting the Leibniz rule for
m
and
\rho
, we get

\phi(\alpha,f\beta)=f\phi(\alpha,\beta)+\psi(\alpha)(f)\beta

which says that
\phi
is a derivation with symbol
\psi
, i.e.
\phi\in\operatorname{Der}^{1}(A),\sigma_{\phi}=\psi
. Note that
\phi
determines
\psi
uniquely from the Leibniz rule.

In order for
m^{\prime}
to satisfy the Jacobi identity, we have,

\displaystyle[m^{\prime},m^{\prime}]=

\displaystyle[m+\phi,m+\phi]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[m,m]+[m,\phi]+[\phi,m]+[\phi,\phi]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\big(\delta\phi-\frac{1}{2}[\phi,\phi]\big)

Hence we simply need
\delta\phi-\frac{1}{2}[\phi,\phi]=0
. A linearization of this equation is
\delta\phi=0
, which says that
\phi
is a cocycle. In this case, we call
\phi
an infinitesimal deformation.

Next, consider a formal one parameter deformation
m_{t}=m+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\phi_{i}t^{i}
. We must have
[m_{t},m_{t}]=0
. Expand the bracket, we have

[m_{t},m_{t}]=t[m,\phi_{1}]+t^{2}\big([\phi_{1},\phi_{1}]+[m,\phi_{2}]+[\phi_{2},m]\big)+O(t^{3})

Hence we have
[m,\phi_{1}]=\delta\phi_{1}=0
, which say that
\phi_{1}
is a cocycle. The second term gives
\delta\phi_{2}-\frac{1}{2}[\phi_{1},\phi_{1}]=0
. Note that
\delta\big(\frac{1}{2}[\phi_{1},\phi_{1}]\big)=\frac{1}{2}[\delta\phi_{1},\phi_{1}]\big)-\frac{1}{2}[\phi_{1},\delta\phi_{1}]\big)=0
since
\delta
is a graded derivation on
\operatorname{Der}^{\bullet}(A)
. Hence,
\frac{1}{2}[\phi_{1},\phi_{1}]\big)\in\operatorname{Der}^{2}(A)
is a cocycle. Therefore, the equation gives that
\frac{1}{2}[\phi_{1},\phi_{1}]\big)
has to be a coboundary, i.e.
[\frac{1}{2}[\phi_{1},\phi_{1}]\big)]=0\in H^{2}\big(\operatorname{Der}^{\bullet}(A)\big)
. Hence, the space
H^{2}\big(\operatorname{Der}^{\bullet}(A)\simeq H^{3}_{def}(A)
is the space of
obstructions
to form a one parameter family of deformations with first order term
\phi_{1}
.

Now suppose that we have shown that
m_{t}=m+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\phi_{i}t^{i}
satisfies Jacobi identity up to order
n
, i.e. all terms in the expansion.

Part III
Homotopy theory of derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids

We are going to study the homotopy theory of Lie
\infty
-groupoids over various derived geometric spaces. Some of these categories have homotopical structure, i.e.
\infty
-categories or model categories, which permits us to work homotopically. Others do not have good homotopy theory, and even worse than that, they usually lack of many limits, for example, pullbacks along arbitrary morphisms. Hence, we are breaking the derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids in the following two kinds of categories:

(1)

Homotopical categories with all finite homotopy limits, which includes:

•

\mathsf{dMfd}
, the category of derived manifolds.

•

\mathsf{dAnSp}_{k}
, the category of derived
k
-analytic spaces.

(2)

Categories without all finite limits:

•

\mathsf{dBan}
, the category of derived Banach manifolds.

We will construct explicit homotopy theory on these categories, which breaks down to the above two cases.

For the first case, we will show that they form
homotopy descent categories
, and derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids these categories have
category of fibrant objects
structure.

For the second case, though we don’t have all finite limits, we can take advantage of the Yoneda embedding
\mathsf{y}:{\mathsf{C}}\to\mathsf{PSh}({\mathsf{C}})
which naturally extends to
\mathsf{y}:s{\mathsf{C}}\to s\mathsf{PSh}({\mathsf{C}})
, then compute limits in
s\mathsf{PSh}({\mathsf{C}})
and show representabilities. Hence, we can equip these categories an
incomplete category of fibrant objects
structures.

10.
\infty
-presheaves and
\infty
-stacks

10.1.
Simplicial presheaves

10.1.1.
Grothendieck pretopology

Let
{\mathsf{C}}
be a category, we want to define presheaves and sheaves on
{\mathsf{C}}
. Recall that, for a topological space
S
, we define sheaves on
S
using gluing data from an open cover of the topological space. Hence, we want to define a ’topology’ on a category, which is the Grothendieck topology.

Definition 10.1
.

Let
{\mathsf{C}}
be a category with coproducts, and a terminal object
\ast
. A
Grothendieck pretopology

\mathcal{T}
on
{\mathsf{C}}
is a collection of morphisms called
covers
(or
covering families
) satisfies:

(1)

each object
X\in{\mathsf{C}}
has a collection of covers
\{U_{i}\to X\}
;

(2)

isomorphisms are covers;

(3)

pullbacks of covers are covers;

(4)

composition of covers are covers;

(5)

the canonical map
X\to\ast
is a cover.

For simplicity, we will simply say pretopology for Grothendieck pretopology if there is no confusion. Grothendieck pretopology is also called
basis for a Grothendieck topology
. As the name suggests, each Grothendieck pretopology generates a
Grothendieck topology
.

Definition 10.2
.

A
Grothendieck topology

\tau
on a category
{\mathsf{C}}
consists of the following data:

(1)

for any object
x\in{\mathsf{C}}
, there is a family
\mathsf{cov}(x)
of covering sieves over
x
, i.e. subfunctors of the representable functor
\mathsf{y}_{x}=\operatorname{Hom}(-,x)
.

(2)

(Stability under base change) For any morphism
f:x\to y
in
{\mathsf{C}}
and
u\in\mathsf{cov}(X)
, we have
f^{*}(u)=u\times_{\mathsf{y}_{x}}\mathsf{y}_{y}
.

(3)

(Local character condition) Let
x\in{\mathsf{C}}
,
u\in\mathsf{cov}(x)
, and
v
be any sieve on
X
. If for all
y\in{\mathsf{C}}
and
f\in u(y)
, we have
f^{*}(v)\in\mathsf{cov}(y)
, then
v\in\mathsf{cov}(x)
.

Given a Grothendieck pretopology
\mathcal{T}
, the Grothendieck topology
\tau
generated from
\mathcal{T}
is that for which a sieve
{S_{i}\to U}
is covering if it contains a covering family of morphisms.
We call a category with Grothendieck topology a
(Grothendieck) site
. For simplicity, we will also call a category with pretopology a site, by which we mean the site generated by the pretopology.

Now consider a category with pretopology, we can define the category of presheaves
\mathsf{PSh}({\mathsf{C}})
on
{\mathsf{C}}
consists of contravariant functors
{\mathsf{C}}\to\mathsf{Set}
.

Definition 10.3
.

A presheaf
F\in\mathsf{PSh}({\mathsf{C}})
is a sheaf if
F(X)
is the limit of the diagram

F(U)\rightrightarrows F(U\times_{X}U)

.

We denote the category of sheaves on
{\mathsf{C}}
by
\mathsf{Sh}({\mathsf{C}})
. The inclusion functor
\iota:\mathsf{Sh}({\mathsf{C}})\to\mathsf{PSh}({\mathsf{C}})
has an exact left adjoint functor,
s:\mathsf{PSh}({\mathsf{C}})\to\mathsf{Sh}({\mathsf{C}})
which is called the
associated sheaf functor
(or
sheafification functor
).
Now we define
s\mathsf{PSh}({\mathsf{C}})
to be the category of simplicial objects in
\mathsf{PSh}({\mathsf{C}})
. Note that we can also define
s\mathsf{PSh}({\mathsf{C}})
as contravariant functor from
{\mathsf{C}}
to
s\mathsf{Set}
. We can endow
s\mathsf{PSh}({\mathsf{C}})
a model structure by the following data:

(1)

A morphism
f:F\to G
in
s\mathsf{PSh}({\mathsf{C}})
is called a
global fibration
if, for any
x\in{\mathsf{C}}
, the induced morphism
F(x)\to G(x)
is a Kan fibration of simplicial sets.

(2)

A morphism
f:F\to G
in
s\mathsf{PSh}({\mathsf{C}})
is called a
global equivalence
if, for any
x\in{\mathsf{C}}
, the induced morphism
F(x)\to G(x)
is a weak equivalence of simplicial sets.

(3)

The cofibrations are defined through the standard lifting property.

We call this model structure the
global model structure
(
[
Jar87
]
) for simplicial presheaves.

Given a simplicial presheaf
F:{\mathsf{C}}^{\operatorname{op}}\to s\mathsf{Set}
, we define a presheaf
\pi^{\mathsf{PSh}}_{0}(F):C^{\operatorname{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
by sending any
x\in{\mathsf{C}}
to
\pi_{0}(F(x))
. Similarly, for any
x\in{\mathsf{C}}
and any 0-simplex
s\in F(X)_{0}
we define presheaves of groups on
{\mathsf{C}}/x

\pi^{\mathsf{PSh}}_{i}(F,s):({\mathsf{C}}/x)^{\operatorname{op}}\to\mathsf{Grp}

by sending
f:y\to x
to
\pi_{i}(F(y),f^{*}(s))
.

Definition 10.4
.

Given a simplicial presheaf
F:{\mathsf{C}}^{\operatorname{op}}\to s\mathsf{Set}
, we define the
homotopy sheaves
of
F
to be the sheafification of
\pi^{\mathsf{PSh}}_{0}(F)
and
\pi^{\mathsf{PSh}}_{i}(F,s)
for
i\geq 1
, which we denote by
\pi_{0}(F)
and
\pi_{i}(F,s)
respectively.

Using homotopy sheaves, we can refine the global model structure as follows:

(1)

A morphism
f:F\to G
in
s\mathsf{PSh}({\mathsf{C}})
is called a
local equivalence
if it satisfies:

•

The induced morphism
\pi_{0}(F)\to\pi_{0}(G)
is an isomorphism of sheaves.

•

For any
x\in{\mathsf{C}}
, any
s\in F(x)_{0}
, and
i\geq 1
, the induced morphism
\pi_{i}(F,s)\to\pi_{i}(F^{\prime},f(s))
is an isomorphism of sheaves on
{\mathsf{C}}/x
.

(2)

The
local cofibration
is defined as the same as the global cofibration.

(3)

The fibrations are defined through the standard lifting property.

This model structure is called the
local model structure
for simplicial presheaves. In
[
DHI04
]
, we have an easy characterization of fibrant object in the local model structure.

Definition 10.5
.

Let
x\in{\mathsf{C}}
, we define a
hypercovering
of
x
to be a simplicial presheaf
H
with a morphism
H\to x
such that,

(1)

For each
n
,
H_{n}
is a disjoint union of representable presheaves.

(2)

For each
n
, the morphism of presheaves

H_{n}\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\Delta[n],H)\to\operatorname{Hom}(\partial\Delta[n],H)\times_{\operatorname{Hom}(\partial\Delta[n],x)}\operatorname{Hom}(\Delta[n],x)

Let
f\in s\mathsf{PSh}({\mathsf{C}})
and
H\to x
a hypercovering of
x\in{\mathsf{C}}
, we can construct an augmented cosimplicial diagram

F(x)\to([n]\mapsto F(H_{n}))

Theorem 10.6

(
[
DHI04
]
)
.

An object
F\in s\mathsf{PSh}({\mathsf{C}})
is fibrant in the local model structure if and only if it satisfies:

(1)

For any
x\in{\mathsf{C}}
,
F(x)
is fibrant.

(2)

For any
x\in{\mathsf{C}}
and any hypercovering
H\to x
, the natural morphism

F(x)\to\operatorname{Hocolim}_{[n]\in\Delta}F(H_{n})

is an equivalence of simplicial sets.

The first condition is rather anodyne, whereas the second one is similar to the condition to be a sheaf. In fact, if
F
is an ordinary presheaf (considered as a constant simplicial presheaf), then the second condition simplifies exactly to the sheaf condition. We will call an object
F\in s\mathsf{PSh}({\mathsf{C}})
satisfying the second condition above a
stack
over
{\mathsf{C}}
, which are also called
\infty
-stack
or
hypercomplete
\infty
-sheaves
in
[
Lur09
]
, and
stacks
in
[
TV02
]
. We call the homotopy category
\mathsf{Ho}(s\mathsf{PSh}({\mathsf{C}}))
the
homotopy category of hypersheaves
on the site
({\mathsf{C}},\tau)
.

10.1.2.
(\infty,1)
-Grothendieck topology

In this section, we will generalize Grothendieck topology to a more general setting, which consider the underlying category has already had some homotopical structure. Roughly speaking, we will define a ’simplicial’ Grothendieck topology on a ’simplicial’ category.

We define an
(
\infty
,1)-Grothendieck topology

\mathcal{T}
on an
\infty
-category
\mathsf{C}
consists of data such that for any object
c
in
\mathsf{C}
, there is a collection of sieves, called
covering sieves
, such that

(1)

For each
c\in\mathsf{C}
, the overcategory
\mathsf{C}/c
is a covering sieve, i.e. the monomorphism
\operatorname{Id}:\mathsf{y}(c)\to\mathsf{y}(c)
is a cover.

(2)

Pullback of a covering sieve is a sieve.

(3)

For a covering sieve
s
on
c\in\mathsf{C}
and
t
any sieve on
c
, if
f^{*}t
is a covering sieve for all
f\in s
, then
t
is a covering sieve.

Equivalently, we have the following characterization

Theorem 10.7

(
[
Lur09a
]
)
.

The data of an
(\infty,1)
-Grothendieck topology is given by the data of an ordinary Grothendieck topology on
\mathsf{Ho}({\mathsf{C}})
. Consider a property
\mathbf{P}
of morphisms in
\mathsf{Ho}({\mathsf{C}})
, we say a morphism
f
in
{\mathsf{C}}
satisfies
\mathbf{P}
if its image in
\mathsf{Ho}({\mathsf{C}})
satisfies
\mathbf{P}
.

Definition 10.8
.

An
\infty
-category equipped with an (
\infty
,1)-Grothendieck topology
\mathcal{T}
is called an
(\infty,1)
-site
.

Definition 10.9

(
[
Lur09a
]
)
.

A simplicial object in an
\infty
-category
\mathsf{C}
is defined to be an
(\infty,1)
-functor
X:\Delta^{op}\to\mathsf{C}
. We denoted the corresponding
\infty
-category to be
\mathsf{C}^{\Delta^{op}}=\operatorname{Fun_{\infty}}(\Delta^{op},\mathsf{C})
.

10.1.3.
\infty
-Yoneda embedding

Let
\mathsf{C}
be an
\infty
-category, then an
(\infty,1)
-presheaf
on
\mathsf{C}
is an
(\infty,1)
-functor
F:\mathsf{C}^{op}\to\mathsf{Grpd_{\infty}}
. Denote the
\infty
-category of
(\infty,1)
sheaves on
\mathsf{C}
by

\mathsf{PSh_{\infty}}(\mathsf{C})
given by

\mathsf{PSh_{\infty}}(\mathsf{C})=\operatorname{Fun_{\infty}}(\mathsf{C}^{op},\mathsf{Grpd_{\infty}})

Definition 10.10
.

((
\infty,1)
-Yoneda embedding)
Let
\mathsf{C}
be an
\infty
category. We define the
(
\infty,1)
-Yoneda embedding
of
\mathsf{C}
to be the
(\infty,1)
-functor

\mathsf{y}:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{PSh_{\infty}}(\mathsf{C})

by
\mathsf{y}(X)=\operatorname{Map}_{\mathsf{C}}(-,X):\mathsf{C}^{op}\to\mathsf{Grpd_{\infty}}
. This map is fully faithful.

Definition 10.11
.

A
sieve
in an
\infty
-category
\mathsf{C}
is a full sub-
\infty
-category
\mathsf{D}
such that
\mathsf{D}
is closed under precomposing morphisms in
\mathsf{C}
. A
sieve
on an object
c\in\mathsf{C}
is a sieve in
\mathsf{C}/c
. This is equivalent to say a sieve on
c
is an equivalent class of monomorphisms
\{U\to\mathsf{y}(c)\}
in
\mathsf{PSh_{\infty}}(\mathsf{C})
.

Let
s
be a sieve, and
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{C}}(d,c)
, we define the
pullback sieve
f^{*}s
on
d
to be all morphisms to
d
such that for any
g\in f^{*}s
,
f\circ g
is equivalent to a morphism in
s
.
Denote the
\infty
-category of
(\infty,1)
-presheaves on
\mathsf{dM}
by
\mathsf{PSh_{\infty}}(\mathsf{dM})
. Note that
\mathsf{PSh_{\infty}}(\mathsf{dM})
is naturally simplicially enriched.

Proposition 10.12

(
[
Lur09a
]
)
.

Let
{\mathsf{C}}
be an
\infty
-category. Consider
X\in{\mathsf{C}}
, denote
\mathsf{y}_{X}\in\mathsf{PSh_{\infty}}({\mathsf{C}})
defined by

\mathsf{y}_{X}(U):=\operatorname{Map}_{{\mathsf{C}}}(U,X)\in\mathsf{Grpd_{\infty}}

Then for any
(\infty,1)
-presheaf
F
on
{\mathsf{C}}
, there is a canonical isomorphism of
\infty
-groupoids

F(X)\simeq\operatorname{Map}_{\mathsf{PSh_{\infty}}({\mathsf{C}})}(\mathsf{y}_{X},F)

Then we have an embedding
\mathsf{y}:{\mathsf{C}}\to\mathsf{PSh_{\infty}}({\mathsf{C}})

Denote the
\infty
-category of simplicial
(\infty,1)
-presheaves on
\mathsf{dM}
by
s\mathsf{PSh_{\infty}}(\mathsf{dM})
.

11.
Derived Lie
\infty
-groupoid

We want to consider the
\infty
-groupoid objects in the
\infty
-category of derived manifolds
\mathsf{dM}
. First, we want to study simplicial derived manifolds. For simplicity, we denote
\mathsf{dM}
for either one of
\mathsf{dMfd}
,
\mathsf{dAnSp}
, and
\mathsf{dBan}
, and call them derived manifolds unless otherwise specified.

Let
(\mathsf{dM},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
be the category of derived manifold equipped with smooth surjection pretopology. Let
X_{\bullet}:\Delta^{op}\to\mathsf{dM}
be a simplicial object in derived manifolds.

Definition 11.1
.

A simplicial map
p:X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
is a
Kan fibration
if for each horn inclusion
\Lambda^{i}[n]\subset\Delta[n]
, the matching map

X(\Delta[n])\longrightarrow X(\Lambda^{i}[n])\times_{Y(\Lambda^{i}[n])}Y(\Delta[n])

is a cover for all
n\geq 1,1\leq 0\leq i\leq n
.

Recall that

Definition 11.2
.

A simplicial set
X_{\bullet}
is an
\infty
-groupoid
if the canonical map
X_{\bullet}\to*
is a Kan fibration.

Definition 11.3
.

Let
\mathsf{C},\mathsf{D}
be two
\infty
-categories (quasi-categories), and
(\infty,1)
-functor

F:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{D}
is a
s\mathsf{Set}
morphism of the underlying simplicial sets.

In general, a simplicial object in an
\infty
-category
\mathsf{C}
is defined to be an
(\infty,1)
-functor
\Delta^{op}\to\mathsf{C}
. We define the
\infty
-category of
simplicial derived manifolds
to be the
\infty
-category of
(\infty,1)
functors

s\mathsf{dM}=\mathsf{dM}^{\Delta^{op}}=\operatorname{Fun_{\infty}}(\Delta^{op}\to\mathsf{dM})

Let
\operatorname{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\Delta}(-,X):s\mathsf{Set}^{\operatorname{op}}\to\mathsf{dM}
be the homotopy right Kan extension of
X_{\bullet}\in s\mathsf{dM}
. Note that since
\mathsf{dM}
has all finite homotopy limits,
\operatorname{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\Delta}(K,X_{\bullet})\in\mathsf{dM}
for finite
K\in s\mathsf{Set}
.

Definition 11.4
.

Let
K
be a simplicial set, we define the
homotopy
K
-matching object
in
\mathsf{dM}
to be
M^{h}_{K}X_{\bullet}=\operatorname{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\Delta}(K,X_{\bullet}).

Let
T
be a finite simplicial set and
S\hookrightarrow T
an inclusion of simplicial subset. Let
f:X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
be a morphism between simplicial derived manifolds, then we denote the
\operatorname{Hom}(S\hookrightarrow T,f)
to be the homotopy fiber product

M^{h}_{S}X_{\bullet}\times^{h}_{M^{h}_{S}Y_{\bullet}}M^{h}_{T}Y_{\bullet}
.

There exists a fully faithful embedding
\mathsf{y}:s\mathsf{dM}\to s\mathsf{Sh}(\mathsf{dM})
.

Definition 11.5
.

Let
X_{\bullet}
be a simplicial derived manifold, then define
\mathsf{y}X_{\bullet}
to be the representable simplicial sheaf such that
\mathsf{y}X(U)_{n}=\operatorname{Map}_{\mathsf{dM}}(U,X_{n})
.

Let
X_{\bullet}
,
Y_{\bullet}
be two simplicial derived manifolds, a map
f:X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
is a
Kan fibration
if the matching map

X_{k}\to M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[k]}X\times_{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[k]}Y}Y_{k}

is a cover for all
0\leq i\leq k
and
k\geq 1
. If the above matching map are all isomorphisms, we call
f
a
unique Kan fibration
. If the above matching map are covers for all
1\leq k\leq n
and are isomorphisms for all
k>n
, then
f
is an
n
-
Kan fibration
. A Kan fibration
f
is a
smooth Kan fibration
if the map restriction to the 0-simplices
f_{0}:X_{0}\to Y_{0}
is a cover.

Definition 11.6
.

We call
X_{\bullet}
a
derived Lie
\infty
-groupoid
if the canonical map
X_{\bullet}\to\ast
is a Kan fibration.

Denote the
\infty
-category of derived Lie
\infty
-groupoid by
\mathsf{Lie_{\infty}Grpd}
.

Let
X_{\bullet}\in\mathsf{Lie_{\infty}Grpd}
. If the Kan fibration
X_{k}\to X(\Lambda^{i}[k])
is an equivalence for
k>n,0\leq i\leq k
, then we say
X_{\bullet}
is a
derived Lie
n
-groupoid
.

Remark 11.7
.

This definition of derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids roughly corresponds to homotopy hypergroupoids in pseudo-model categories in
[
Pri13
]
.
[
BG17
]
defined geometric
\infty
-category in a descent category.
[
RZ20
]
defined Lie
\infty
-groupoids in Banach manifolds in a similar fashion.

Similarly, we can define a geometric
\infty
-category in
\mathsf{dM}
.

Definition 11.8
.

Let
X_{\bullet}
be a simplicial derived manifold.
X
is a
derived Lie
\infty
-category
if for each
0<i<k
and
k\geq 1
.

X_{k}\to Y_{k}\times_{Y(\Lambda^{i}[k])}X(\Lambda^{i}[k])

is a cover. If the above matching map are all isomorphisms
k>n
, then
X_{\bullet}
is a
derived Lie
n
-category
.

A map between derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids
f:X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
is called a
hypercover
if

X_{k}\to Y_{k}\times_{Y(\partial\Delta^{i}[k])}X(\partial\Delta^{i}[k])

are covers for all
k
. This definition is roughly an acyclic Kan fibration.

Next, we define a homotopy version of descent category in
[
BG17
]
.

Remark 11.9
.

Note that in our definition, derived Lie
\infty
-category is irrelevant to the derived
\infty
-category of dg-modules which is an enhancement of the classical derived category. From now on, we shall only call Lie
\infty
-category in some specific derived spaces.

[
BG17
]
defines the
descent category
, which is a weaker notion than pretopology but with finite completeness assumption. For homotopical categories, we have a natural extension of this notion:

Definition 11.10
.

Let
{\mathsf{C}}
be a homotopical category with a subcategory called covers. We call
{\mathsf{C}}
is a
homotopy descent category
, if the following axioms are satisfied:

(1)

{\mathsf{C}}
has finite homotopy limits;

(2)

pullback of a cover is a cover;

(3)

if
f
is a cover and
gf
is a cover, then
g
is a cover.

By lemma
8.14
,
\mathsf{dMfd}
with smooth surjections or étale maps is a homotopy descent category. By lemma,
\mathsf{dAnSp}_{k}
with subjective submersion or étale maps is a homotopy descent category.
\mathsf{dBan}
does not satisfy this axiom, hence we want to develop other tools to fix it.

11.1.
Points in Grothendieck topology

We will define a new tool in a Grothendieck topology which allows us to test properties infinitesimally. In a topological space, we consider a sequence of open neighborhoods of a given point
x
, and then watching the behavior of some geometric objects over these neighborhoods. We want to construct a similar notion for a category with a Grothendieck topology.

Definition 11.11

(
[
RZ20
]
)
.

Let
({\mathsf{C}},\mathcal{T})
be a category of equipped with a Grothendieck pretopology..

•

A
point
is a functor
\mathsf{p}:\mathsf{Sh}({\mathsf{C}})\to\mathsf{Set}
which preserves finite limits and small colimits.

•

({\mathsf{C}},\mathcal{T})
is said to have
enough points
if there exists a collection of points
\{\mathsf{p}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
such that a sheaf morphism
\phi:F\to G
is an isomorphism if and only if
\mathsf{p}_{*}(\phi):\mathsf{p}(F)\to\mathsf{p}(G)
is an isomorphism of sets for all
\mathsf{p}\in\{\mathsf{p}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
. In this case, we say
\{\mathsf{p}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is
jointly conservative
with respect to
({\mathsf{C}},\mathcal{T})
.

Points were originally introduced in topos theory as an adjuntion

x:\mathsf{Set}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overset{x^{*}}{\leftarrow}}}{{\underset{x_{*}}{\to}}}{\mathsf{C}}

between the base topos
\mathsf{Set}
to
{\mathsf{C}}
. Given an object
c\in{\mathsf{C}}
, we call
x^{*}(c)
the
stalk
. We won’t need this level of generality, so we stick with the notion in
[
RZ20
]
which is sufficient for our construction.

Similarly, we define points in a homotopical category with an
\operatorname{(\infty,1)}
-Grothendieck pretopology.

Definition 11.12

(
[
RZ20
]
)
.

Let
({\mathsf{C}},\mathcal{T})
be a homotopical category with an
\operatorname{(\infty,1)}
-Grothendieck pretopology.

•

A
point
is a functor
\mathsf{p}:\mathsf{Sh}({\mathsf{C}})\to\mathsf{Set}
which preserves finite homotopy limits and small homotopy colimits.

•

({\mathsf{C}},\mathcal{T})
is said to have
enough points
if there exists a collection of points
\{\mathsf{p}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
such that a sheaf morphism
\phi:F\to G
is an isomorphism if and only if
\mathsf{p}_{*}(\phi):\mathsf{p}(F)\to\mathsf{p}(G)
is an isomorphism of sets for all
\mathsf{p}\in\{\mathsf{p}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
. In this case, we say
\{\mathsf{p}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is
jointly conservative
with respect to
({\mathsf{C}},\mathcal{T})
.

For simplicity, we will usually refer
\operatorname{(\infty,1)}
-Grothendieck pretopology simply as Grothendieck pretopology when the underlying category is a homotopical category and there is no other confusion.

Let
X_{\bullet}
be a simplicial object in
({\mathsf{C}},\mathcal{T})
. Given a point
\mathsf{p}:\mathsf{Sh}({\mathsf{C}})\to\mathsf{Set}
, there is a natural extension of
\mathsf{p}
to a map
\mathsf{Sh}(s{\mathsf{C}})\to s\mathsf{Set}
by

\mathsf{p}X_{n}=\mathsf{p}(\mathsf{y}X_{n})

and all structure maps are images under the Yoneda embedding.

Consider
(\mathsf{dMfd},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
the category of derived manifold equipped with smooth surjection pretopology and
(\mathsf{dBan},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
with surjection submersion pretopology. We want to show that both these sites have enough points.

Proposition 11.13
.

Let
G_{\bullet}
be a derived Lie
\infty
-groupoid in
(\mathsf{dMfd},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
or
(\mathsf{dBan},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
, and
K_{\bullet}
a finitely generated simplicial set. Then there exists a unique natural isomorphism

\mathsf{p}\operatorname{Hom}(K,X)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{s\mathsf{Set}}(K,\mathsf{p}G_{\bullet})

for each
\mathsf{p}
.

Proof.

First, we have
\mathsf{p}\operatorname{Hom}(K_{\bullet},X_{\bullet})\simeq\mathsf{p}\operatorname{Hom}(K_{\bullet},\mathsf{y}X_{\bullet})
. Since
K_{\bullet}
is finitely generated,
\operatorname{Hom}(K_{\bullet},\mathsf{y}X_{\bullet})
is a finite limit. Using the fact that
\mathsf{p}
preserves finite limits, we get directly
\mathsf{p}\operatorname{Hom}(K_{\bullet},\mathsf{y}X_{\bullet})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{s\mathsf{Set}}(K_{\bullet},\mathsf{p}X_{\bullet})
.
∎

Definition 11.14
.

Consider
(\mathsf{dM},\mathcal{T})
be either
(\mathsf{dMfd},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
or
(\mathsf{dBan},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
. Let
F,G\in\mathsf{Sh}(\mathsf{dM})
. We say a sheaf morphism
\phi:F\to G
is a
local surjection
if, given any object
X\in\mathsf{dM}
and
y\in G(X)
, there exists a cover
f:U\to X
such that
f^{*}y
lies in the image of
\phi_{U}:F(U)\to G(U)
.

Definition 11.15
.

Consider
(\mathsf{dM},\mathcal{T})
be either
(\mathsf{dMfd},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
or
(\mathsf{dBan},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
. Let
\{\mathsf{p}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a collection of jointly conservative points of
(\mathsf{dM},\mathcal{T})
. We say
\phi
is a
stalkwise surjection
with respect to
\{\mathsf{p}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
if, for all
\mathsf{p}_{i}
,

\mathsf{p}_{i}(\phi):\mathsf{p}_{i}(F)\to\mathsf{p}_{i}(G)
is surjective.

Proposition 11.16
.

Let
(\mathsf{dM},\mathcal{T})
be either
(\mathsf{dMfd},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
or
(\mathsf{dBan},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
.
We have

(1)

Local surjections of sheaves on
(\mathsf{dM},\mathcal{T})
are epimorphisms.

(2)

Epimorphism of sheaves on
(\mathsf{dM},\mathcal{T})
are stalkwise surjection with respect to any collection of jointly conservative points.

(3)

Let
f
be a cover, then
\mathsf{y}(f)
is a stalkwise surjection with respect to any collection of jointly conservative points.

Proof.

Let
\phi:F\to G
be a morphism in
\mathsf{Sh}(\mathsf{dM})
.

(1) Suppose
\phi
is a local surjection. Consider two morphism
\alpha,\beta:G\to H
in
\mathsf{Sh}(\mathsf{dM})
, such that
\alpha\circ\phi=\beta\circ\phi
, we want to show that
∎

Definition 11.17
.

Let
\{\mathsf{p}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a collection of jointly conservative points of
(\mathsf{dM},\mathcal{T})
.
A morphism
\psi:X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
of derived Lie
\infty
-groupoid in
(\mathsf{dM},\mathcal{T})
is a
stalkwise weak equivalence
if for any
\mathsf{p}_{i}\in\{\mathsf{p}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, the induced map
\mathsf{p}\psi:\mathsf{p}X_{\bullet}\to\mathsf{p}Y_{\bullet}
is a weak equivalence of simplicial sets.

If a morphism of derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids is both a stalkwise Kan fibration and stalkwise weak equivalence, then we call it a
stalkwise acyclic fibration
.

Proposition 11.18
.

Let
\{\mathsf{p}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a collection of jointly conservative points of
(\mathsf{dM},\mathcal{T})
. A morphism
\psi:X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
of derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids in
(\mathsf{dM},\mathcal{T})
is a stalkwise acyclic fibration if and only if

X_{k}\to Y_{k}\times_{Y(\partial\Delta^{i}[k])}X(\partial\Delta^{i}[k])

are stalkwise surjections for all
k\geq 0
.

Corollary 11.19
.

A hypercover of derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids is both a Kan fibration and a stalkwise weak equivalence.

Proposition 11.20
.

(\mathsf{dMfd},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
has enough points. In fact, let
M\in\mathsf{dM}
and
x\in M
, define

(11.1)

\mathsf{p}_{x}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{U^{\mathsf{Aff},open}\subset M}F(U)

where each
U^{\mathsf{Aff},open}
is an affine open derived manifold and
U\to M
is a cover, then
\{\mathsf{p}_{x}\}
is a jointly conservative collection of points.

Proof.

First, note that
\mathsf{p}_{x}
is a filtered colimit for any
x\in M
, hence it preserves finite limits and small colimits. Let
\phi:F\to G
be a sheaf morphism, and suppose
(\mathsf{p}_{x})_{*}(\phi):\phi_{x}F\to\phi_{x}G
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Set}
.

First we show
\phi
is injective. Let
M\in\mathsf{dM}
and
f,g\in F(M)
with
\phi_{M}(f)=\phi_{M}(g)\in G(M)
. Note that since
(\mathsf{p}_{x})_{*}(\phi)
is an injection, we have if
f\in F(U_{1}),g\in F(U_{2})
and
\mathsf{p}_{x}(\phi)(\overline{f})=\mathsf{p}_{x}(\phi)(\overline{g})
where
x\in U_{1}\cap U_{2}
, then there exists
U_{12}\subset U_{1}\cap U_{2}
containing
x
such that
i^{*}_{1}f=i^{*}_{2}g
where
i_{n}:U_{12}\to U_{n},n=1,2
are inclusions. Let
x\in M
, then there exists an affine open derived manifold
U_{x}
, i.e.
U_{x}=\operatorname{Spec}A_{x}
for some
A_{x}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\mathsf{Alg}
such that

\coprod_{x\in M}U_{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i_{x})}}{{\longrightarrow}}M

is a cover, where each
i_{x}:U_{x}\to M
is an inclusion. By pulling back along each
i_{x}
,

\phi_{U_{x}}(i_{x}^{*}f)=\phi_{U_{x}}(i_{x}^{*}g)

implies that

(\mathsf{p}_{x})_{*}(\phi_{U_{x}})(\overline{i_{x}^{*}f})=(\mathsf{p}_{x})_{*}(\phi_{U_{x}})(\overline{i_{x}^{*}g})

From previous observation, for each
U_{x}
, there exists
i^{\prime}:U_{x}^{\prime}\subset U_{x}
such that
(i^{\prime})^{*}f=(i^{\prime})^{*}g
. Now since
F
is a sheaf and
\coprod_{x\in M}U_{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i_{x})}}{{\longrightarrow}}M
is a cover, we have
f=g
.

Next, we show
\phi
is surjective. Let
g\in G(M)
and the pullback along
i_{x}
is
i^{*}_{x}(g)\in G(U_{x})
. Since
(\mathsf{p}_{x})_{*}(\phi)
is surjective, there exists a
j_{x}:U_{x}^{\prime}\subset U_{x}
and
f_{x}\in F(U_{x}^{\prime})
such that
\phi(f_{x})=j_{x}^{*}i_{x}^{*}(g)\hookrightarrow M
. Now consider the fiber product

\coprod_{x\in M}U_{x}^{\prime}\times_{M}\coprod_{x\in M}U_{x}^{\prime}\quad\overset{p_{1}}{\underset{p_{2}}{{\rightrightarrows}}}\quad M

Observe that

\phi(p_{1}^{*}(f_{x}))=p_{1}^{*}(\phi(f_{x}))=p_{1}^{*}(j^{*}_{x}i_{x}^{*}(g))

and
p_{1}^{*}(j^{*}_{x}i_{x}^{*}(g))=p_{2}^{*}(j^{*}_{x}i_{x}^{*}(g))
since
G
is a sheaf.
Therefore,

p_{2}^{*}(j^{*}_{x}i_{x}^{*}(g))=p_{2}^{*}(\phi(f_{x})=\phi(p_{2}^{*}(f_{x}))

By the injectivity of
\phi
from the first part, we have
p_{1}^{*}(f_{x})=p_{2}^{*}(f_{x})
. Since
F
is sheaf, there exists a global section
f\in F(M)
such that
f\big|_{U_{x}^{\prime}}=f_{x}
. Hence
j_{x}^{*}\phi(f)=\phi(f_{x})=j^{*}_{x}i_{x}^{*}(g)
, which implies that
\phi(f)=g
.

Finally, suppose
\phi
is a sheaf isomorphism, then it is obvious that each
\mathsf{p}_{x}
gives isomorphism of sets.
∎

Corollary 11.21
.

(\mathsf{dBan},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
has enough points. In fact, let
M\in\mathsf{dM}
and
x\in M
, define

(11.2)

\mathsf{p}_{x}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{U^{open}\subset M}F(U)

where each
U^{,open}
is an open derived Banach manifold and
U\to M
is a cover, then
\{\mathsf{p}_{x}\}
is a jointly conservative collection of points.

Remark 11.22
.

By our construction, each
\mathsf{p}_{x}
is local. hence only depends on affine
\{U_{x}=\operatorname{Spec}A_{x}\}
which contains
x
.

Proof.

Similar to the case of derived manifold, we just need to replace the affine opens to be open balls.
∎

11.2.
Locally stalkwise pretopology

Definition 11.23
.

Let
(\mathsf{C},\mathcal{T},\{\mathsf{p}_{i}\}_{i\in I})
be a site with enough points. A morphism
F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\to}}\mathsf{y}Y
in
\mathsf{Sh}(\mathsf{C})
is a
local stalkwise cover
iff there exists an object
X\in\mathsf{C}
and a stalkwise surjection
\mathsf{y}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}F
such that
g\circ f
is a cover.

Definition 11.24

(
[
RZ20
]
)
.

The pretopology on a site
(\mathsf{C},\mathcal{T})
is a
locally stalkwise pretopology
if it satisfies

(1)

Let
g,f
be morphisms in
{\mathsf{C}}
. If
g\circ f
is a cover and
\mathsf{y}(f)
is a stalkwise surjection in
\mathsf{Sh}(\mathsf{C})
with respect to a joint conservative collection of points
\{\mathsf{p}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, then
g
is a cover.

(2)

Let
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q}}{{\to}}Y
and
Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}Y
be two morphisms in
\mathsf{C}
. Suppose
\mathsf{y}(q)
is a stalkwise surjection with respect to
\{\mathsf{p}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and the base change
X\times_{Y}Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{p}}}{{\to}}Z
is a local stalkwise cover, then
p
is a cover.

The most important property of a locally stalkwise pretopology is that it allows us to characterize Hypercovers by Kan fibrations and stalkwise weak equivalences.

Proposition 11.25

(
[
RZ20
]
)
.

Consider a category with pretopology
({\mathsf{C}},\mathcal{T})
equipped with a locally stalkwise pretopology with respect to a jointly conservative collection of points
\{\mathsf{p}\}_{i}
. Let
f:X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
be a morphism of Lie
\infty
-groupoids in
({\mathsf{C}},\mathcal{T})
, then the followings are equivalent:

(1)

f
is a Kan fibration and a stalkwise weak equivalence with respect to
\{\mathsf{p}\}_{i}
.

(2)

f
is a Kan fibration and a stalkwise weak equivalence with respect to any jointly conservative collection of points of
({\mathsf{C}},\mathcal{T})
.

(3)

f
is a hypercover.

Proof.

See
[
RZ20
, Proposition 6.7]
.
∎

We will show the pretopologies of both
(\mathsf{dMfd},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
,
(\mathsf{dAnSp},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
, and
(\mathsf{dBan},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
are locally stalkwise.

Lemma 11.26
.

Let
f:X\to Y
,
g:Y\to Z
be morphisms in
\mathsf{dMfd}
. Suppose
g\circ f
is a smooth surjection and
f
is surjective, then
g
is also a smooth surjection.

Proof.

Let
y\in Y
. Denote
z=f(y)
and
x\in f^{-1}(y)
any preimage of
y
. Since
g\circ f
is smooth, there exists a local section
\sigma_{UV}:U\to V
where
U
and
V
are affine open neighborhood of
z
and
x
, i.e.
\sigma_{UV}(z)=x
and
(g\circ f)\circ\sigma_{UV}=\operatorname{Id}_{U}
. Let
W=g^{-1}U
, then
\sigma=f\circ\sigma_{UV}:U\to W
is a local section such that
\sigma(z)=y
.
g
is clearly surjective.
∎

Remark 11.27
.

The above proof also works for étale topology (i.e. étale maps as covers) since we have local lifting property for étale maps as well.

Corollary 11.28
.

Let
f:X\to Y
,
g:Y\to Z
be morphisms in
\mathsf{dBan}
or
\mathsf{dAnSp}
. Suppose
g\circ f
is a surjective submersion and
f
is surjective, then
g
is also a surjective submersion.

Proof.

Similar to previous proof.
∎

Lemma 11.29
.

Let
f:X\to Y
,
g:Y\to Z
be morphisms in
\mathsf{dMfd}
. Suppose
g\circ f
is a smooth surjection and
f
is a stalkwise surjection, then
g
is also a smooth surjection.

Proof.

Since
f
is stalkwise surjective, it has to be surjective.
∎

Remark 11.30
.

Again, the result still holds if we replace surjective submersion by étale maps.

Corollary 11.31
.

Let
f:X\to Y
,
g:Y\to Z
be morphisms in
\mathsf{dBan}
or
\mathsf{dAnSp}
. Suppose
g\circ f
is a surjective submersion and
f
is a stalkwise surjection, then
g
is also a surjective submersion.

Proposition 11.32
.

(\mathsf{dMfd},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
and
(\mathsf{dAnSp},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
are categories with locally stalkwise pretopology.

Proof.

We will prove the case for
(\mathsf{dMfd},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
, and the case for
(\mathsf{dAnSp},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
is similar.

It suffices to verify the second axiom. Pick
z\in Z
and denote
p(z)=y
. Since
\mathsf{y}(q)
is a stalkwise surjection, for any
y\in Y
, we can find an affine open neighborhood
O_{y}
of
y
such that there exists a
x\in q^{-1}(y)\subset X
and an affine open neighborhood
O_{y}
of
y
such that
s_{yx}:O_{y}\to O_{x}
is a local section of
q|_{O_{x}}
such that
s_{yx}(y)=x
. Let
O_{z}=p^{-1}(O_{y})
, then we have a pullback diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{x}\times_{O_{y}}O_{z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

where
O_{x}\times_{O_{y}}O_{z}=p^{\prime-1}(O_{x})=q^{\prime-1}O_{z}
by construction. Note that both
q|_{O_{x}}
and
q^{\prime}|_{O_{x}\times_{O_{y}}O_{z}}
are smooth surjections, In particular, we can shrink both of them to make
q
a projection when restricts to
O_{x}
. By construction, we can find a
w\in q^{\prime-1}(z)\subset O_{x}\times_{O_{y}}O_{z}
such that
p^{\prime}(w)=x
and a local section
s_{zw}
of
q^{\prime}|_{O_{x}\times_{O_{y}}O_{z}}
with
s_{zw}(z)=w
. Since
p^{\prime}
is a locally stalkwise cover, there exists some
U\in\mathsf{dMfd}
with a map
f:U\to O_{x}\times_{O_{y}}O_{z}
such that
p^{\prime}\circ f
is a smooth surjection. Hence, we can find a section
s_{xu}:O_{x}\to O_{u}
where
O_{u}=(p^{\prime}\circ f)^{-1}O_{x}
. Now
s_{xw}=f\circ s_{xu}:O_{x}\to O_{w}
is the desired section of
p^{\prime}
. To construct the section of
p
, we just need to take
s_{yz}=q^{\prime}\circ f\circ s_{xu}\circ s_{yx}
.
∎

Remark 11.33
.

The key of the proof is the ’inverse function theorem’ for smooth surjections of derived manifolds. Hence, it also shows that the result hold for étale topology and other topology which satisfies the ’inverse’ function theorem. For more about inverse function theorem in derived manifolds, see
[
Nui18
, Proposition 6.2.1]
.

Proposition 11.34
.

(\mathsf{dBan},\mathcal{T}_{ss})
is a category with locally stalkwise pretopology.

Proof.

The case for the category of (ordinary) Banach manifolds is shown in
[
RZ20
, Proposition 6.12]
. The proof for derived case follows mostly from the ordinary case. The only thing different from the above proof is that we need to take care of the representability issues in Banach manifolds. In this paper we do not construct homotopy structures on
\mathsf{dBan}
which will be developed in future work. When we compute fiber product in
\mathsf{dBan}
, we compute pushout in the dga’s, hence we only need to care about the representability of the degree 0 terms, which follows from the
[
RZ20
, Lemma 6.9]
and
[
RZ20
, Lemma 6.11]
. Again, the core of the proof is the inverse function theorem for submersion.
∎

11.3.
Collapsible extensions

In this section, we will study a special class of simplicial maps, which will be used heavily later when we prove some representability results on simplicial sheaves.

Definition 11.35
.

Let
T_{\bullet}
be a finitely generated simplicial set and
S_{\bullet}
a simplicial subset. The inclusion map
\iota:S_{\bullet}\to T_{\bullet}
is called a
collapsible extension
if and only if it can decomposed as a sequence of inclusion maps

S_{\bullet}=S^{0}_{\bullet}\hookrightarrow S^{1}_{\bullet}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow S^{l}_{\bullet}=T_{\bullet}

i.e. for each
i
,
S^{i}=S^{i-1}\sqcup_{\Lambda^{j}[m]}\Delta[m]
for some horn
\Lambda^{j}[m]
and
m>0
. If
T_{\bullet}
is a collapsible extension of a point, we say it is
collapsible
.

So roughly speaking, collapsible extension is a sequence of filling some horns. We can also define similar maps which fill in boundaries.

Definition 11.36
.

Let
T_{\bullet}
be a finitely generated simplicial set and
S_{\bullet}
a simplicial subset. The inclusion map
\iota:S_{\bullet}\to T_{\bullet}
is called a
boundary extension
if and only if it can decomposed as a sequence of inclusion maps

S_{\bullet}=S^{0}_{\bullet}\hookrightarrow S^{1}_{\bullet}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow S^{l}_{\bullet}=T_{\bullet}

i.e. for each
i
,
S^{i}=S^{i-1}\sqcup_{\partial\Delta[m]}\Delta[m]
for some horn
\Lambda^{j}[m]
and
m>0
.

An obvious result is

Lemma 11.37
.

The inclusion of any face
\Delta[k]\to\Delta[n]
is a collapsible extension for
0\leq k\leq n
.

Proof.

See
[
Li15
, Lemma2.44]
.
∎

Next, we will see how collapsible extension relate to representablity of Lie
\infty
-groupoids.

Lemma 11.38
.

Let
X_{\bullet}
be a Lie
\infty
-groupoid. Suppose
S_{\bullet}\hookrightarrow T_{\bullet}
is a collapsible extension. If
\operatorname{Hom}(S_{\bullet},X_{\bullet})
is representable, then
\operatorname{Hom}(T_{\bullet},X_{\bullet})
is also representable, and the induced map

\operatorname{Hom}(T_{\bullet},X_{\bullet})\to\operatorname{Hom}(S_{\bullet},X_{\bullet})

is a cover.

Proof.

This is
[
RZ20
, Lemma 3.7]
.
Let
S_{\bullet}=S^{0}_{\bullet}\hookrightarrow S^{1}_{\bullet}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow S^{l}_{\bullet}=T_{\bullet}
be the collapsible extension. Since covers are closed under composition, we can just restrict to the case of one inclusion. Let
T_{\bullet}=S_{\bullet}\sqcup_{\Lambda^{j}[m]}\Delta[m]
. Applying
\operatorname{Hom}(-,X_{\bullet})

\operatorname{Hom}(T_{\bullet},X_{\bullet})=\operatorname{Hom}(S_{\bullet},X_{\bullet})\times_{\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda^{j}[m],X_{\bullet})}\operatorname{Hom}(\Delta[n],X_{\bullet})

Since
X
is a Lie
\infty
-groupoid,
\operatorname{Hom}(\Delta[n],X_{\bullet})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda^{j}[m],X_{\bullet})
is a cover between representable sheaves. Therefore, by axioms of pretopology, we get
\operatorname{Hom}(T_{\bullet},X_{\bullet})
is representable and
\operatorname{Hom}(T_{\bullet},X_{\bullet})\to\operatorname{Hom}(S_{\bullet},X_{\bullet})
is a cover.
∎

Remark 11.39
.

For
X_{\bullet}
being a Lie
n
-groupoid, and
T_{\bullet}=S_{\bullet}\sqcup_{\Lambda^{j}[m]}\Delta[m]
with
m>n,0\leq j\leq m
, and suppose
\operatorname{Hom}(S_{\bullet},X_{\bullet})
is representable, then
\operatorname{Hom}(T_{\bullet},X_{\bullet})\to\operatorname{Hom}(S_{\bullet},X_{\bullet})
is actually an isomorphism.

Next, we consider the representability of sheaves.

Lemma 11.40
.

Let
S\subset\Delta[n]
be a collapsible simplicial subset,
X_{\bullet}
a simplicial manifold, and
Y_{\bullet}
a Lie
\infty
-groupoid. If
f:X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
is a morphism satisfies
\operatorname{Kan}(m,j)
for
m<k
and
0\leq j\leq m
, then the sheaf on
\mathsf{dM}

\operatorname{Hom}(S_{\bullet}\hookrightarrow\Delta[k],X_{\bullet}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}Y_{\bullet})
is representable.

Proof.

This is
[
RZ20
, Lemma 3.9]
. Consider
S^{0}=*\hookrightarrow\Delta[k]
. Note that
\operatorname{Hom}(*\hookrightarrow\Delta[k],X_{\bullet}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}Y_{\bullet})
is represented by
X_{0}\times_{Y_{0}}Y_{k}
. By previous two lemmas, we see
Y_{k}\to Y_{0}
is a cover.
∎

Corollary 11.41
.

For same assumption as above,
\operatorname{Hom}(S_{\bullet}\hookrightarrow\Delta[k],X_{\bullet}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}Y_{\bullet})
is representable.

Proof.

Applying previous lemma to the horn
\Lambda^{j}[m]
which is collapsible for all
j
’s.
∎

Corollary 11.42
.

Suppose
X_{\bullet}\to*
satisfies
\operatorname{Kan}(m,j)
for
1\leq m<k
, then
\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda^{j}[m],X_{\bullet})
is representable.

Lemma 11.43
.

Let
f:\Lambda^{i}[1]\to\Delta[1]
for
i=0,1
be the standard inclusion. If
\iota:S_{\bullet}\to T_{\bullet}
is a boundary extension, then the induced map

(S_{\bullet}\otimes\Delta[1])\sqcup_{S_{\bullet}\otimes\Lambda^{i}[1]}(T_{\bullet}\otimes\Lambda^{i}[1])\to(T_{\bullet}\otimes\Delta[1])

is a collapsible extension.

Proof.

See
[
Hov07
, Lemma 3.3.3]
for the case of
\iota
being the standard inclusion
\partial\Delta[n]\to\Delta[n]
. Suppose
F:s\mathsf{Set}\times s\mathsf{Set}\to s\mathsf{Set}
is a co-continues functor and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

is a pushout square of simplicial set, then applying the same technique in
[
Li15
, Lemma 2.42]
, we have
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X_{\bullet},\Delta[1])\sqcup_{F(X_{\bullet},\Lambda^{i}[1])}F(Y_{\bullet},\Lambda^{i}[1])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y_{\bullet},\Delta[1])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(S_{\bullet},\Delta[1])\sqcup_{F(S_{\bullet},\Lambda^{i}[1])}F(T_{\bullet},\Lambda^{i}[1])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(T_{\bullet},\Delta[1])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

is also a pushout square. Now take
F
to be the product and proceed by induction.
∎

Remark 11.44
.

Since collapsible extensions are boundary extensions,
replacing the boundary extension assumption in the previous lemma by collapsible extension, the result still holds.

Lemma 11.45
.

The inclusion
\Lambda^{j}[n]\times\Delta[1]\to\Delta[n]\times\Delta[1]
is a collapsible extension.

Proof.

Regard
\Lambda^{j}[n]\times\Delta[1]\to\Delta[n]\times\Delta[1]
as a composition

\Lambda^{j}[n]\times\Delta[1]\to(\Lambda^{j}[n]\times\Delta[1])\sqcup_{\Lambda^{j}[n]\times\Lambda^{i}[1]}(\Delta[n]\times\Lambda^{i}[1])\to\Delta[n]\times\Delta[1]

It is clear that the first map is collapsible. The second map is also collapsible by the previous lemma.
∎

Lemma 11.46
.

The inclusion

(\Lambda^{j}[n]\times\Delta[1])\sqcup_{\Lambda^{j}[n]\times\partial\Delta[1]}(\Delta[n]\times\partial\Delta[1])\to\Delta[n]\times\Delta[1]

is a collapsible extension.

Proof.

See
[
RZ20
, Appendix A]
.
∎

12.
Homotopy theory of derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids

12.1.
Category of fibrant objects

Category of fibrant objects (CFO), also known as Brown category, is a weaker notion of a Quillen model category which still allow us to perform many operations in homotopy theory.

Definition 12.1

(
[
Bro73
]
,
[
BG17
]
,
[
RZ20
]
)
.

Let
{\mathsf{C}}
be a small category, we say that
{\mathsf{C}}
is a
category of fibrant objects
(CFO) such that there exists two distinguished subcategories
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{F}
called
weak equivalences
and
fibrations
respectively, and it satisfies the following conditions

(1)

{\mathsf{C}}
has all finite products, and in particular a terminal object
\ast
.

(2)

Pullback of a fibration along arbitrary morphisms exist, and it is also a fibration.

(3)

The morphisms which sit in both
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{F}
are call
acyclic fibrations
. The Pullbacks of acyclic fibrations are acyclic fibrations.

(4)

Weak equivalences satisfy 2-out-of-3, and contain all isomorphisms.

(5)

Composition of fibrations are fibrations, and all isomorphisms are fibrations.

(6)

Given any object
B
, there exists a
path object

B^{\Delta[1]}
that fits into the diagram

B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma}}{{\longrightarrow}}B^{\Delta[1]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d_{0},d_{1})}}{{\longrightarrow}}B\times B

where
\sigma
is a weak equivalence and
(d_{0},d_{1})
is a fibration, and the composition
B\to B\times B
is the diagonal map.

(7)

For any objects
B
, the canonical map
B\to\ast
is a fibration, i.e. all objects are
fibrant
.

Example 12.2
.

Fibrant model categories are trivial examples of categories of fibrant objects, for example:

•

\mathsf{Top}
with the Quillen model structure.

•

\operatorname{ch}_{k}^{\geq 0}
with projective model structures.

•

\mathsf{Mod}_{A}^{\geq 0}
with projective model structures.

Example 12.3
.

The next simple examples are restriction of model categories to their fibrant objects, for example

•

The subcategory of
s\mathsf{Set}
consisting of Kan complexes, which we call the category of
\infty
-groupoids
\mathsf{Grpd_{\infty}}
.

Example 12.4

(Simplicial sheaves)
.

Let
({\mathsf{C}},\mathcal{T})
be a site with enough points. For example, take
{\mathsf{C}}=\operatorname{Open}(X)
the category of open subsets for a topological space
X
. Then the category of simplicial sheaves on
{\mathsf{C}}
whose stalks are Kan complexes form a category of fibrant objects. Hence, this gives a model for the homotopy category of
\infty
-stacks over
{\mathsf{C}}
. This is a motivating example in
[
Bro73
]
to introduce categories of fibrant objects.

Example 12.5

(
C^{*}
-algebras)
.

Let
\mathsf{C^{*}Alg}
be the category of
C^{*}
-algebras.
[
Sch84
]
construct a category of fibrant objects structure on
\mathsf{C^{*}Alg}
as follows.

Denote
\pi_{0}\mathsf{C^{*}Alg}
the ordinary homotopy category of
\mathsf{C^{*}Alg}
, i.e. the same objects as
\mathsf{C^{*}Alg}
with homotopy classes of maps in
\mathsf{C^{*}Alg}
. We say a map
f:A\to B
is a
homotopy equivalence
if
\pi_{0}(f)
is invertible in
\pi_{0}\mathsf{C^{*}Alg}
. A map
f:A\to B
is called a
a Schochet fibration
if its induced map

f_{*}:\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{C^{*}Alg}}(C,A)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{C^{*}Alg}}(C,B)

has the path lifting property for all
C\in\mathsf{C^{*}Alg}
.

\mathsf{C^{*}Alg}
with homotopy equivalences as weak equivalences and Schochet fibrations as fibrations is a category of fibrant objects.

[
UUY
]
construct another category of fibrant objects structure the category of separable
C^{*}
-algebras
\mathsf{C^{*}Alg}^{{\operatorname{sep}}}
forms a category of fibrant objects with weak equivalences the
KK
-equivalences and fibrations the Schochet fibrations, whose homotopy category
\mathsf{Ho}(\mathsf{C^{*}Alg}^{{\operatorname{sep}}})
is equivalent to the
KK
-category of Kasparov
[
Kas07
]
. This implies that Kasparov’s
KK
-category is a stable triangulated category.

Example 12.6

(Behrend-Liao-Xu derived manifolds)
.

[
BLX21
]
develops a theory of derived manifolds using bundles of curved
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebras. They construct a category of fibrant objects on their category of derived manifolds as follows:

•

A morphism is a weak equivalence if:

(1)

It induces a bijection on classical loci.

(2)

Its linear part induces a quasi-isomorphism on tangent complexes at all classical points.

•

A morphism is a fibration if:

(1)

The underlying morphism of manifolds is a submersion,

(2)

The linear part of the morphism of
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebras is levelwise surjective.

Sometimes we want to deal with categories which do not contain all finite limits, but we still want to do homotopy theory on it. It turns out that we can loosen the limits criteria sometimes, and consider
incomplete category of fibrant objects
(iCFO), where we do not assume all pullbacks of fibrations exists, and only for those pullbacks exist, the pullbacks are still fibrations. In summary,

Definition 12.7

(
[
RZ20
]
)
.

We say a category
{\mathsf{C}}
is an
incomplete category of fibrant objects
(iCFO), if it satisfies the conditions (3)-(7) of categories of fibrant objects, and we replace (2) by

•

If the pullback of a fibration exists, then it is a fibration.

Example 12.8
.

As a prototypical example,
[
RZ20
]
shows that Lie
\infty
-groupoids in
\mathsf{Ban}
with surjective submersion pretopology is an incomplete category of fibrant objects.

12.1.1.
Homotopical algebra for categories of fibrant objects

CFO allows us to perform explicit homotopical operations, for example, compute
\operatorname{(\infty,1)}
-limits explicitly.

Recall that in a homotopical category, the homotopy pullback of two maps
\mathcal{F}:A\to C
,
g:B\to C
are defined as a universal object
X
such that the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

commutes up to homotopy. Thanks to the existence of path object, we can compute homotopy pullbacks explicitly and easily in categories of fibrant objects.

Theorem 12.9

(
[
Bro73
]
)
.

Let
{\mathsf{C}}
be a category of fibrant object, then the homotopy pullback(or homotopy fiber product
A\times^{h}_{C}\times B
of two maps
\mathcal{F}:A\to C
,
g:B\to C
is presented by
A\times_{C}C^{I}\times_{C}B
, i.e. the ordinary limit of
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times_{C}C^{I}\times_{C}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{I}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Moreover, the projection map
\pi:A\times_{C}C^{I}\times_{C}B\to A
a fibration. If in addition
v:B\to C
is a weak equivalence, then
\pi
is an acyclic fibration.

Another useful property of CFO is that we have a nice simplification of homotopy mapping space. We follow the construction in
[
NSS12
, Section 3.6.3]
.

Definition 12.10
.

Let
{\mathsf{C}}
be a category of fibrant objects. Let
X,Y\in{\mathsf{C}}
be two objects. Define a category
\mathsf{Cocycle}(X,Y)
by

(1)

Objects are spans, i.e. diagrams of the following form

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longleftarrow}}A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}Y

where the left morphism is an acyclic fibration.

(2)

Morphisms are given by commutative diagrams of the following form
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}

Note that the map
f:A_{1}\to A_{2}
is necessarily a weak equivalence by 2-out-of-3.

Theorem 12.11

(
[
NSS12
]
)
.

Let
{\mathsf{C}}
be a category of fibrant objects. Let
X,Y\in{\mathsf{C}}
be two objects. Given any objects
X,Y\in{\mathsf{C}}
, the canonical inclusions

\mathcal{N}\mathsf{Cocycle}(X,Y)\to\mathcal{N}\mathsf{wCocycle}(X,Y)\to L^{H}{\mathsf{C}}(X,Y)

weak equivalences, where
L^{H}{\mathsf{C}}
is the hammock localization of
{\mathsf{C}}
.

Hence, we can compute the homotopy mapping spaces (or derived Hom space in
[
NSS12
]
