) of a category of fibrant objects simply using its category of spans
\mathsf{Cocycle}({\mathsf{C}})
or
\mathsf{wCocycle}({\mathsf{C}})
. As an easy consequence of this theorem, the homotopy fiber product we get in Theorem
12.9
presents the correct
\operatorname{(\infty,1)}
-limit.

12.1.2.
Fibrations in derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids

In this section, we will prove some basic properties of Kan fibrations and hypercovers of derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids in various categories. For simplicity, we use
\mathsf{dM}
to denote either
\mathsf{dMfd}
,
\mathsf{dAnSp}
, or
\mathsf{dBan}
. The proofs will work for any of these categories unless specified explicitly.

Proposition 12.12
.

Let
f:X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
,
g:Y_{\bullet}\to Z_{\bullet}
be Kan fibrations between derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids in
\mathsf{dM}
, then
g\circ f
is also a Kan fibration.

Proof.

We want to show the induced map
X_{k}\to M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[k]}X\times_{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[k]}Z}Z_{k}
is a cover. We have the following commutative diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[k]}X\times_{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[k]}Y}Y_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[k]}X\times_{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[k]}Z}Z_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[k]}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[k]}Y\times_{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[k]}Z}Z_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[k]}Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[k]}Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{*}}

The bottom square and the composition of bottom and middle squares are pullbacks, hence the middle one is as well. The composition of left and middle squares are pullbacks, hence the left square is also a pullback. Therefore,
g^{*}
is a cover since
\xi
is. Hence, the composition
X_{k}\to{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[k]}X\times_{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[k]}Y}Y_{k}}\to{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[k]}X\times_{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[k]}Z}Z_{k}}
is a cover.
∎

Corollary 12.13
.

The composition of
n
-Kan fibrations are
n
-Kan fibrations. In particular, composition of unique Kan fibrations are unique Kan fibrations.

Lemma 12.14
.

Hypercovers between derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids in
\mathsf{dM}
are Kan fibrations.

Proof.

Apply the canonical inclusion
\Lambda^{i}[k]\to\partial\Delta[k]
.
∎

Lemma 12.15
.

Let
f:X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
,
g:Y_{\bullet}\to Z_{\bullet}
be hypercovers between derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids in
\mathsf{dM}
, then
g\circ f
is also a hypercover.

Proof.

Similar to the case of Kan fibrations by replacing homotopy matching space of
\Lambda^{i}[k]
by homotopy matching space of
\partial\Delta[n]
.
∎

Proposition 12.16
.

Let
f:X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
be a Kan fibration between derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids in
\mathsf{dMfd}
or
\mathsf{dAnSp}
. Then the pullback of
f
along any morphisms

g:Z_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
exists and
h:X_{\bullet}\times_{Y_{\bullet}}Z_{\bullet}\to Z_{\bullet}
is a Kan fibration.

Proof.

The pullback has
n
-simplices
X_{n}\times^{h}_{Y_{n}}Z_{n}\in\mathsf{dM}
, hence is a simplicial derived manifold. We have the following commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times^{h}_{Y}Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Lambda^{i}[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}}

Hence we have a pullback diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{n}\times^{h}_{Y_{n}}Z_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[n]}{(X\times^{h}_{Y}Z)}\big)\times^{h}_{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[n]}Z}Z_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[n]}X}\times^{h}_{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[n]}Y}Y_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

Hence
\psi
is a cover since
\xi
is. Hence
h
is a fibration. Note that

\big(M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[n]}(X\times^{h}_{Y}Z)\big)\times^{h}_{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[n]}Z}Z_{n}\to\big(M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[n]}{(X\times^{h}_{Y}Z)}\big)

is a cover since it is the pullback of
Z_{n}\to M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[n]}Z
which is a cover.

∎

The above proposition generalizes to the case where
X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet},Z_{\bullet}
are Lie
n
-groupoids. It is easy to show that in this case the fiber product is also a Lie
n
-groupoid.

Remark 12.17
.

Clearly this won’t work for derived Banach manifolds due to lacking of limits. We will prove later that once pullback of a fibration exists, then it is a fibration, which is a key component in the iCFO structure on derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids in
\mathsf{dBan}
.

Proposition 12.18
.

Let
f:X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
be a hypercover between derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids in
\mathsf{dMfd}
or
\mathsf{dAnSp}
. Then the pullback of
f
along any morphism

g:Z_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
exists and
h:X_{\bullet}\times_{Y_{\bullet}}Z_{\bullet}\to Z_{\bullet}
is a hypercover.

Proof.

By similar argument as above, we have a pullback diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{n}\times^{h}_{Y_{n}}Z_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(M^{h}_{\Delta[n]}(X\times^{h}_{Y}Z)\big)\times^{h}_{M^{h}_{\Delta[n]}Z}Z_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{h}_{\Delta[n]}X\times^{h}_{M^{h}_{\Delta[n]}Y}Y_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

Hence
\psi
is a cover and
h
is then a hypercover.

∎

Next, we will show simplicial derived manifolds and simplicial derived
k
-analytic spaces also form a homotopy descent category.

Proposition 12.19
.

Both
s\mathsf{dMfd}
and
\mathsf{dAnSp}
are homotopy descent categories with hypercovers as covers.

We have shown pullbacks of hypercovers are hypercovers, we just need to verify the last criteria.

Lemma 12.20
.

Let
f:X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
,
g:Y_{\bullet}\to Z_{\bullet}
be morphisms of
s\mathsf{dMfd}
or
s\mathsf{dAnSp}
. Suppose
f
and
g\circ f
hypercovers, then
g
is also hypercover.

Proof.

We have the following commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[n]X}}\times^{h}_{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[n]}Y}Y_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[n]X}}\times^{h}_{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[n]}Z}Z_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[n]Y}}\times^{h}_{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[n]}Z}Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

∎

12.2.
Path object

Proposition 12.21
.

Let
X_{\bullet}
be a derived Lie
\infty
-groupoid in
\mathsf{dM}
, then there exists a path object
X^{\Delta[1]}_{\bullet}
, that is, we have a factorization

X_{\bullet}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s^{*}_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X^{\Delta[1]}_{\bullet}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d^{*}_{0},d^{*}_{1})}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{\bullet}\times X_{\bullet}

which is a factorization of the diagonal map
X_{\bullet}\to X_{\bullet}\times X_{\bullet}
into a stalkwise weak equivalence
s^{*}_{0}
followed by a Kan fibration
(d^{*}_{0},d^{*}_{1})
.

First, we want to look at the sheaf level.

Lemma 12.22
.

Let
X_{\bullet}
be a derived Lie
\infty
-groupoid in
\mathsf{dM}
, then we have a factorization

{\mathbf{R}}\mathsf{y}X_{\bullet}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s^{*}_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}({\mathbf{R}}\mathsf{y}X_{\bullet})^{\Delta[1]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d^{*}_{0},d^{*}_{1})}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbf{R}}\mathsf{y}X_{\bullet}\times{\mathbf{R}}\mathsf{y}X_{\bullet}

where
s^{*}_{0}
is a stalkwise weak equivalence and
(d^{*}_{0},d^{*}_{1})
is a stalkwise Kan fibration.

Proof.

Note that for any
\mathsf{p}\in\mathcal{P}
,
\mathsf{p}X
is a Kan complex in
s\mathsf{Set}
. Applying
\mathsf{p}
to the previous diagram we have

\mathsf{p}X_{\bullet}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{p}(s^{*}_{0})}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\mathsf{p}X_{\bullet})^{\Delta[1]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{p}(d^{*}_{0},d^{*}_{1})}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{p}X_{\bullet}\times\mathsf{p}X_{\bullet}

where

\big(\mathsf{p}X_{\bullet}\big)_{n}^{\Delta[1]}=\big(\mathsf{p}(\mathsf{y}X_{n})\big)^{\Delta[1]}=\operatorname{Hom}_{s\mathsf{Set}}(\Delta[n]\times\Delta[1],\mathsf{p}X_{\bullet})

which is the path object in
s\mathsf{Set}
for
\mathsf{p}X_{\bullet}
.
∎

Lemma 12.23
.

(\mathsf{y}X_{\bullet})^{\Delta[1]}\in s\mathsf{Sh}(\mathsf{dM})
is a representable simplicial presheaf which is represented by a derived Lie
\infty
-groupoid
X^{\Delta[1]}_{\bullet}
.

Proof.

First note that by the simplicial structure of simplicial sheaves

\displaystyle(\mathbf{R}\mathsf{y}X_{\bullet})^{\Delta[1]}(U)_{n}

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{s\mathsf{Set}}(\Delta[n]\times\Delta[1],{\mathbf{R}}\mathsf{y}X_{\bullet}(U))

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{s\mathsf{dM}}\big((\Delta[n]\times\Delta[1])\otimes U,X_{\bullet}\big)

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{s\mathsf{dM}}\big((U,\operatorname{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{s\mathsf{Set}}(\Delta[n]\times\Delta[1],X_{\bullet})\big)

In order for
({\mathbf{R}}\mathsf{y}X_{\bullet})^{\Delta[1]}
to be representable, we to show that
\operatorname{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{s\mathsf{Set}}(\Delta[n]\times\Delta[1],X_{\bullet})
is a derived manifold.

Note that
\Delta[n]\times\Delta[1]
has a canonical decomposition into
(n+1)

n+1
-simplices
\Delta[n]
, hence we have

{\mathbf{R}}\operatorname{Hom}(\Delta[n]\times\Delta[1],X_{\bullet})\simeq X_{n+1}\times_{d_{1},d_{1}}^{h}X_{n+1}\times_{d_{2},d_{2}}^{h}\cdots\times_{d_{n},d_{n}}^{h}X_{n+1}

which is a derived manifold. Hence,
({\mathbf{R}}\mathsf{y}X_{\bullet})^{\Delta[1]}
is represented by a simplicial derived manifold, and we denote it by
X_{\bullet}^{\Delta[1]}
.

Next, we want to prove that
X_{\bullet}^{\Delta[1]}
is a derived Lie
\infty
-groupoid, i.e.
X_{\bullet}^{\Delta[1]}(\Delta[n])\to X_{\bullet}^{\Delta[1]}(\Lambda^{i}[n])
is a cover.

First we have

M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[n]}X_{\bullet}^{\Delta[1]}=\operatorname{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{s\mathsf{Set}}(\Lambda^{i}[n],X_{\bullet}^{\Delta[1]})

which is characterized by the sheaf

U\mapsto\operatorname{Hom}_{s\mathsf{dM}}(\Lambda^{i}[n]\otimes U,X_{\bullet}^{\Delta[1]})

Since

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{s\mathsf{dM}}(\Lambda^{i}[n]\otimes U,X_{\bullet}^{\Delta[1]})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{s\mathsf{Set}}(\Lambda^{i}[n],{\mathbf{R}}\mathsf{y}\big(X_{\bullet}^{\Delta[1]})(U)\big)

note that by construction
{\mathbf{R}}\mathsf{y}\big(X_{\bullet}^{\Delta[1]})\simeq({\mathbf{R}}\mathsf{y}X_{\bullet})^{\Delta[1]}

we get

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{s\mathsf{Set}}(\Lambda^{i}[n],{\mathbf{R}}\mathsf{y}\big(X_{\bullet}^{\Delta[1]})(U)\big)

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{s\mathsf{Set}}\big(\Lambda^{i}[n]\times\Delta[1],{\mathbf{R}}\mathsf{y}X_{\bullet}(U)\big)

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{s\mathsf{dM}}\big((\Lambda^{i}[n]\times\Delta[1])\otimes U,X_{\bullet}\big)

Hence, we have
M^{h}_{\Lambda^{i}[n]}X_{\bullet}^{\Delta[1]}\simeq\operatorname{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{s\mathsf{Set}}\big((\Lambda^{i}[n]\times\Delta[1]),X_{\bullet}\big)
.

We will show that
\operatorname{Hom}(\Delta[n]\times\Delta[1],X_{\bullet})\to\operatorname{Hom}\big((\Lambda^{i}[n]\times\Delta[1]),X_{\bullet}\big)
is a cover by induction. Since
\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda^{i}[1]\times\Delta[1]X_{\bullet})\simeq\hom(\Delta[1],X_{\bullet})\simeq X_{1}
which is representable. Combining this with the fact that
\Lambda^{i}[1]\times\Delta[1]\hookrightarrow\Delta[1]\times\Delta[1]
is a collapsible extension and
X_{\bullet}
is an
\infty
-groupoid object, the base case holds. Now consider
n>1
and
\operatorname{Kan}(k,i)
holds for all
k<n,0\leq i\leq k
. Since
\Lambda^{i}[n]\hookrightarrow\Delta[n]
is a collapsible extension, we have
\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda^{i}[n],X_{\bullet}^{\Delta[1]})
is representable. Therefore,
\operatorname{Hom}(\Delta[n]\times\Delta[1],X_{\bullet})\to\operatorname{Hom}\big((\Lambda^{i}[n]\times\Delta[1]),X_{\bullet}\big)
is a cover.
∎

Remark 12.24
.

If
X_{\bullet}
happens to be a Lie
k
-groupoid for some
k<\infty
, we can actually show that
X_{\bullet}^{\Delta^{1}}
is also a Lie
k
-groupoid.

Now by our previous construction, we have a factorization
X_{\bullet}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s^{*}_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X^{\Delta[1]}_{\bullet}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d^{*}_{0},d^{*}_{1})}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{\bullet}\times X_{\bullet}
with

d^{*}_{i}\circ s_{0}^{*}=\operatorname{Id}_{X_{\bullet}}
, where
s^{*}_{0}:X_{\bullet}\simeq X_{\bullet}^{\Delta[0]}\to X_{\bullet}^{\Delta[1]}
is induced by
s_{0}:\Delta[1]\to\Delta[0]\subset\partial\Delta[1]
, and
(d^{*}_{0},d^{*}_{1}):X_{\bullet}^{\Delta[1]}\to X_{\bullet}\times X_{\bullet}\simeq X_{\bullet}^{\Delta[0]}\times X_{\bullet}^{\Delta[0]}
is induced by
(d_{0},d_{1}):\partial\Delta[1]\to\Delta[1]
. Note that
s^{*}_{0}
is a stalkwise weak equivalence by definition. Hence, we simply need to show
f=(d^{*}_{0},d^{*}_{1})
is a Kan fibration, i.e. for all
n>=1
and
0\leq j\leq n
, the morphisms of sheaves

X_{n}^{\Delta[1]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\iota^{*}_{n,j},f_{*})}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbf{R}}\operatorname{Hom}(\iota_{n,j},f)

can be represented by a cover. By the isomorphism

{\mathbf{R}}\operatorname{Hom}(L,X^{K})\simeq{\mathbf{R}}\operatorname{Hom}(L\times K,X)

for any finitely generated simplicial set
K
and, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbf{R}}\operatorname{Hom}(\iota_{n,j},f)=

\displaystyle{\mathbf{R}}\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda^{j}[n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{n,j}}}{{\rightarrow}}\Delta[n],X^{\Delta[1]}_{\bullet}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{\bullet}\times X_{\bullet})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M^{h}_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}X^{\Delta[1]}_{\bullet}\times_{M^{h}_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}(X_{\bullet}\times X_{\bullet})}M^{h}_{\Delta[n]}(X_{\bullet}\times X_{\bullet})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathbf{R}}\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda^{j}[n]\times\Delta[1],X_{\bullet})\times_{{\mathbf{R}}\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda^{j}[n]\times\partial\Delta[1],X_{\bullet})}{\mathbf{R}}\operatorname{Hom}(\Delta[n]\times\partial\Delta[1],X_{\bullet})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathbf{R}}\operatorname{Hom}\big((\Lambda^{j}[n]\times\Delta[1])\sqcup_{\Lambda^{j}[n]\times\partial\Delta[1]}(\Delta[n]\times\partial\Delta[1]),X_{\bullet}\big)

Hence, the
\operatorname{Kan}(n,j)
condition simplifies to

{\mathbf{R}}\operatorname{Hom}(\Delta[n]\times\Delta[1],X_{bt})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\iota^{*}_{n,j},f_{*})}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbf{R}}\operatorname{Hom}\big((\Lambda^{j}[n]\times\Delta[1])\sqcup_{\Lambda^{j}[n]\times\partial\Delta[1]}(\Delta[n]\times\partial\Delta[1]),X_{\bullet}\big)

being a cover.
Since
(\Lambda^{j}[n]\times\Delta[1])\sqcup_{\Lambda^{j}[n]\times\partial\Delta[1]}(\Delta[n]\times\partial\Delta[1])\to\Delta[n]\times\Delta[1]
is a collapsible extension by Lemma
11.46
, it suffices to show that

{\mathbf{R}}\operatorname{Hom}(\iota_{n,j},f)={\mathbf{R}}\operatorname{Hom}\big((\Lambda^{j}[n]\times\Delta[1])\sqcup_{\Lambda^{j}[n]\times\partial\Delta[1]}(\Delta[n]\times\partial\Delta[1]),X_{\bullet}\big)

is represented by a cover for all
n,j
and then applying Lemma
11.38
we are done.

First consider
n=1
. We have a pullback square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{R}}\operatorname{Hom}(\iota_{n,j},f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}\times X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}\times X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{j},d_{j})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{0},d_{1})}

(d_{j},d_{j})
is clearly a cover, hence the pullback exists and
{{\mathbf{R}}\operatorname{Hom}(\iota_{n,j},f)}
is representable. For higher
j,n
we can apply Lemma
11.46
and proceed by induction.

Remark 12.25
.

The canonical identification of
\Delta[n]\times\Delta[1]=\cup_{1\leq k\leq n}x_{k}
as
n+1

(n+1)
-simplices is constructed by defining
x_{k}
to be the
(n+1)
-simplex generated by the following points

\big\{(0,0),(1,0),\cdots,(k,0),(k,1),(k+1,1),\cdots,(n,1)\big\}

Let’s look at first few examples. For
n=0
, we have
\Delta[0]\times\Delta[1]\simeq{\Delta[1]}
which is a single 1-simplex. For
n=1
, the decomposition of
\Delta[1]\times\Delta[1]
is
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,0)}}

where
x_{0}
is the 2-simplex generated by
\{(0,0),(0,1),(1,1)\}
which corresponds to the upper 2-simplex, and
x_{1}
is then the 2-simplex generated by
\{(0,0),(1,1),(1,1)\}
which corresponds to the lower 2-simplex. The fiber product quotients out the edge
(0,0)\to(1,1)
.

Similarly, for
n=2
. we decompose
\Delta[2]\times\Delta[1]
as three 3-simplices
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(2,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(2,0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,0)}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

where
x_{0}
is the 2-simplex generated by
\{(0,0),(0,1),(1,1),(2,1)\}
which corresponds to the 2-simplex bounded by the diagonal edges
f
and
g
,
x_{1}
is the 2-simplex generated by
\{(0,0),(1,0),(1,1),(2,1)\}
which corresponds to the 2-simplex bounded by the diagonal edges
h
and
f
, and

x_{2}
is the 2-simplex generated by
\{(0,0),(0,1),(0,2),(2,1)\}
which corresponds to the 2-simplex bounded by the diagonal edges
h
and
g
.

Remark 12.26
.

Note that for constant simplicial objects without underlying homotopy theory, the path objects are trivial. For example, take
M
be a Banach manifold and consider
M_{\bullet}
with
M_{i}=M
and all structure maps are identities. Let’s look at
M_{\bullet}^{\Delta[1]}
. The 0-simplex is simply
\operatorname{Hom}(\Delta[0]\times\Delta[1]\to M_{\bullet})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\Delta[1]\to M_{\bullet})\simeq M
. The 1-simplex is

(12.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(\Delta[0]\times\Delta[1]\to M_{\bullet})

\displaystyle\simeq M_{2}\times_{d_{1},d_{1}}M_{2}

(12.2)

\displaystyle\simeq M_{2}\times_{M_{1}}M_{2}\simeq M

By similar computation we see that
M_{\bullet}^{\Delta[1]}
is just the
M
itself. This justifies that we do not suppose any homotopy theory on Banach manifolds.

Theorem 12.27
.

Let
(\mathsf{dM},\mathcal{T})
be a category with pretopology, then the category of derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids in
(\mathsf{dM},\mathcal{T})
,
\mathsf{Lie_{\infty}Grpd}_{\mathsf{dM}}
, carries a category of fibrant object structure, where fibrations are Kan fibrations, and weak equivalences are stalkwise weak equivalences.

By our construction, the result can be adapted to any homotopy descent categories.

Corollary 12.28
.

Let
{\mathsf{C}}
be a homotopy descent category, then the category of derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids in
{\mathsf{C}}
is a category of fibrant objects.

First, let’s verify axiom (4), (5).

Lemma 12.29
.

For a homotopy descent category with pretopology
(\mathsf{dM},\mathcal{T})
,
\mathsf{Lie_{\infty}Grpd}_{\mathsf{dM}}
satisfies:

•

All isomorphisms in
\mathsf{Lie_{\infty}Grpd}_{\mathsf{dM}}
are both weak equivalences and Kan fibrations.

•

Weak equivalences satisfy 2-out-of-3.

•

Composition of fibrations are fibrations.

Proof.

(1) is trivial. For (2), note that weak equivalence of simplicial set satisfies 2-out-of-3, and by the construction of stalkwise weak equivalences, it is obvious that it also satisfies 2-out-of-3. (3) follows from Proposition
12.12
.
∎

(2) and (3) follows from Proposition
12.16
and Proposition
12.18
respectively. (1) and (7) are trivial by our construction. Therefore, we finish the proof.

12.3.
iCFO for incomplete derived spaces

For incomplete or non-small categories, the previous technique won’t work. The main issue is that due to lack of limits, pullback might not exist in general. Hence, we can only construct an iCFO structure. We will take advantage of the technique developed in
[
RZ20
]
which looks at category with locally stalkwise pretopology.

Theorem 12.30
.

Given an incomplete category with locally stalkwise pretopology
(\mathsf{dM},\mathcal{T})
, then the category of derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids in
(\mathsf{dM},\mathcal{T})
carries a category of fibrant object structure, where fibrations are Kan fibrations, and weak equivalences are stalkwise weak equivalences.

Note that we only need to verify (2) and (3), and all (4)-(7) follows from the homotopy descent category case.

Proposition 12.31
.

Let
f:X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
and
g:Z_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
be two morphisms in
\mathsf{Lie_{\infty}Grpd}_{\mathsf{dM}}
, where
f
is a Kan fibration and
g
is arbitrary. Suppose the pullback
Z_{0}\times_{Y_{0}}X_{0}
exists in
\mathsf{dM}
, then we have

(1)

All
Z_{k}\times_{Y_{k}}X_{k}
exists for
k>=1
, and the induced map
Z_{\bullet}\times_{Y_{\bullet}}X_{\bullet}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{f}}}{{\rightarrow}}Z_{\bullet}
is a Kan fibration in
s\mathsf{dM}
.

(2)

Z_{\bullet}\times_{Y_{\bullet}}X_{\bullet}
is a derived Lie
\infty
-groupoid in
\mathsf{dM}
.

Proof.

First, let’s look at (1). We want to show that the morphism of sheaves

Z_{n}\times_{Y_{n}}X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\iota^{*},{p_{f}}_{*})}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda^{j}[n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\to}}\Delta[n],Z_{\bullet}\times_{Y_{\bullet}}X_{\bullet}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{f}}}{{\rightarrow}}Z_{\bullet})

is represented by a cover. Since
M_{K_{\bullet}}(-):s\mathsf{dM}\to s\mathsf{Sh}(\mathsf{dM})
preserves limits, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(\iota,p_{f})=

\displaystyle M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}(Z_{\bullet}\times_{Y_{\bullet}}X_{\bullet})\times_{M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}Z_{\bullet}}Z_{n}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\big(M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}Z_{\bullet}\times_{M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}Y_{\bullet}}M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}X_{\bullet}\big)\times_{M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}Z_{\bullet}}Z_{n}

On the other hand, we also have a composition of pullbacks

(12.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}(\iota,p_{f})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}Z_{\bullet}\times_{M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}Y_{\bullet}}M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}X_{\bullet}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}Z_{\bullet}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}X_{\bullet}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}Y_{\bullet}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{pr}}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{pr}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{p_{f}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{pr}}_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}

Therefore we have

(12.4)

\operatorname{Hom}(\iota,p_{f})\simeq{M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}X_{\bullet}}\times_{M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}Y_{\bullet}}Z_{n}

We also have another compositions of pullbacks

(12.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{n}\times_{y_{n}}X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}(\iota,p_{f})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{n}\times_{M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}Y_{\bullet}}M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}X_{\bullet}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}X_{\bullet}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}Y_{\bullet}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota^{*},{p_{f}}_{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota^{*},f_{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{p_{f}}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}}

Now by assumption we see
(\iota^{*},f_{*})
is a cover, so we just need to show that
\operatorname{Hom}(\iota,p_{f})\simeq{M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}X_{\bullet}}\times_{M_{\Lambda^{j}[n]}Y_{\bullet}}Z_{n}
is representable, then
(\iota^{*},{p_{f}}_{*})
is a cover and
Z_{n}\times_{y_{n}}X_{n}
is representable.

We shall proceed by induction. Consider
n=1
. By the top square in diagram
12.3
, and replacing
\operatorname{Hom}(\iota,p_{f})
by the isomorphism
12.4
from the whole square, we have a pullback square

(12.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}\times_{Y_{0}}Z_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{0}\times_{Y_{0}}X_{0}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{pr}}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{pr}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{p_{f}}_{*}}

Note that
\iota^{*}=d_{j}:Z_{1}\to Z_{0}
which is a cover. Since
Z_{0}\times_{Y_{0}}X_{0}
is representable, so is
X_{0}\times_{Y_{0}}Z_{1}
and
{\operatorname{pr}}_{2}
is a cover. Hence,
X_{1}\times_{Y_{1}}Z_{1}
is representable and
p_{f}
satisfies Kan condition for
n=1.

Now suppose
p_{f}
satisfies Kan condition
\operatorname{Kan}(m,j)
for
1\leq m<n
and
1\leq j\leq m
, we want to show the Kan condition holds for
m=n
and
1\leq j\leq m
as well. Applying Lemma
11.40
with
S_{\bullet}=\Lambda^{j}[n]
and
T_{\bullet}=\Delta[n]
, we get that
\operatorname{Hom}(\iota_{n,j},p_{f})
is representable and
p_{f}
satisfies
\operatorname{Kan}(n,j)
for all
0\leq j\leq n
. Therefore,
p_{f}
is a Kan fibration.

Finally, let’s look at (3). By assumption
z:Z_{\bullet}\to*
is a Kan fibration, then by Proposition
12.12
,
z\circ p_{f}:Z_{\bullet}\times_{Y_{\bullet}}X_{\bullet}\to*
is also a Kan fibration. Hence,
Z_{\bullet}\times_{Y_{\bullet}}X_{\bullet}
is a Lie
\infty
-groupoid.
∎

Part IV
Homotopical algebra of derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids and algebroids

In this chapter,
we study homotopical algebras for derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids and algebroids and study their homotopy-coherent representations, which we call
\infty
-representations. We relate
\infty
-representations of
L_{\infty}
-algebroids to (quasi-) cohesive modules developed by Block, and
\infty
-representations of Lie
\infty
-groupoids to
\infty
-local system introduced by Block-Smith. Then we apply these tools in studying singular foliations and their characteristic classes. We then construct Atiyah classes for
L_{\infty}
-algebroids pairs.

12.4.
Semi-model categories

Definition 12.32

(
[
Nui19
]
,
[
WY18
]
)
.

Let
{\mathsf{C}}
be a bicomplete category. We say
{\mathsf{C}}
is a (left)
semi-model category
if it is equipped with wide subcategories of weak equivalences
\mathcal{W}
, cofibrations
\mathcal{C}
, and fibrations
\mathcal{F}
, which satisfy the following data:

(1)

The weak equivalences satisfy 2-out-of-3.

(2)

The weak equivalences, fibrations, and cofibrations are stable under retracts.

(3)

The cofibrations have the left lifting property with respect to the trivial fibrations. The trivial cofibrations with cofibrant domain (i.e. with a domain
X
for which the map
\to X
is a cofibration) have the left lifting property with respect to the fibrations.

(4)

Every map can be factored functorially into a cofibration followed by a trivial fibration. Every map with cofibrant domain can be factored functorially into a trivial cofibration followed by a fibration.

(5)

The fibrations and trivial fibrations are stable under transfinite composition, product, and base change.

Remark 12.33
.

It is also possible to define a semi-model category through specific adjunction to model categories. For more details, see
[
WY18
]
.

Remark 12.34
.

In a semi-model category, only the cofibrations and trivial fibrations are determined by each other via the lifting property, which implies that a semi-model structure is only determined by its weak equivalences and fibrations.

13.
Derived
L_{\infty}
-algebroid

Let
A
be a (unital) commutative dga over characteristic 0. The
tangent module

T_{A}
associated to
A
is defined by the space of graded
k
-derivations
\operatorname{Der}_{k}(A,A)
. Note that
T_{A}
is both a dg-
A
-module and dg-Lie algebra over
k
.

Definition 13.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a dg-
A
-module.
We say
\mathfrak{g}
is a
dg-Lie algebroid
over
A
if it also has a dg-Lie algebra structure over
k
and with anchor map
\rho:\mathfrak{g}\to T_{A}
satisfies

(1)

\rho
is a map of dg-
A
-modules;

(2)

\rho
is a map of dg-Lie algebras;

(3)

The following Leibniz rule holds

[x,a\cdot y]=(-1)^{|x|\cdot|a|}[x,y]+\rho(x)(a)y

for
a\in A
,
x,y\in\mathfrak{g}
.

Morphisms between two dg-
A
-algebroids over
A
are
A
-linear morphisms of dg-
A
-module over
T_{A}
which preserves the Lie brackets.

Definition 13.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a dg-
A
-module.
We say
\mathfrak{g}
is a
L_{\infty}
-algebroid
over
A
if it also has a
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure over
k
and with an anchor map
\rho:\mathfrak{g}\to T_{A}
which satisfies

(1)

\rho
is a map of dg-
A
-modules;

(2)

\rho
is a map of
L_{\infty}
-algebras;

(3)

The following Leibniz rule holds

(13.1)

\displaystyle[x,a\cdot y]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|x|\cdot|a|}a[x,y]+\rho(x)(a)y

(13.2)

\displaystyle[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n-1},a\cdot x_{n}]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n|a|}(-1)^{a(|x_{1}|+\cdots+|x_{n}|)}a[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}]\quad n\geq 3

for
a\in A
,
x,y,x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in\mathfrak{g}
.

Example 13.3
.

Let
A
be an ordinary
k
-algebra and
\mathfrak{g}
is
\geq 0
-graded, then an
L_{\infty}
-structure on
\mathfrak{g}
is equivalent to a differential on the Chevalley-Eilenberg algebra
\operatorname{Sym}_{A}(\mathfrak{g}[1])^{\vee}
.

In particular, if
A
is a (dg-)
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
-ring, we call
\mathfrak{g}
a (derived)NQ-(super)manifold.

Example 13.4

(Action
L_{\infty}
-algebroids)
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be an
L_{\infty}
-algebra, and
\rho:\mathfrak{g}\to T_{A}
be a map of
L_{\infty}
-algebra over
k
. We can equip
A\otimes\mathfrak{g}
with a structure of
L_{\infty}
-algebroid by extending
\rho
to an
A
-linear map, and brackets are given by

\displaystyle[a\otimes x,b\otimes y]=

\displaystyle\pm ab\otimes[x,y],+a\cdot\rho(x)(b)\otimes y-(\pm)b\cdot\rho(y)(a)\otimes x

\displaystyle[a_{1}\otimes x_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\otimes x_{n}]=

\displaystyle a_{1}\cdots a_{n}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}]

Here
\pm
is the Koszul sign for the grading.

Example 13.5

(Singular foliations)
.

Consider
A={\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(M)
. Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a singular foliation which admits a resolution by a complex of vector bundles
E_{\bullet}
, then we are able to construct an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid structure on
E_{\bullet}

[
LLS20
]
, which is called the
universal
L_{\infty}
-algebroid
of the singular foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
.

Apparently there are more choices of defining a sub-
L_{\infty}
-algebroids due to the homotopical nature of
L_{\infty}
-algebroids. We will use the following definition throughout this paper.

Definition 13.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid over
A
, then we define a
sub-
L_{\infty}
-algebroid
(or simply
subalgebroid
) of
\mathfrak{g}
to be a sub-
A
-module of the kernel of the anchor map which is also closed under the brackets and the differential. Later we will see that,
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}
inherits an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid structure, which plays the role of ’normal bundle’ of
\mathfrak{h}
.

We have two differential type of morphisms of
L_{\infty}
-algebroid.

Definition 13.7
.

A
(strict) morphism
between
L_{\infty}
-algebroids is a
A
-linear morphisms of dg-
A
-module over
T_{A}
which preserves the
L_{\infty}
structure.

In differential geometry, we often work with a weaker type of morphisms.

Definition 13.8
.

An
L_{\infty}
-morphism
\mathfrak{g}\leadsto\mathfrak{h}
between
L_{\infty}
-algebras is a
twisting cochain

\overline{C}_{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathfrak{h}[1]

or, equivalently, a map of commutative dg-coalgebra
\overline{C}_{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g})\to\overline{C}_{\bullet}(\mathfrak{h})
.

Definition 13.9
.

An
L_{\infty}
-morphism

\mathfrak{g}\leadsto\mathfrak{h}
between
L_{\infty}
-algebroids is an
L_{\infty}
-morphism of
L_{\infty}
-algebras
\tau:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{h}
such that

(1)

the composition
\rho_{\mathfrak{h}}:\overline{C}_{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathfrak{h}[1]\to T_{A}[1]
first takes the quotient by
\operatorname{Sym}_{k}^{\geq 2}\mathfrak{g}[1]\subset\overline{C}_{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g})
and then applies the anchor map
\rho
of
\mathfrak{g}
to the remaining of
\mathfrak{g}[1]
.

(2)

the map of graded vector spaces
\tau:\operatorname{Sym}_{k}^{\geq 1}\mathfrak{g}[1]\to\mathfrak{h}[1]
descends to a graded
A
-linear map.

Remark 13.10
.

One motivation for
L_{\infty}
-morphisms comes from differential geometry and mathematical physics. Consider
A
to be a ordinary algebra, and the dg-
A
-module underlying the
L_{\infty}
-algebroids
\mathfrak{g}
is a nonnegatively graded complexes of finitely generated projective
A
-module, then an
L_{\infty}
-morphism
\mathfrak{g}\leadsto\mathfrak{h}
is equivalent to a map of cdga’s
\operatorname{Sym}_{A}(\mathfrak{g}[1])^{\vee}\to\operatorname{Sym}_{A}(\mathfrak{h}[1])^{\vee}
. In fact, this is the same as the morphisms of NQ-manifolds or dg-manifolds. For example
A={\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}{(M)}
and
\mathfrak{g}
is a complex of finite dimensional vector bundles.

Theorem 13.11

(
[
Nui19
]
)
.

Let
A
be a fixed cdga. There is a
right proper, tractable semi-model structures on the category of derived
L_{\infty}
-algebroid
\mathsf{L_{\infty}Algd}^{\operatorname{dg}}_{A}
, in which a map is a weak equivalence (resp. a fibration) if and only if it is a quasi-isomorphism (a degreewise surjection).

Let
A
be a dg-
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
-ring (or simply a cdga over
{\mathbb{R}}
). Denote
A^{c}
the cofibrant replacement of
A
. The
\infty
-category associated to the semi-model category of
L_{\infty}
-algebroids over
A
is

\mathtt{L_{\infty}Algd}_{A}=\mathsf{L_{\infty}Algd}^{\operatorname{dg}}_{A^{c}}[W^{-1}].

Note that here we need to pass to the cofibrant replacement of
A
since the tangent module
T_{A}
is only homotopy invariant when
A
is cofibrant.

Proposition 13.12
.

There is combinatorial stable model category structures on the category of derived
L_{\infty}
-algebroid
\mathsf{L_{\infty}Algd}^{\operatorname{dg}}_{A}
, which presents the
\infty
-category
\mathtt{L_{\infty}Algd}
.

Definition 13.13
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a derived
L_{\infty}
-algebroid. Define
\Omega^{1}_{\mathfrak{g}}
to be the sheaf of smooth 1-form of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
. Note that
\Omega^{1}_{\mathfrak{g}}
is actually a sheaf of chain cochain complexes. We write
\Omega^{p}_{\mathfrak{g}}=\bigwedge^{p}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\Omega^{1}_{\mathfrak{g}}
. Denote the chain cochain complexes of global sections
\Gamma(\mathfrak{g},\Omega^{p}_{\mathfrak{g}})
by
\Omega^{p}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(g)}
.

Define
T_{\mathfrak{g}}
to be the sheaf
\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{g}}}(\Omega^{1}_{\mathfrak{g}},{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{g}})
, and let
T_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g})}
denote the chain cochain complexes of the global section
\Gamma(\mathfrak{g},T_{\mathfrak{g}})
.

Remark 13.14
.

Here
\Omega^{1}_{\mathfrak{g}}
is not the module of Kähler differential of the cdga
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g})
since we need the derivation
d:{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g})\to\Omega^{1}_{\mathfrak{g}}
to be a
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
-derivation when restricted to
A^{0}
.

Definition 13.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a derived
L_{\infty}
-algebroid over
A
. We define the de Rham complex
\mathsf{dR}(\mathfrak{g})
to be the product of total cochain complex of the triple complex

{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{\mathsf{dR}}}}{{\to}}\Omega^{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{\mathsf{dR}}}}{{\to}}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{\mathsf{dR}}}}{{\to}}\cdots

therefore,
\mathsf{dR}(\mathfrak{g})^{m}=\prod_{i+j-k=m}(\Omega^{i}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g})})^{j}_{k}
with total differential
D=\delta+d^{\operatorname{CE}}+d^{\mathsf{dR}}
, where
d^{\operatorname{CE}}
is the Chevalley-Eilenberg differential on
\mathfrak{g}
and
d^{\mathsf{dR}}
is the de Rham differential defined above.

13.1.
Perfect complexes

Definition 13.16
.

Let
M
be a derived manifold, a quasi-coherent sheaf
{\mathcal{E}}
is a
perfect complex
if it is locally finitely presentable, i.e for every
x\in M
, there exists an open neighborhood
U
such that
{\mathcal{E}}|_{U}
can be obtained from the structure sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}|_{U}
be finite limits and colimits. We denote
\mathsf{Perf}(M)\subset\mathsf{QCoh}(M)
the category of perfect complexes.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold, then the most common perfect complexes are finite chain complexes of vector bundles. On the other hand, any perfect complex on
M
is locally quasi-isomorphic to finite chain complexes of vector bundles. For compact manifolds, global resolutions exist.

Definition 13.17
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}\in\mathsf{Perf}(M)
. We say
{\mathcal{E}}
has
Tor-amplitude
contained in
[a,b]
if the associated sheaf
{\mathcal{E}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{M}}\pi_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{M})
’s homotopy sheaves vanish outside degree
[a,b]
. We denote the subcategory of perfect complexes with Tor-amplitude contained in
[a,b]
by
\mathsf{Perf}^{[a,b]}(M)
.

14.
\infty
-Lie differentiation

Definition 14.1
.

Let
A
be a commutative cosimplicial dga, we construct its
normalization
as follows.

First, we define a cochain complex
N^{\bullet}A
where

N^{m}A=\{a\in A^{m}:\sigma^{j}\in A^{m-1},0\leq j\leq m\}

with differential
d=\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\partial_{i}
. Next we define an associative product
\cup
similar to the usual Alexander-Whitney product on
N^{\bullet}A
by

a\cup b=(\partial^{[m+1,m+n]}a)\cdot(\partial^{[1,m]}b)

for
a\in N^{m}A,b\in N^{n}B
.

Now we define a commutative cochain algebra
D^{\bullet}A
as the quotient of
N^{\bullet}A
by

(\partial^{I}a)\cdot(\partial^{J}b)\simeq\begin{cases}(-1)^{(J,I)}(a\cup b)&a\in A^{|J|},b\in A^{|I|}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

For each disjoint sets (possibly empty)
I,J
. Here
(-1)^{(J,I)}
denote the sign of permutation of integers
I\sqcup J
which sends first
|I|
elements to
I
(in order) and the left to
T
(in order).

We can easily see that
D^{\bullet}
is a functor from the category of cosimplicial cdga’s to the category of stacky cdga’s. In fact, we have:

Proposition 14.2

(
[
Pri17
,
Lemma3.5
]
)
.

D^{\bullet}
is a left Quillen functor from the Reedy model structure on cosimplicial cdga’s to the model structure on stacky cdga’s in Lemma
15.7
.

Next, we can define a generalization of constructing Lie algebroids from Lie groupoids.

Definition 14.3
.

Let
G_{\bullet}
be a derived Lie
\infty
-groupoid, define the normalization
NG
to be a derived
L_{\infty}
-algebroid with structure sheaf
D^{\bullet}\big((\sigma^{0})^{-\bullet}{\mathcal{O}}_{G}\big)
, where
(\sigma^{0})^{-\bullet}{\mathcal{O}}_{G}
is the cosimplicial sheaf with

\big((\sigma^{0})^{-\bullet}{\mathcal{O}}_{G}\big)^{m}=\big((\sigma^{0})^{m}\big)^{-1}{\mathcal{O}}_{G_{m}}

Lemma 14.4

(
[
Pri20a
]
)
.

Let
G_{\bullet}
a derived Lie
n
-groupoid, then its normalization is an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid with degree
\leq n
.

Proof.

Similar to the standard Dold-Kan construction. See
[
Pri20a
, Lemma 4.17]
.
∎

14.1.
Tangent complex

Let
A\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\mathsf{Alg}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}
be a dg
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
ring and
E\in\mathsf{Mod}_{A}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}
be a dg module over
A
. A
multiderivation
of degree
n
is a graded symmetric multilinear map

D:E^{\otimes(n+1)}\to E

which is a derivation in each variable, i.e. there is a
symbol
map
\sigma_{D}:E^{\otimes(n+1)}\to T_{A}
such that the following graded Leibniz rule holds

(14.1)

D(s_{0},s_{1},\cdots fs_{n})=fD(s_{0},s_{1},\cdots s_{n})+\sigma_{D}(s_{0},s_{1},\cdots s_{n})(f)

Lemma 14.5
.

Let
D\in\operatorname{Der}^{n}(E)
, then we have the short exact sequence of dg modules

(14.2)

0\to\operatorname{Sym}^{n+1}E^{\vee}\otimes E\to\operatorname{Der}^{n}E\to\operatorname{Sym}^{n}E^{\vee}\otimes T_{A}\to 0

and
\operatorname{Der}^{n}(E)=0
for
n>\operatorname{rk}E
.

15.
Cohesive modules over stacky dga

15.1.
Stacky dga

Though the category of differential graded algebra suffices for most of our work, sometimes it is still necessary to consider a (cohomologically graded) cdga
A^{\bullet}
(for example, Chevalley-Eilenberg algebra of a (derived)
L_{\infty}
-algebroid), where
A^{0}
is a (homologically graded) dga. Hence, we are looking for a specific kind of double complex where the homological dga and cohomological dga structures are compatible.

Definition 15.1
.

Define a chain-cochain complex
V
over
k
to be a bigraded
k
-vector space equipped with two differentials
d:V^{i}_{j}\to V_{j}^{i+1}
and
\delta:V^{i}_{j}\to V_{j-1}^{j}
such that
(d+\delta)^{2}=d\delta+\delta d=0.

There is an obvious tensor product
\otimes
in the category defined above, which allows us to define the algebra structure on it.

Definition 15.2

(
[
Pri17
]
)
.

A
stacky dga

A
is a chain-cochain complex
A^{\bullet}_{\bullet}
equipped with a commutative product
A\otimes A\to A
and a unit
k\to A
. We can regard all chain complexes as chain-cochain complexes by
V=V^{0}_{\bullet}
. Given a chain dga
R
, a stacky dga
A
over
R
is given by a map of stacky dga
R\to A
. If in addition
A
is graded commutative, then we say
A
is a
stacky cdga
.

As the name suggests, ’stacky’ means these dga’s are enhanced in the ’stacky’ direction, i.e. they are not only model for derived spaces, but also derived (infinitesimal) stacks. We denote
\mathsf{dgCAlg}_{R}^{{\operatorname{St}}}
the category of stacky cdga’s over
R
, and
\mathsf{dg^{\geq 0}CAlg}_{R}^{{\operatorname{St}}}
the full subcategory consists of stacky cdga’s which are concentrated in non-negative cochain degrees.

Example 15.3

(derived
L_{\infty}
-algebroids)
.

A large class of stacky cdga’s is given by derived
L_{\infty}
-algebroids. Recall that a derived
L_{\infty}
-algebroid
\mathfrak{g}
over a derived manifold
(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
is given by an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid structure over the dga
A=\Gamma({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
. The chain part is given by the derived direction

\cdots{\mathcal{O}}_{X,2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta}}{{\to}}{\mathcal{O}}_{X,1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta}}{{\to}}{\mathcal{O}}_{X,0}={\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(X)

and the cochain part is given by the stacky direction

{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(X)=(\operatorname{Sym}\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}[-1])^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}(\operatorname{Sym}\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}[-1])^{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}(\operatorname{Sym}\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}[-1])^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\cdots

Example 15.4

(BRST complex for coisotropic reduction)
.

BRST complexes, introduced in
[
BRS75
]
[
Tyu75
]
, is a tool in mathematical physics to describe both the homotopy quotients and homotopy intersections.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold of dimension
2n
, and
M_{0}
a coisotropic submanifold of codimension
k
, i.e.
(T_{p}M_{0})^{\perp}\subset T_{p}M_{0}
for all
p\in M_{0}
. For simplicity, we assume that
M_{0}
has a trivial normal bundle. Now we can write
M_{0}
as the zero set of a smooth function
\phi:M\to V
, where
V
is a vector space of codimension
k
. Pick a basis
\{e_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq k}
for
V
, then we can write
\phi=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\phi_{i}e_{i}
, where
\phi_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(M)
. Since
M_{0}
is a submanifold of
M
,
\phi_{i}
’s generate the vanishing ideal
\mathcal{I}
of
M_{0}
, so

\mathcal{I}=\{\sum_{i}f_{i}\phi_{i}|f_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(M)\}

Note that, since
M_{0}
is coisotropic,
\mathcal{I}
is closed under the Poisson bracket, i.e.
\{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}\}\subset\mathcal{I}
.

Now we define the BRST complex by

C^{p,q}=\bigwedge^{p}V^{\vee}\otimes\bigwedge^{q}V

where we regard
V
as a trivial vector bundle on
M
.
Clearly, by the graded algebra structure induced from exterior product, we get a stacky dga
A^{\bullet}_{\bullet}
with
A^{q}_{p}=C^{p,q}
, with
D=d+(-1)^{i}\delta
where
d
is the Chevalley-Eilenberg differential and
\delta
is the Koszul differential. Note that
H^{0}(A)\simeq{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(M_{0}/{\mathcal{F}})
as a Poisson algebra, where
{\mathcal{F}}
is the foliation generate by the
(TM_{0})^{\perp}
. This is a prototypical example of a stacky cdga, where the underlying geometric space is the leaf space of the foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
on
M_{0}
. We can see that the derived direction is a generalization of submanifolds or subspaces, whereas the stacky direction generalizes the (homotopy) quotient or orbit space.

Example 15.5

(de-Rham algebras of derived manifolds)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
be a derived manifold
(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
. Denote the chain complex of the global sections of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
by
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(X)
. Recall
\Omega_{X}^{1}
denotes the sheaf of chain complexes of smooth 1-form on
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
, and
\Omega^{P}_{X}=\bigwedge^{p}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}\Omega^{1}_{X}
. The de Rham complex
\mathsf{dR}(X)
is the product total cochain complex of the double complex,

{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\Omega^{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(X)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(X)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\cdots

hence
\mathsf{dR}(X)^{m}=\prod_{j}(\Omega^{m+j}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(X)})_{j}
.
\mathsf{dR}(X)
is then a stacky cdga with
D=d+(-1)^{i}\delta
, where
\delta
is the differential of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
, and the product structure comes from the exterior algebra.

Definition 15.6
.

Recall that a morphism
U\to V
between a chain-cochain complexes is a levelwise quasi-isomorphism if
U^{i}\to V^{i}
is a quasi-isomorphism for all
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. We call a morphism of stacky cdga’s a levelwise quasi-isomorphism if the underlying chain-cochain complex is so.

The following is
[
Pri17
]
, Lemma 3.4.

Lemma 15.7
.

There is a cofibrantly generated model structure on stacky cdga’s over
R
in which fibrations are surjections and weak equivalences are levelwise quasi-isomorphisms.

Next, we will generalize stacky dga’s to curved stacky dga’s, where the integrability condition
d^{2}=0
is no longer satisfied. Instead, we have a ’curvature’ for each stacky dga.

Definition 15.8
.

A
curved stacky dga
is a quadruple
\mathsf{A}=(A^{\bullet}_{\bullet},d,\delta,c)
where
A^{\bullet}_{\bullet}\in\mathsf{dg^{\geq 0}CAlg}_{k}
is a stacky dga where the cohomological degree is non-negatively graded, with a derivation
D=d+\delta
which satisfies the usual graded Leibniz rule and

D^{2}(a)=[c,a]

for a fixed
c\in(A^{\bullet}_{\bullet})^{2}
satisfying the Bianchi identity
Dc=0
.

[
PP05
]
[
Blo05
]
discuss the case where
A
are ordinary dga’s.

For simplicity, when we write a single superscript
A^{\bullet}
for a stacky dga, we will always mean total degrees, i.e
A^{n}=\oplus_{p-q=n}A^{p}_{q}
.

Example 15.9

(Endomorphism module of an affine derived manifold)
.

Example 15.10

(derived
L_{\infty}
-algebroids)
.

15.2.
Cohesive modules over stacky dga’s

Let
\mathsf{A}=(A^{\bullet}_{\bullet},d,\delta,c)
be a curved stacky dga, and let
E=E^{\bullet}
be a (right) dg-
A^{0}
-module.

Definition 15.11
.

Let
\mathbb{E}:E^{\bullet}\otimes_{{A}^{0}}A^{\bullet}_{\bullet}\to E^{\bullet}\otimes_{{A}^{0}}A^{\bullet}_{\bullet}
be a
k
-linear map of total degree one which satisfies the graded Leibniz rule

\mathbb{E}(e\omega)=(\mathbb{E}(e\otimes 1))\omega+(-1)^{|e|}ed\omega,

then we call
\mathbb{E}
a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection
on
E
.

A
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection is determined by its value on
E^{\bullet}
part. We can write
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}^{0}+\mathbb{E}^{1}+\mathbb{E}^{2}+\cdots
, where
\mathbb{E}^{k}:E^{\bullet}\to E^{\bullet-k+1}\otimes_{{A}^{0}}{A}^{k}
. Clearly,
\mathbb{E}^{1}
part corresponds to ordinary connections on each
E^{n}
, and
\mathbb{E}^{k}
is
A^{0}
-linear.

Note that the usual definition of curvature
\mathbb{E}^{2}
will not work, since it won’t be
{A}^{0}
-linear. Instead, we define the
relative curvature
of
\mathbb{E}
to be the operator

R_{\mathbb{E}}=\mathbb{E}^{2}(e)+e\cdot c

where
c
is the curvature of
{A}
. Note that
R_{\mathbb{E}}
is then
A^{0}
-linear.

Definition 15.12
.

Let
E=E^{\bullet}
be a dg
{A}^{0}
-module (bounded in both directions) together with a flat
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection
\mathbb{E}
, i.e.
R_{\mathbb{E}}=0
, then we call
E
a
quasi-cohesive module
. If
E
is also finitely generated and projective over
A^{0}
and bounded in both directions, then we call
E
a
cohesive module
. Denote the category of cohesive modules over
{A}
to be
\mathsf{Mod}^{{\operatorname{coh}}}_{{A}}
, and the category of quasi-cohesive modules by
\mathsf{Mod}^{{\operatorname{qcoh}}}_{{A}}
. Note that
\mathsf{Mod}^{{\operatorname{coh}}}_{{A}}
is the same as
\mathcal{P}_{A}
in
[
Blo05
]
,
[
BS14
]
,
[
BD10
]
, and
[
BZ
]
. For more about the theory of cohesive modules and quasi-cohesive modules, see
[
Blo05
]
.

We define the degree
k
morphisms between two cohesive modules
E_{1}=(E_{1}^{\bullet},\mathbb{E}_{1})
and
E_{2}=(E_{2}^{\bullet},\mathbb{E}_{2})
to be

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}^{k}(E_{1},E_{2})=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{A^{\bullet}}^{k}(E^{\bullet}_{1}\otimes_{A^{0}}A^{\bullet},E^{\bullet}_{2}\otimes_{A^{0}}A^{\bullet})

, i.e. the set of degree
k

A^{\bullet}
-linear map from
E^{\bullet}_{1}\otimes_{A^{0}}A^{\bullet}
to
E^{\bullet}_{2}\otimes_{A^{0}}A^{\bullet}
. By a similar argument as above, we have

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{A^{\bullet}}^{k}(E^{\bullet}_{1}\otimes_{A^{0}}A^{\bullet},E^{\bullet}_{2}\otimes_{A^{0}}A^{\bullet})=\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{0}}^{k}(E^{\bullet}_{1},E^{\bullet}_{2}\otimes_{A^{0}}A^{\bullet})

. We define a differential on the morphisms
d_{\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}}:\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}^{\bullet}(E_{1},E_{2})\to\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}^{\bullet+1}(E_{1},E_{2})\to
by

d_{\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}}(e)=\mathbb{E}_{2}(\phi(e))-(-1)^{|\phi|}\phi(\mathbb{E}_{1}(e)).

It is easy to verify that
d_{\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}}^{2}=0
, and hence
\mathsf{Mod}^{{\operatorname{coh}}}_{{A}}
is a dg-category.

Given a dg-category
{\mathsf{C}}
, we have a subcategory
Z^{0}({\mathsf{C}})
which has the same objects as
{\mathsf{C}}
and morphisms

Z^{0}({\mathsf{C}})(x,y)=Z^{0}({\mathsf{C}}(x,y))

i.e. degree 0 closed morphisms in
{\mathsf{C}}(x,y)
. On the other hand, we can form the
homotopy category

\mathsf{Ho}({\mathsf{C}})
which has the same objects as
{\mathsf{C}}
and morphisms,

\mathsf{Ho}({\mathsf{C}})(x,y)=H^{0}({\mathsf{C}}(x,y))

which is the 0th cohomology of the morphism complex.

Next, we will briefly discuss the triangulated structure of cohesive modules and explore homotopy equivalences between cohesive modules.

First, we define a shift functor. For
(E,\mathbb{E})\in\mathsf{Mod}^{{\operatorname{coh}}}_{{A}}
, we set
E[1]=(E[1]=(E^{\bullet+1},-{\mathbb{E}})
. Next, for
(E_{1},{\mathbb{E}}_{1}),(E_{2},{\mathbb{E}}_{2})\in\mathsf{Mod}^{{\operatorname{coh}}}_{{A}}
and
\phi\in Z^{0}\mathsf{Mod}^{{\operatorname{coh}}}_{{A}}(E_{1},E_{2})
, we define the cone of
\phi
,
C_{\phi}=(C_{\phi}^{\bullet},{\mathbb{E}}_{\phi})
by

C_{\phi}^{\bullet}=\begin{pmatrix}E_{2}^{\bullet}\\
\oplus\\
E_{1}[1]^{\bullet}\end{pmatrix}

and

C_{\phi}^{\bullet}=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbb{E}}_{2}&\phi\\
0&-{\mathbb{E}}_{1}^{\bullet}\end{pmatrix}

Now we have a triangle of degree 0 closed morphisms

(15.1)

{\mathcal{E}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi}}{{\to}}F\to C_{\phi}\to E[1]

Under this construction,
\mathsf{Mod}^{{\operatorname{coh}}}_{{A}}
is pre-triangulated, and
\mathsf{Ho}(\mathsf{Mod}^{{\operatorname{coh}}}_{{A}})
is triangulated with the collection of distinguished triangles being isomorphic to form
15.1
.

A degree 0 closed morphism
\phi\in\mathsf{Mod}^{{\operatorname{coh}}}_{{A}}(E_{1},E_{2})
is a
homotopy equivalence
if it induces an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Ho}(\mathsf{Mod}^{{\operatorname{coh}}}_{{A}})
. We will give a simple criterion to determine whether a map is a homotopy equivalence. Consider the following decreasing filtration

F^{k}\mathsf{Mod}^{{\operatorname{coh}}}_{{A}}(E_{1},E_{2})=\{\phi\in\mathsf{Mod}^{{\operatorname{coh}}}_{{A}}(E_{1},E_{2})|\phi^{i}=0\text{ for $i<k$}\}

Lemma 15.13
.

There exists a spectral sequence

E^{pq}_{0}\Longrightarrow H^{p+q}(\mathsf{Mod}^{{\operatorname{coh}}}_{{A}}(E_{1},E_{2}))

where

E^{pq}_{0}=\operatorname{gr}\big(\mathsf{Mod}^{{\operatorname{coh}}}_{{A}}(E_{1},E_{2})\big)=\{\phi^{p}\in(\mathsf{Mod}^{{\operatorname{coh}}}_{{A}})^{p+q}(E_{1},E_{2}):E_{1}^{\bullet}\to E_{2}^{\bullet+q}\otimes_{A_{0}}A^{p}\}

with differential
d_{0}(\phi^{p})={\mathbb{E}}_{2}\circ\phi^{p}-(-1)^{p+q}\phi^{p}\circ{\mathbb{E}}_{1}
.

Proposition 15.14
.

A closed morphism
\phi\in(\mathsf{Mod}^{{\operatorname{coh}}}_{{A}})^{0}(E_{1},E_{2})
is a homotopy equivalence if and only if
\phi^{0}:(E_{1}^{bt},{\mathbb{E}}_{1})\to(E_{2}^{bt},{\mathbb{E}}_{2})
is a quasi-isomorphism of complexes of
A^{0}
-modules.

Proof.

Follows from
[
Blo05
, Proposition 2.9]
.
∎

Definition 15.15
.

The
\infty
-category
\mathtt{Mod}_{{A}}^{{\operatorname{coh}}}
of cohesive modules over
{A}
is the
\infty
-category associated to the dg-category
\mathsf{Mod}_{{A}}^{{\operatorname{coh}}}
under the dg-nerve

\mathtt{Mod}^{Coh}_{\mathsf{A}}=\mathsf{N}_{{\operatorname{dg}}}(\mathsf{Mod}^{Coh}_{\mathsf{A}}).

16.
\infty
-representations

16.0.1.
\infty
-representations of
L_{\infty}
-algebroids

Definition 16.1
.

The
Chevalley-Eilenberg algebra
of
\mathfrak{g}
with coefficients in a dg
A
-module is the dga

\operatorname{CE}(\mathfrak{g},E)=\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\operatorname{Sym}_{A}\mathfrak{g}[-1],E)

with differential given by

(\partial\alpha)(X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{n})

If
E=\mathfrak{g}
, then we denote the
\operatorname{CE}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g})
simply by
\operatorname{CE}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Definition 16.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}\in\mathsf{L_{\infty}Algd}^{\operatorname{dg}}_{A}
, an
\infty
-representation
of
\mathfrak{g}
is a dg
\mathsf{A}
-module
E
, together with a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection

\nabla:\operatorname{CE}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{A}E\to\operatorname{CE}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{A}E

of total degree one which is flat and satisfies graded Leibniz rule

\nabla(\omega\eta)=d_{A}(\omega)\eta+(-1)^{|\omega|}\omega\nabla(\eta)

for all
\omega\in\operatorname{CE}(\mathfrak{g})
,
\eta\in\operatorname{CE}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{A}E
.

In literature,
\infty
-representations are also called
representations up to homotopy
or
sh-representations
. Denote the category of
\infty
-representations of an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid
\mathfrak{g}\in\mathsf{L_{\infty}Algd}^{\operatorname{dg}}_{A}
by
\mathtt{Rep}_{\mathfrak{g},A}

Proposition 16.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid over
\mathsf{A}
, and
E
a dg-
\mathsf{A}
-module, then an
\infty
-representation of
\mathfrak{g}

on
E
is equivalent to any of the following:

(1)

A quasi-cohesive module structure on
E
over
\operatorname{CE}(\mathfrak{g})
.

(2)

A quasi-cohesive module structure on
E^{\vee}
over
\operatorname{CE}(\mathfrak{g})
.

(3)

A square-zero degree 1 derivation
Q\in\operatorname{Der}({\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g},E))
extending the differential
d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})}
on
{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})
.

(4)

An Abelian extension
\mathfrak{g}\oplus E
of
\mathfrak{g}
along
E
:

•

L
is an
L_{\infty}
-subalgebroid;

•

E is an ideal, i.e.
\tilde{l}_{k}(E,\cdots)\subset E
, where
\tilde{l}_{k}
’s are the extension of
l_{k}
’s of
\mathfrak{g}
;

•

E is Abelian, i.e.
\tilde{l}_{k}
vanishes when evaluating at more than two elements of
E
.

(5)

The structure of a retract diagram of
L_{\infty}
-algebroids on
\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}\oplus E\to\mathfrak{g}
, which is square zero, i.e. all brackets vanish when evaluated on at least two elements of
E
.

(6)

A collection of operations
[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},-]:E\to E
of degree
|x_{1}|+\cdots|x_{n}|+n-2
for
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},e
such that

\displaystyle[x_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,x_{\sigma(n)},e]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\sigma}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},e]\quad\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}

\displaystyle[a\cdot x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},e]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(n-1)a}a\cdot[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},e]

\displaystyle[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},a\cdot e]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(n-1)a}a\cdot[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},e]\quad n\geq 2

\displaystyle[x_{1},a\cdot e]

\displaystyle=a\cdot[x_{1},e]+x_{1}(a)\cdot e.

Here we ignore all Koszul signs due to permutations of variables. Moreover, these brackets determines a module structure, i.e.

J^{n+1}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},a\cdot e)=0

for all
n\geq 0
.

(7)

An
L_{\infty}
-morphism
\mathfrak{g}\to\operatorname{At}(E)
.

Proof.

(1) is apparently equivalent to the definition of the
\infty
-representation.

(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2): This is apparent from the construction of
(E^{\bullet\vee},\mathbb{E}^{\vee}
, where

(\mathbb{E}^{\vee}\phi)(e)=d(\phi(e))-(-1)^{|\phi|}\phi(\mathbb{E}(e)).

(2)
\Leftrightarrow
(3): Obvious.

(3)
\Leftrightarrow
(4): Note that
Q^{2}=0
implies that
\mathfrak{g}\oplus E
is an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid, where the natural inclusion
\mathfrak{g}\subset\mathfrak{g}\oplus E
is a subalgebroid.

(i) is obvious. (ii) and (iii) follows from the fact that
Q(E^{\vee})\subset{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g},E^{\vee})
.

(4)
\Leftrightarrow
(5): Denote the
m
-ary bracket
[\cdots]
by
m_{k}
, then we simply set

m_{k}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k-1},e)=\tilde{l}_{k}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k-1},e).

The Jacobi identities follows from the
L_{\infty}
structure on
\mathfrak{g}\oplus E
.

(5)
\Leftrightarrow
(6) The
\infty
-representation of
\mathfrak{g}
on
E
is equivalent to the data of a twisting cochain
\tau:\overline{C}_{*}(\mathfrak{g})\to\operatorname{End}_{k}(E)[1]
, where
\operatorname{End}_{k}(E)
is the endomorphism Lie algebra of
E
, and
\tau
is given by

\operatorname{Sym}^{n}_{k}\mathfrak{g}[1]\to\operatorname{End}_{k}(E)[1]:x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{n}\to[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},-]

is equivalent to the data

(\rho,\tau):\overline{C}_{*}(\mathfrak{g})\to(T_{\mathsf{A}}\oplus\operatorname{End}_{k}(E))[1]

which is graded
\mathsf{A}
-linear and takes values in the Atiyah Lie algebroid of
E
.
∎

Hence, we see an
\infty
-representation of an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid
\mathfrak{g}
on
E
is equivalent to a cohesive module structure on
E
over
\operatorname{CE}(\mathfrak{g})
. Therefore, we get

Lemma 16.4
.

There exists an equivalence of dg-categories

\mathsf{Mod}^{Coh}_{\operatorname{CE}{(\mathfrak{g})}}\simeq\mathtt{Rep}_{\mathsf{A}}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g})

Hence we will use cohesive modules and
\infty
-representations over
L_{\infty}
-algebroids interchangeably. In particular, we call a cohesive module
E
over an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid
\mathfrak{g}
when
E
is a cohesive module over
\operatorname{CE}(\mathfrak{g})
. For simplicity, we will also call
E
a
\mathfrak{g}
-module if there is no confusion.

16.0.2.
\infty
-representations of simplicial sets

For any
K_{\bullet}
be a simplicial set, let
(C_{\bullet}(K_{\bullet}),\partial,\Delta)
be the dg coalgebra of simplicial chains on
K_{\bullet}
over
k
with the Alexander-Whitney coproduct
\Delta
. Consider the maps
\partial^{\prime}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}K(d_{i})
and the reduced coproduct
\Delta^{\prime}(x)=\Delta(x)-x\otimes 1-1\otimes x
, we get dg coalgebra structure on
C_{\bullet}(K_{\bullet})
and on the shifted graded module
s^{-1}C_{\bullet>0}(K_{\bullet})
.

We want to define a functor
\Lambda:s\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{dgCat}_{k}
. For
K_{\bullet}
, define a dg-category
\Lambda(K_{\bullet})
, where the objects are
K_{0}
, and for any
x,y\in K_{0}
we construct a chain complex

(\Lambda(K_{\bullet})(x,y),d_{\Lambda})

as follows:
\Lambda(K_{\bullet})(x,y)
is the quotient of a free
k
-module generated by monomials

(\sigma_{1}|\cdots|\sigma_{k})

, where each

\sigma_{i}\in s^{-1}C_{\bullet>0}(K_{\bullet})
is a generator and satisfies
\max\sigma_{i}=\min\sigma_{i+1}
, by the equivalence relations generated by

(1)

(\sigma_{1}|\cdots|\sigma_{k})\sim(\sigma_{1}|\cdots|\sigma_{i-1}|\sigma_{i+1}|\sigma_{k})

if
\sigma_{i}
is a degenerate 1-simplex for some
1\leq i\leq k
and
k\geq 2
;

(2)

(\sigma_{1}|\cdots|\sigma_{k})\sim 0
if
\sigma_{i}\in C_{n_{i}}(K_{\bullet})
is a degenerate simplex for some
1\leq i\leq k
,
n_{i}\geq 2
, and
k\geq 1
. Denote the equivalence class of
(\sigma_{1}|\cdots|\sigma_{k})
by
[\sigma_{1}|\cdots|\sigma_{k}]
.

Compositions are given by concatenations of monomials. The differential
d_{\Lambda}
is given by extending
-\partial^{\prime}+\Delta^{\prime}
as a derivation on monomials.
d_{\Lambda}
is then well-defined on equivalence classes and satisfies
d_{\Lambda}\circ d_{\Lambda}=0
.

Definition 16.5
.

We define the
dg nerve
functor
\operatorname{N}_{{\operatorname{dg}}}:s\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{dgCat}_{k}
by setting

\operatorname{N}_{{\operatorname{dg}}}({\mathsf{C}}):=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{dgCat}_{k}}(\Lambda(\Delta^{n}),{\mathsf{C}})

This definition agrees with Lurie’s dg-nerve functor, hence
\Lambda
is the left adjoint to Lurie’s dg-nerve, and
\mathsf{N}_{{\operatorname{dg}}}({\mathsf{C}})
is an
\infty
-category for any dg-category
{\mathsf{C}}
.

Let
\mathsf{Ch}_{k}
denote the dg-category of chain complexes over a field
k
of characteristic 0. Let
{\mathsf{C}}
be a dg-category and
\operatorname{N}_{{\operatorname{dg}}}{\mathsf{C}}\in s\mathsf{Set}
its dg nerve.

Definition 16.6
.

An
\infty
-representation
of
K_{\bullet}
valued in
{\mathsf{C}}
is an
\infty
-functor
\mathtt{F}:K_{\bullet}\to\operatorname{N}_{{\operatorname{dg}}}{\mathsf{C}}
, i.e. a morphism between the underlying simplicial sets. Denote
\mathtt{Rep}_{{\mathsf{C}}}(K_{\bullet})=\operatorname{Fun_{\infty}}(K_{\bullet},\operatorname{N}_{{\operatorname{dg}}}{\mathsf{C}})
the
\infty
-category of
\infty
-representations of
K_{\bullet}
valued in
{\mathsf{C}}
.

The
n
-simplices of
\mathtt{Rep}_{{\mathsf{C}}}(K_{\bullet})
are
\operatorname{Fun_{\infty}}(\Delta^{n}\times K_{\bullet},\operatorname{N}_{{\operatorname{dg}}}{\mathsf{C}})\simeq\mathsf{dgCat}_{k}\big(\Lambda(\Delta^{n}\times K_{\bullet}),{\mathsf{C}}\big)
.

Let’s look at the structure of an
\infty
-representation of a simplicial set.

Definition 16.7
.

Let
G_{\bullet}
be a (derived) Lie
\infty
-groupoid, then an
\infty
-representation of
G_{\bullet}
on a dg-category is defined as an
\infty
-representation of simplicial sets and all structure maps are required to be
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
. We denote the category of
\infty
-representation of a (derived) Lie
\infty
-groupoid by
\mathtt{Rep}_{{\mathsf{C}}}(G_{\bullet})
.

Lemma 16.8
.

Definition 16.9
.

We define an
\infty
-local system
\infty
-local system
on a (derived) Lie
{\infty}
-groupoid
G_{\bullet}
to be an
\infty
-representation of
G_{\bullet}
valued in
{\mathsf{C}}
.

Note that the data of an
\infty
-local system is roughly a simplicial map from the simplicial set
G_{\bullet}
to the dg-nerve of
\mathsf{Ch}_{k}
. By a Dold-Kan type correspondence, we can characterize the data of an
\infty
-local system as a dg-map between dg-categories.

Let
K_{\bullet}
be a Lie
\infty
-groupoid and
{\mathsf{C}}
a dg-category over
k
. Fix a map
F:K_{0}\to\operatorname{Obj}{\mathsf{C}}
, i.e. a map on 0-simplices. Define

{\mathsf{C}}^{i,j}_{F}=\{f:K_{i}\to{\mathsf{C}}^{j}|f(\sigma)\in{\mathsf{C}}^{j}(F(\sigma_{(i)}),F(\sigma_{(0)}))\}

and

{\mathsf{C}}^{k}_{F}(K_{\bullet})=\bigoplus_{i+j=k,k\geq 0}{\mathsf{C}}^{i,j}_{F}

Now
C_{F}(K_{\bullet})=\bigoplus_{k}{\mathsf{C}}^{k}_{F}(K_{\bullet})
forms a dga with differential
\hat{\delta}
and product
\cup
defined by

\displaystyle(\hat{\delta}f^{i})(\sigma_{i+1})=

\displaystyle\sum^{i}_{l=1}(-1)^{l+|f^{i}|}f^{i}(\partial_{l}(\sigma))

\displaystyle(f\cup g)(\sigma_{k})=

\displaystyle\sum_{t=0}^{k}(-1)^{t|g^{k-t}|}f^{t}(\sigma_{(0\cdots t)})g^{k-t}(\sigma_{(t\cdots k)})

Definition 16.10
.

We define an
\infty
-local system to be a pair
(F,f)
with
F:K_{0}\to\operatorname{Obj}{\mathsf{C}}
and
f\in C_{F}(K_{\bullet})
which satisfies Maurer-Cartan equation, i.e.
f\in C^{1}_{F}(K)
and

(16.1)

\displaystyle d_{F(\sigma_{(i)})}=f^{0}(\sigma_{(i)})

(16.2)

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}f+f\cup f=0.

Remark 16.11
.

By a little abuse of notation, we will refer an
\infty
-local system
(F,f)
simply by
F
. To avoid confusion about
F(x)
and
f(x)
for any zero simplices
x
, we will use
F_{x}
to denote the former, and
F(x)
to denote the latter.

Example 16.12
.

Let’s take
G_{\bullet}
to be the smooth fundamental groupoid
\Pi^{\infty}(M)
of a manifold
M
, and
{\mathsf{C}}=\mathsf{Ch}_{k}
. Then the data of an
\infty
-local system consists of:

(1)

A graded vector space
E_{x}=\bigoplus_{i}E^{i}_{x}
for
x\in M
.

(2)

A sequence of
k
-cochains
f^{k}\in\operatorname{Hom}^{1-k}(E_{\sigma_{(k)}},E_{\sigma_{(0)}})
for
\sigma\in\Pi^{\infty}(M)_{k}
, which satisfies equation
16.1
(note the notation is a little different here).

We can put a dg-category structure on the category of
\infty
-local systems. For two
\infty
-local systems
F,G
over
K_{\bullet}
valued in
{\mathsf{C}}
, define a complex of morphisms

\mathsf{Loc}_{{\mathsf{C}}}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}(K_{\bullet})(F,G)=\bigoplus_{i+j=k}\{\phi:K_{i}\to{\mathsf{C}}^{j}|\phi(\sigma)\in{\mathsf{C}}^{j}(F(\sigma_{(i)}),G(\sigma_{(0)})\}

and a differential
D
on it

D\phi=\hat{\delta}\phi+G\cup\phi-(-1)^{|\phi|}\phi\cup F.

where
\phi=\sum_{i\geq 0}\phi^{i}
with total degree
|\phi|=p
, and

(\hat{\delta}(\sigma_{k})=\delta\circ T=\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}(-1)^{j+|\phi|}\phi^{k-1}(\partial_{j}(\sigma_{k}))

This yields a dg-category
\mathsf{Loc}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}_{{\mathsf{C}}}
, where the composition of morphisms is given by
\cup
. Denote the corresponding
\infty
-category
\mathtt{Loc}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}_{{\mathsf{C}}}=\mathsf{Loc}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}_{{\mathsf{C}}}(W^{-1})
.

Proposition 16.13
.

Given a Lie
\infty
-groupoid
K_{\bullet}
and a dg-category
{\mathsf{C}}
. There exists an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\mathtt{Loc}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}_{{\mathsf{C}}}(K_{\bullet})\simeq\mathtt{Rep}^{\infty}_{{\mathsf{C}}}(K_{\bullet})

Proof.

See
[
Smi11
]
Appendix.
∎

For pre-triangulated
{\mathsf{C}}
, we can define shift and cone on
\mathsf{Loc}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}_{{\mathsf{C}}}(K_{\bullet})
.
First, let’s define the shift functor. Let
F\in\mathsf{Loc}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}_{{\mathsf{C}}}(K_{\bullet})
, we define
F[i]
by

\displaystyle F[i]_{(x\in K_{0})}=F_{x}[i]

\displaystyle F[i](\sigma_{k})=(-1)^{i(k-1)}F(\sigma_{k})

On morphisms, we define

\phi[i](\sigma_{k})=(-1)^{ik}\phi

Next, we define the cone. Given a morphism
\phi\in\mathsf{Loc}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}_{{\mathsf{C}}}(K_{\bullet})(F,G)
of total degree
i
. Define

\displaystyle C_{\phi}:K_{0}

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Obj}{\mathsf{C}}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto F[1-i]_{x}\oplus F_{x}

and
c_{\phi}\in{\mathsf{C}}^{1}_{C_{\phi}}
by

c_{\phi}=\begin{pmatrix}F[1-i]&0\\
\phi[1-i]&G\end{pmatrix}

Remark 16.14
.

Note that
(C_{\phi},c_{\phi})
will not be an
\infty
-local system in general unless
\phi
is closed.

Definition 16.15
.

Let
\phi\in\mathsf{Loc}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}_{{\mathsf{C}}}(K_{\bullet})(F,G)
be a degree 0 closed morphism, we say
\phi
is a
homotopy equivalence
if it induces an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Ho}\mathsf{Loc}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}_{{\mathsf{C}}}(K_{\bullet})
.

Next, we will give an easy criterion to determine whether a map is a homotopy equivalence. Consider the following decreasing filtration

F^{k}\mathsf{Loc}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}_{{\mathsf{C}}}(K_{\bullet})(F,G)=\{\phi\in\mathsf{Loc}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}_{{\mathsf{C}}}(K_{\bullet})(F,G)|\phi^{i}=0\text{for}i<k\}

Lemma 16.16
.

There exists a spectral sequence

E^{pq}_{0}\Longrightarrow H^{p+q}(\mathsf{Loc}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}_{{\mathsf{C}}}(K_{\bullet})(F,G))

where

E^{pq}_{0}=\operatorname{gr}\big(\mathsf{Loc}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}_{{\mathsf{C}}}(K_{\bullet})(F,G)\big)=\{\phi:K_{p}\to{\mathsf{C}}^{q}\phi(\sigma)\in{\mathsf{C}}^{q}(F(\sigma_{(p)}),G(\sigma_{(0)})\}

with differential
d_{0}(\phi^{p})=d_{G}\circ\phi^{p}-(-1)^{p+q}\phi^{p}\circ d_{F}
.

Corollary 16.17
.

The
E_{1}
-page of the above spectral sequence is a local system valued in graded vector space in the usual sense.

Now we can give the criterion we want.

Proposition 16.18
.

For
{\mathsf{C}}=\mathsf{Ch}_{k}
, a closed morphism
\phi\in\mathsf{Loc}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}_{{\mathsf{C}}}(K_{\bullet})^{0}(F,G)
is a homotopy equivalence if and only if
\phi^{0}:(F_{x},d_{F})\to(G_{x},d_{G})
is a quasi-isomorphism of complexes for all
x\in K_{0}
.

Proof.

Follows from
[
Blo05
, Proposition 2.9]
.
∎

17.
Cohomology of derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids

17.1.
Actions of derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids

Let’s look at the action of derived Lie
\infty
-groupoids on a general space. First, let’s recall the ordinary Lie groupoid action on a manifold. Let
G_{\bullet}
be a Lie groupoid acting on a manifold
M
. The data of this groupoid action is encoded in an
action groupoid

A_{\bullet}
and a groupoid morphism
\pi:A_{\bullet}\to G_{\bullet}
over a
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
map
M\to G_{0}
, where
A_{0}=M
and

A_{1}=M\times_{G_{0},t}G_{1}=\{(x,g):t(g)=\epsilon(y)

with structure maps
s(x,g)=xg,t(x,g)=x
. In fact we have a double pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\times_{G_{0},t}G_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{pr}}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}

Lemma 17.1
.

Kan fibrations between Lie groupoids are equivalent to the data of Lie groupoid actions.

Proof.

(\Leftarrow)
Given a Lie groupoid action
G_{\bullet}
on
M
, we get a groupoid morphism
\pi:A_{\bullet}\to G_{\bullet}
. It suffices to show
\pi
is a Kan fibration.
\operatorname{Kan}(1,0)
is equivalent to

A_{1}\to M\times_{\epsilon,G_{0},t}G_{1}

which is an isomorphism by construction. By applying the inverse map,
\operatorname{Kan}(1,0)
is also satisfied. Higher Kan conditions follows from degree 1 case.

(\Rightarrow)
Given a Kan fibration
\pi:A_{\bullet}\to G_{\bullet}
, we want to show there is an action of
G_{\bullet}
on
A_{0}
. By the
\operatorname{Kan}(1,0)
condition and unique Kan conditions for
n>1
, we see that

A_{1}\to A_{0}\times_{\epsilon,G_{0},t}G_{1}

Hence we can define
s(x,g)=xg,t(x,g)=x
, which gives us the desired data for action.
∎

Therefore, this inspires us to define a higher groupoid action using Kan fibrations.

Definition 17.2
.

Let
G_{\bullet}
be a Lie
\infty
-groupoid, then an
\infty
-action
of
G_{\bullet}
is a Kan fibration
\pi:A_{\bullet}\to G_{\bullet}
.

If
G_{\bullet}
be a Lie
n
-groupoid, an
n
-action is a
n
-Kan fibration
\pi:A_{\bullet}\to G_{\bullet}
of Lie
n
-groupoids.

In
[
Li15
]
the 2-groupoid case is shown to be the correct definition of actions.

17.2.
Derived Lie
\infty
-groupoid Cohomology

Definition 17.3
.

Let
G_{\bullet}
be a derived Lie
\infty
-groupoid over a dga
A
. We denote by
C^{\bullet}(G_{\bullet})
the smooth cochain complex on
G_{\bullet}
, where
C^{k}(G_{\bullet})
consists of smooth functions on
G_{k}
, i.e.

C^{k}(G_{\bullet})={\mathcal{O}}_{G_{k}}

The differential
d=\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}d^{*}_{i}
.

Consider a derived Lie
\infty
-groupoid
G_{\bullet}
over a cdga
A
, and
E_{\bullet}\in\mathsf{Mod}_{A}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}
. We form a dg-
C^{\bullet}(G_{\bullet})
-module
C^{\bullet}(G_{\bullet};E_{\bullet})
whose degree
k
part is

(17.1)

C^{k}(G_{\bullet};E_{\bullet})=\bigoplus_{i+j=k}\Gamma(G_{i};Q_{0}^{*}E_{j})

where
Q_{0}
is defined as

Q_{i}:=d_{1}\circ\cdots\circ d_{i}:G_{i}\to G_{0}

is the projection on the last vertex.

Definition 17.4
.

We define a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection

{\mathbb{E}}:C^{\bullet}(G_{\bullet};E_{\bullet})\to C^{\bullet+1}(G_{\bullet};E_{\bullet})

on
C^{\bullet}(G_{\bullet};E_{\bullet})
to be a
k
-linear map of total degree one which satisfies the grade Leibniz rule

\mathbb{E}(e\omega)=(\mathbb{E}(e\otimes 1))\omega+(-1)^{|e|}ed\omega,

for
e\in C^{\bullet}(G_{\bullet};E_{\bullet}),\omega\in C^{\bullet}(G_{\bullet})
.

Definition 17.5
.

We define an
\infty
-representation
of
G_{\bullet}
to be a dg-
A
-module
E_{\bullet}
together with a flat
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection on
C^{\bullet}(G_{\bullet};E_{\bullet})
.

We denote the resulting

We equip the category of
\infty
-representation a dg structure by defining the morphism complex

\mathsf{Rep}(G_{\bullet})(F,G)^{k}=\bigoplus_{i+j=k}\Gamma\big(G_{i},\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}^{j}(F,G)\big)

Proposition 17.6
.

Let
{\mathsf{C}}=\mathsf{Mod}_{A}^{{\operatorname{dg}}}
, then there exists an dg equivalence between
\mathsf{Loc}_{\infty}^{A}(G_{\bullet})
and
\mathsf{Rep}^{A}_{\infty}(G_{\bullet})
.

Proof.

It’s easy to see that
C^{\bullet}(G_{\bullet};E_{\bullet})
is a
C^{\bullet}(G_{\bullet})
-module generated by

\Gamma(G_{\bullet},E_{\bullet})=\bigoplus_{i+j=k}\Gamma(G_{k},Q_{1}^{*}E_{j})

The Leibniz rule for
{\mathbb{E}}
implies that we have a decomposition

{\mathbb{E}}={\mathbb{E}}^{0}+{\mathbb{E}}^{1}+{\mathbb{E}}^{2}+\cdots

where
{\mathbb{E}}^{i}\in\operatorname{Hom}\big(\Gamma(G_{\bullet},E_{\bullet}),\Gamma(G_{\bullet+i},E_{\bullet+1-i})\big)
. For
i\not=1
, since
D_{i}
is
C^{\bullet}(G_{\bullet})
-linear, we can identify them as an element of
\Gamma\Big(G_{i},\operatorname{Hom}^{1-i}\big(P^{*}_{i}(E_{\bullet}),Q^{*}_{0}(E_{\bullet})\big)\Big)
, which is exactly the
{\mathsf{C}}^{i,1-i}_{F}
we defined in
\infty
-local systems. For
i=1
,
{\mathbb{E}}^{1}
is a derivation, which can be identified as
{\mathbb{E}}^{1}=\hat{\delta}+\omega
for some
\omega\in\Gamma\Big(G_{1},\operatorname{Hom}^{0}\big(P^{*}_{1}(E_{\bullet}),Q^{*}_{0}(E_{\bullet})\big)\Big)
and
\hat{\delta}
is dual to the face map. Now the conditions for
E
to be an
\infty
-local system are

(1)

d_{F(\sigma_{(i)})}=f^{0}(\sigma_{(i)})
, which means
\mathbb{E_{0}}\in\Gamma\Big(G_{0},\operatorname{Hom}^{1}\big(P^{*}_{0}(E_{\bullet}),Q^{*}_{0}(E_{\bullet})\big)\Big)
is exactly the differential for
E_{\bullet}
.

(2)

\hat{\delta}f+f\cup f=0
means
{\mathbb{E}}\circ{\mathbb{E}}=0
.

which are both satisfied by the construction. The dg structures on
\mathsf{Loc}_{\infty}^{A}(G_{\bullet})
and
\mathsf{Rep}^{A}_{\infty}(G_{\bullet})
are exactly the same.
∎

Definition 17.7
.

We define the
differentiable cohomology
of a derived Lie
\infty
-groupoid valued in
E
to be

H^{\bullet}_{\operatorname{diff}}(G_{\bullet};E)=H^{\bullet}\big(C^{\bullet}(G_{\bullet};E_{\bullet}),{\mathbb{E}}\big)

18.
Chern-Weil theory for perfect dg modules

18.1.
Chern-Weil theory for perfect dg modules

Let
(E^{\bullet},\mathbb{E})
be a dg-
A^{0}
module over a dga
A^{0}
with a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection
\mathbb{E}
. In this section, we will develop a general theory of constructing characteristic classes valued in
A
. Later we shall apply it to singular foliations.

The curvature of the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection
\mathbb{E}
is defined by the usual formula

R_{\mathbb{E}}=\mathbb{E}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}[\mathbb{E},\mathbb{E}]\in A({\mathcal{F}},\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))

In order to define the characteristic forms, we need to define a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded supertrace map
\operatorname{Str}:A(M,\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))\to\mathsf{A}
. For each

\phi_{i}\in\Gamma(\operatorname{End}(E^{i}))
, define
\operatorname{Str}(\phi_{i})=(-1)^{i}\operatorname{Tr}(\phi_{i})
. Extend
\operatorname{Str}
to a (
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded)
A
-linear map we get
\operatorname{Str}:A({\mathcal{F}},\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))\to A({\mathcal{F}})
. Note that by construction
\operatorname{Str}
vanishes on
A({\mathcal{F}},\underline{\operatorname{End}}_{i}(E))
for all
i\not=0
.

Proposition 18.1

(Bianchi identity)
.

\mathbb{E}R_{\mathbb{E}}^{i}=0
for all
i\geq 1
.

Proof.

It follows from
\mathbb{E}R_{\mathbb{E}}^{i}=[\mathbb{E},\mathbb{E}^{2i}]=0
.
∎

Lemma 18.2
.

d_{\mathsf{A}}\operatorname{Str}(R_{\mathbb{E}}^{i})=0
.

Proof.

d_{\mathsf{A}}\operatorname{Str}(R_{\mathbb{E}}^{i})=\sum_{i}\operatorname{Str}(R_{\mathbb{E}}^{j-1}[\mathbb{E},R_{\mathbb{E}}]R_{\mathbb{E}}^{i-j})

where each summand is zero by the Bianchi identity. Hence,
\operatorname{Str}(R_{\mathbb{E}}^{i})
is closed.
∎

Let
f(z)
be a convergent formal power series in
z
, then
f(R_{\mathbb{E}})
is an element of
A^{even}(M,\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
, defined by

f(R_{\mathbb{E}})=\sum\frac{f^{(k)}(0)}{k!}(\mathbb{E}^{2})^{k}

Applying supertrace map to
f(R_{\mathbb{E}})
we get an element in
\mathsf{A}
which is a combination of even elements. We will call this element the
\mathsf{A}
-characteristic form
, or simply characteristic form if there are no confusions, of
\mathbb{E}
corresponding to
f(z)
.

Proposition 18.3
.

Given a perfect
A^{0}
-module with
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection
(E,\mathbb{E})
over a dga
\mathsf{A}
. Then

(1)

The characteristic form
\operatorname{Str}(f(R_{\mathbb{E}}))
is a closed element of even degree.

(2)

(Transgression formaula) If
\mathbb{E}_{t}
is a smooth 1-parameter family of
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection on
E
, then

(18.1)

\frac{d}{dt}\operatorname{Str}(f(R_{\mathbb{E}_{t}}))=d\operatorname{Str}\Big(\frac{d\mathbb{E}_{t}}{dt}f^{\prime}(R_{\mathbb{E}_{t}})\Big)

(3)

The cohomology class of
\operatorname{Str}(f(R_{\mathbb{E}}))
in
H^{\bullet}(\mathsf{A})
is independent of the choice of
\mathbb{E}
.

Proof.

We need the following lemma

Lemma 18.4
.

For any
\alpha\in A(M,\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
, we have
d_{\mathsf{A}}(\operatorname{Str}\alpha)=\operatorname{Str}([\mathbb{E},\alpha])
.

Proof.

Locally, we can write
\mathbb{E}=d+\omega
, so
\operatorname{Str}([\mathbb{E},\alpha])=\operatorname{Str}([d,\alpha])+\operatorname{Str}([\omega,\alpha])
. The second term vanishes by the definition of
\operatorname{Str}
, and the first terms equals
\operatorname{Str}(d\alpha)
.
∎

By this lemma, we have

d\operatorname{Str}(f(R_{\mathbb{E}}))=\operatorname{Str}([\mathbb{E},f(\mathbb{E}^{2})])=0.

Hence,
\operatorname{Str}(f(R_{\mathbb{E}}))
is closed. The degree of it is clearly even.
We show prove (2) and (3) together.
Let
\mathbb{E}_{1}
and
\mathbb{E}_{2}
be two
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection over
\mathsf{A}
on
E^{\bullet}
.
\mathbb{E}_{1}-\mathbb{E}_{2}
is
\mathsf{A}
-linear hence we can write
\mathbb{E}_{1}-\mathbb{E}_{2}=\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
for some
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\in A(M,\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))_{1}
. Set
\mathbb{E}_{t}=\mathbb{E}_{2}+t\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
. Note that
\mathbb{E}_{t}
is a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection for any
t\in[0,1]
.
Let
\mathbb{E}_{t}
be a smooth 1-parameter family of
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connections on
E
. The curvature of
\mathbb{E}_{t}
is

R_{\mathbb{E}_{t}}=(\mathbb{E}_{2}+t\mathbb{E}^{\prime})^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{2}^{2}+t[\mathbb{E}_{2},\mathbb{E}^{\prime}]+\frac{1}{2}t^{2}[\mathbb{E}^{\prime},\mathbb{E}^{\prime}]

The deformation of curvature is

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}R_{\mathbb{E}_{t}}=[\mathbb{E}_{2},\mathbb{E}^{\prime}]+t[\mathbb{E}^{\prime},\mathbb{E}^{\prime}]=[\mathbb{E}_{t},\mathbb{E}^{\prime}]

We will prove a more general lemma first.

Lemma 18.5
.

Let
\alpha_{t}\in A^{even}(M,\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
be a smooth family of forms of even total degree, then

\frac{d}{dt}\operatorname{Str}(f(\alpha_{t}))=\operatorname{Str}(\frac{d}{dt}(\alpha_{t})f^{\prime}(\alpha_{t}))

Proof.

It suffices to consider
f
being monomials. Consider
f(z)=z^{n}
. Then

\frac{d}{dt}\operatorname{Str}(\alpha_{t}^{n})=\operatorname{Str}\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{t}^{i}\Big(\frac{d}{dt}\alpha_{t}\Big)\alpha_{t}^{n-i-1}\Big)=n\operatorname{Str}\Big(\Big(\frac{d}{dt}\alpha_{t}\Big)\alpha_{t}^{n-1}\Big)

∎

Apply this lemma to
\alpha_{t}=\mathbb{E}_{t}^{2}
,
