\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\operatorname{Str}(f(\mathbb{E}_{t}^{2}))=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Str}\bigg(\frac{d\mathbb{E}_{t}^{2}}{dt}f^{\prime}(\mathbb{E}_{t}^{2})\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Str}\bigg(\Big[\mathbb{E}_{t},\Big(\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{t}^{2}\Big)f^{\prime}(\mathbb{E}_{t}^{2})\Big]\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d\operatorname{Str}\bigg(\frac{d\mathbb{E}_{t}}{dt}f^{\prime}(\mathbb{E}_{t}^{2})\bigg)

This proves (2).

Now we integrate the transgression formula with respect to
t
, then we get

(18.2)

\operatorname{Str}(f(R_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}))-\operatorname{Str}(f(R_{\mathbb{E}_{2}}))=d\int_{0}^{1}\operatorname{Str}\big(\mathbb{E}^{\prime}f^{\prime}(\mathbb{E}_{t}^{2})\big)dt

Note that
\frac{d\mathbb{E}_{t}}{dt}=\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
. Hence, the cohomology class of
\operatorname{Str}(f(R_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}))
and
\operatorname{Str}(f(R_{\mathbb{E}_{0}}))
in
H^{\bullet}(\mathsf{A})
are the same.

∎

Definition 18.6
.

Given a dg-
A^{0}
module over a dga
A^{0}
with a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection
\mathbb{E}

(E,\mathbb{E})
, we define the
\mathsf{A}
-Pontryagin algebra of
E

\operatorname{Pont}^{\bullet}_{\mathsf{A}}\subset H^{\bullet}(\mathsf{A})

to be the subalgebra generated by

\sigma^{i}_{\mathsf{A}}(E)=[\operatorname{Str}(R_{\mathbb{E}}^{i})]\in H^{2i}(\mathsf{A})

and we call
\sigma^{i}_{\mathsf{A}}(E)
the
\mathsf{A}
-Pontryagin character of
E
.

18.2.
L_{\infty}
-pairs over a dga

In this section, we will define
L_{\infty}
-pairs over a dga. As a special case, given a regular foliation
F
, then
(T_{M},{\mathcal{F}})
is an
L_{\infty}
-pair over
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(M)
.

Definition 18.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a
L_{\infty}
-algebroid over a dga
\mathsf{A}
, and
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
a subalgebroid. Note the brackets
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i}
of
\mathfrak{h}
is the restriction of the brackets
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i}
. We call
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
an
L_{\infty}
-pair
.

Note that the inclusion map
\iota:\mathfrak{h}\to\mathfrak{g}
gives
\iota^{\vee}:\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\to\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}
, consequently we have a surjective morphism of dga
\iota^{\vee}:\operatorname{Sym}\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}[-1]\to\operatorname{Sym}\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}[-1]

Example 18.8
.

For a regular foliation
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
,
(T_{M},{\mathcal{F}})
is an
L_{\infty}
-pair
.

Example 18.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebroid and
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
a subalgebroid, then
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
is an
L_{\infty}
-pair
, which is called a
Lie pair
. As a special case, if
\mathfrak{g}
is a Lie algebra and
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
a Lie subalgebra, then

(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
is an
L_{\infty}
-pair
over a point.

Now, for a Lie pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
, we denote the quotient
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}
by
N
.

Lemma 18.10
.

There is an
\infty
-representation over
\mathfrak{h}
which gives
N
an
\mathfrak{h}
-module structure.

Proof.

There is an exact sequence of
dg
-
\mathsf{A}
-modules

0\longrightarrow\mathfrak{h}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathfrak{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}N\to 0

The
\mathfrak{h}
-module structure

[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n-1},y]:\operatorname{Sym}^{n-1}\mathfrak{g}\otimes N\to N,n\geq 1

is given by

[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n-1},y]=p\circ l_{k}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n-1},y^{\prime})

for any
y^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g}
such that
p(y^{\prime})=y
. These brackets are well-defined, since
\mathfrak{h}
is a subalgebroid. By construction,
[\cdots]
is an
\infty
-representation of
\mathfrak{h}
on
N
.
∎

Lemma 18.11
.

N^{\vee}
is also an
\mathfrak{h}
-module.

Remark 18.12
.

Note that
N^{\vee}=(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h})^{\vee}\simeq\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}=\ker(\iota^{\vee}:\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\to\mathfrak{h}^{\vee})
. We denote the
\mathfrak{h}
-module structure of
N^{\vee}
by
(\mathfrak{h}^{\vee},\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}})
, where
\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}}=d_{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{h})}+D^{\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}}
.

Let
d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
and
d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{\mathfrak{h}}
be the algebraic de Rham operator on
{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{g})
and
{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{h})
respectively.
Define an operator
J:{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{g})\to A(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
by
J=(\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1\circ d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, i.e. the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{g})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathsf{dR}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1}

Hence
(1\otimes\iota^{\vee})\circ J=\iota^{\vee}\circ d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathsf{dR}}=d_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathsf{dR}}\circ\iota^{\vee}
.
It is obvious that
J
is a derivation on the
{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})
-bimodule
{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
, i.e. for all
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})
,

J(\omega\odot\omega^{\prime})=\iota^{\vee}(\omega)\odot J(\omega^{\prime})+J(\omega)\odot\iota^{\vee}(\omega^{\prime})=\iota^{\vee}(\omega)\odot J(\omega^{\prime})+(-1)^{|\omega||\omega^{\prime}|}\iota^{\vee}(\omega^{\prime})\odot J(\omega)
.

Now we get a map

(18.3)

J\otimes 1:{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g},\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))\to{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))

by setting

(18.4)

(J\otimes 1)(\phi\circ\psi)=(\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(\phi)\circ(J\otimes 1)(\psi)+(J\otimes 1)(\phi)\circ(\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(\psi)

for all
\phi,\psi\in{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g},\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
.

Lemma 18.13
.

Let
\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}}=d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}+D^{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}}
be the
\mathfrak{h}
-module structure on
N^{\vee}\simeq\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}
, then for
\omega\in\ker(\iota^{\vee})
, we have
J(\omega)\in{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp})
and

(18.5)

\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}}(J(\omega))=J(d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})}(\omega))

Proof.

First, we will show that

D^{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}}(\xi)=J(d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})}\xi)

for all
\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}
.
For
k=2

For
k>=3
.

\displaystyle<D^{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}}(\xi),p(l)>(a_{1}\odot\cdots\odot a_{k})=

\displaystyle(-1)^{|\xi|+1}<\xi,D^{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}}(p(l))(a_{1}\odot\cdots\odot a_{k})>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{|\xi|+|l|_{*_{k}}+k}<\xi,m_{k+1}(a_{1}\odot\cdots\odot a_{k},p(l))>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{|\xi|+|l|_{*_{k}}+k}<\xi,p\circ l_{k+1}(a_{1}\odot\cdots\odot a_{k},p(l))>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{k+1}<(\iota^{\vee}\circ d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{\mathfrak{g}}\circ l_{k+1}^{\vee})(\xi),p(l))>(a_{1}\odot\cdots\odot a_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle<(J\circ d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})}(\xi),p(l)>(a_{1}\odot\cdots\odot a_{k})

where
*_{k}
denotes
\sum_{i=1}^{k}|a_{i}|
.

Now let us prove the proposition. It suffices to consider the elements of the form
\omega\odot\xi\in{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})\odot\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}
. Applying the previous equation, we get

\displaystyle J\circ d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})}(\omega\odot\xi)=

\displaystyle J\big(d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})}(\omega)\odot\xi+(-1)^{|\omega|}\omega\odot d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})}\xi\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iota^{\vee}(d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})}(\omega)\odot\xi+J(d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})}(\omega))\odot\iota^{\vee}(\xi)+

\displaystyle(-1)^{|\omega|}\big(J(\omega)\odot\iota^{\vee}(d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})}\xi)+\iota^{\vee}(\omega)\odot J(d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})}\xi)\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})}(\iota^{\vee}(\omega))\odot\xi+(-1)^{|\omega|}\iota^{\vee}(\omega)\odot D^{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}}(\xi)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}}(J(\omega\odot\xi))

∎

18.3.
Characteristic classes of singular foliations

Let
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
be a perfect singular foliation, i.e.
{\mathcal{F}}
is a perfect module. Let
(E_{\bullet},\mathbb{E})
be a cohesive module resolves
{\mathcal{F}}
, i.e

0\to E_{-n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{n}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}E_{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}E_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{F}}\longrightarrow 0

By similar method for holomorphic singular foliation, we can construct an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid structure on
E_{\bullet}
. Let
\operatorname{CE}(E_{\bullet})=\operatorname{Sym}^{\bullet}E^{\vee}_{\bullet}[-1]
be Chevalley-Eilenberg algebra of the
L_{\infty}
-algebroid
E_{\bullet}
.

Proposition 18.14
.

Let
N{\mathcal{F}}=T_{M}/{\mathcal{F}}
be the normal sheaf of the singular foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
which is perfect, then
N{\mathcal{F}}
is also perfect, and we have a normal complex

0\to E_{-n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{n}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}E_{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}E_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{M}\longrightarrow N{\mathcal{F}}\longrightarrow 0

which resolves
N{\mathcal{F}}
and carries a Bott
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection
\mathbb{B}
.

Proof.

Directly follows from the
L_{\infty}
-pair
(T_{M},E_{\bullet})
.
∎

Corollary 18.15
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a perfect singular foliation and let
E_{\bullet}
be an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid which resolves
{\mathcal{F}}
. Then there exists an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid structure on
E_{\bullet}\oplus(E_{\bullet}[1]\to T_{M})
which is quasi-isomorphic to the tangent module
T_{M}
.

18.4.
Atiyah class for
L_{\infty}
-algebroids

In this section, we will construct the
Atiyah class
for an
L_{\infty}
-pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
. Let
(E,\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{E})
be a cohesive module over
\mathfrak{h}
. Here
\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{E}=d_{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{h})}+D^{\mathfrak{h},E}
where
D^{\mathfrak{h},E}
corresponds to the
\mathfrak{h}
-module structure on
E
which is an
{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{h})
-linear map
{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{h})\otimes E\to{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{h})\otimes E
.

Recall that
\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}=N^{\vee}
is also an
\mathfrak{h}
-module, hence
\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E)
inherits a
\infty
-representation over
\mathfrak{h}
, with the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection defined by

(18.6)

\mathbb{E}^{\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E)}_{\mathfrak{h}}=d_{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{h})}+D^{\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}}+[D^{\mathfrak{h},E},-]

Denote the cohomology of the complex
(\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E),\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E)})
by
H^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
.

By the surjectivity of the map
\iota^{\vee}
, we can lift
D^{\mathfrak{h},E}\in({\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{h})\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))_{1}
to an element
D^{\mathfrak{g},E}\in({\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))_{1}
. We get a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection
\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}=d_{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{g})}+D^{\mathfrak{g},E}
.

Note that
(E,\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E})
is not necessarily a cohesive module, i.e. the curvature
R_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}}
might not vanish.

We can easily calculate
R_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}}=d_{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{h})}\circ D^{\mathfrak{g},E}+(D^{\mathfrak{g},E})^{2}
.

We have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{h})\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\otimes E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{h})\otimes E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{h})\otimes\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}\otimes E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes 1}

which implies that

(1\otimes\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)\circ(J\otimes 1)\circ R_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}}=0

Therefore, we get an element
\alpha_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}}
of total degree 2.

Proposition 18.16
.

(1)

d_{{\mathcal{O}}(h)}\alpha_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}}=0
, hence we get a cocycle in the Chevalley-Eilenberg complex of
(\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E),\mathbb{E}^{\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E)})
.

(2)

The cohomology class
[\alpha_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}}]
in the

L_{\infty}
-algebroid cohomology
H^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
is independent of the extension
\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}
. We call
[\alpha_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}}]
the Atiyah class of the
L_{\infty}
-pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
with respect to
E
.

(3)

For the canonical
\mathfrak{h}
-module
(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h})
, there is a canonical Atiyah class

[\alpha^{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}]\in H^{2}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}))=H^{2}(\mathfrak{h},\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h})).

Proof.

First we need a lemma

Lemma 18.17
.

Let
x\in{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g},\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
satisfy
(\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(x)=0
, then

(18.7)

[D^{\mathfrak{h},E},(J\otimes 1)(x)]=(J\otimes 1)[D^{\mathfrak{g},E},x].

Proof.

It suffices to prove for
x
homogeneous. We have

\displaystyle(J\otimes 1)[D^{\mathfrak{g},E},x]=

\displaystyle(J\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{g},E}\circ x-(-1)^{|x|}x\circ D^{\mathfrak{g},E})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{g},E})\circ(J\otimes 1)(x)+(J\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{g},E})\circ(\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(x)

\displaystyle-(-1)^{|x|}\big((\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(x)\circ(J\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{g},E})+(J\otimes 1)(x)\circ(\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{g},E})\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D^{\mathfrak{h},E}\circ(J\otimes 1)(x)-(-1)^{|x|}(J\otimes 1)(x)\circ D^{\mathfrak{h},E}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[D^{\mathfrak{h},E},(J\otimes 1)(x)].

∎

Now, let us prove the proposition. By the previous commutative diagram
(\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)\circ R_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}}=R_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{E}}=0
, hence
(J\otimes 1)(R_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}})\in{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E)}(\alpha_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}})=

\displaystyle(d_{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{h})}+D^{\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}}+[D^{\mathfrak{h},E},-])((J\otimes 1)(R_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(d_{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{h})}+D^{\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}})((J\otimes 1)(R_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}}))+[D^{\mathfrak{h},E},(J\otimes 1)(R_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(J\otimes 1)(d_{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{g})}R_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}}+[D^{\mathfrak{g},E},(R_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}})])

where the last step follows from the Bianchi identity.

Next, let us look at (2). Consider another
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}=d_{{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{g})}+{D^{\mathfrak{g},E}}^{\prime}
lifts the flat
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection
\mathbb{E}^{\mathfrak{h},E}_{\mathfrak{h}}
. Let
\omega=\mathbb{E}^{\mathfrak{g},E}_{\mathfrak{g}}-\mathbb{E}^{\prime}=D^{\mathfrak{g},E}-{D^{\mathfrak{g},E}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g},\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))^{1}
, and we have
(\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(\omega)=0
and
(J\otimes 1)(\omega)\in{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
, which implies
(J\otimes 1)(\omega^{2})=0
. Now we have

\displaystyle\alpha_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}}-\alpha_{\mathbb{E}^{\prime}}=

\displaystyle(J\otimes 1)(R_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}}-R_{\mathbb{E}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(J\otimes 1)(d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})}(D^{\mathfrak{g},E})+(D^{\mathfrak{g},E})^{2}-d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})}({D^{\mathfrak{g},E}}^{\prime})-({D^{\mathfrak{g},E}}^{\prime})^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(J\otimes 1)(d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})}\omega+\omega^{2}+[{D^{\mathfrak{g},E}}^{\prime},\omega])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}+D^{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}})((J\otimes 1)(\omega)+[D^{\mathfrak{g},E},(J\otimes 1)(\omega)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}+D^{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}}+[D^{\mathfrak{g},E},-])((J\otimes 1)(\omega))=\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E)}((J\otimes 1)(\omega))

which implies that the cohomology classes of
\alpha_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}}
and
\alpha_{\mathbb{E}^{\prime}}
are the same.

Finally, (3) follows from the standard identification
\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\simeq(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h})^{\vee}
.

∎

Next, we shall construct the Atiyah classes from another way. Again, let
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
be an
L_{\infty}
-pair. On
\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}
, there exists a coadjoint
\mathfrak{h}
-module structure
\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}}=d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}+D^{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}}
which is dual to the adjoint
\infty
-representation of
\mathfrak{h}
on itself. There is a natural
\mathfrak{h}
-module structure on
\mathfrak{g}
, and similarly on
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
. We have a short exact sequence of
\mathfrak{h}
-modules

0\to\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\to\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota^{\vee}}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}\to 0

Now consider
(E,\mathbb{E})
an
\mathfrak{h}
-module, then we have

0\to\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E)\to\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E)\to 0

which induces a short exact sequence of dga’s

0\to{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))\to{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1\otimes\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1}}{{\to}}{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))\to 0

Hence we have a long exact sequence of
L_{\infty}
-algebroid cohomology

\displaystyle\cdots H^{1}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))\to H^{1}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1\otimes\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1}}{{\to}}H^{1}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta}}{{\to}}H^{2}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))\to H^{2}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1\otimes\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1}}{{\to}}H^{2}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))\cdots

Lemma 18.18
.

The element
(d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})\in{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
is a degree 1 cocycle.

Proof.

Since
d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}
is a derivation on
{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\vee})
,
d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}\otimes 1
is a derivation on
{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
. Hence,

\displaystyle(d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}\otimes 1)({(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})}^{2})=

\displaystyle(d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})\circ D^{\mathfrak{h},E}+D^{\mathfrak{h},E}\circ(d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[D^{\mathfrak{h},E},(d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}\otimes 1)D^{\mathfrak{h},E}]

It is easy to verify that

(\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}}\otimes 1)(d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})=(d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}\otimes 1)(d_{A(\mathfrak{h})}D^{\mathfrak{h},E})

Now

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E)}((d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{h},E}))

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}}+[D^{\mathfrak{h},E},-])((d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{h},E}))

\displaystyle=(d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}\otimes 1)(d_{A(\mathfrak{h})}D^{\mathfrak{h},E})+(d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}\otimes 1)({(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})}^{2})

\displaystyle=(d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}\otimes 1)(d_{A(\mathfrak{h})}D^{\mathfrak{h},E}+{(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})}^{2})=0

since
d_{A(\mathfrak{h})}D^{\mathfrak{h},E}+{(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})}^{2}=0
which is the Maurer-Cartan equation.
∎

Proposition 18.19
.

The cohomology class
\delta[(d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})]\in H^{2}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
is the same as the Atiyah class
\alpha^{E}_{\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h}}
.

Proof.

First we choose an element
\beta\in{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
of degree 1 such that
(1\otimes\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(\beta)=(d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})
. Then we get
\alpha\in{\mathcal{A}}^{2}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
by

\alpha=\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E)}(\beta)=(d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}+D^{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}}+[D^{\mathfrak{h},E},-])(\beta)

whose cohomology class is the one given by
\delta[(d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})]
. We want to show that
[\alpha]
actually agrees with the Atiyah class
[\alpha^{E}_{\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h}}]
. We will do this by extending the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection
\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{E}
given by the
\mathfrak{h}
-module structure of
E
and extend it to a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection
\mathbb{E}^{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}
over
\mathfrak{g}
, and show that the resulting Atiyah cocycle
\alpha^{E}_{\mathbb{E}^{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}}
coincides with
\alpha
.

First, we want to find an element
D^{\mathfrak{g},E}\in{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g},\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
with
(J\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{g},E})=\beta
and
(\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{g},E})=D^{\mathfrak{h},E}
. By surjectivity of
J
, we can find some
K^{\mathfrak{g},E}\in{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g},\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
such that
(J\otimes 1)(K^{\mathfrak{g},E})=\beta
. Now

\displaystyle(d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}\otimes 1)(\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(K^{\mathfrak{g},E})=

\displaystyle(1\otimes\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(J\otimes 1)(K^{\mathfrak{g},E})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1\otimes\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(\beta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})

e Now
(\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(K^{\mathfrak{g},E})-D^{\mathfrak{h},E}=\phi
for some
\phi\in\Gamma(\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
as
\ker d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}\simeq{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(M)
. Hence, we could let
D^{\mathfrak{g},E}=K^{\mathfrak{h},E}-\phi
.

Now

\displaystyle\alpha^{E}_{\mathbb{E}^{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}}=

\displaystyle(J\otimes 1)(d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})}D^{\mathfrak{g},E}+(D^{\mathfrak{g},E})^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}}\circ(J\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{g},E})+[(\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{g},E}),(J\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}+D^{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}})(J\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{g},E})+[D^{\mathfrak{h},E},(J\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h})}+D^{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}}+[D^{\mathfrak{h},E},-])(\beta)=\alpha

Hence,
[\alpha]
agrees with
[\alpha^{E}_{\mathbb{E}^{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}}]
.
∎

Let
\mathfrak{h}
be an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid and
(E,\mathbb{E})
a
\mathfrak{h}
-module, we have the canonical Abelian extension
(\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{h}\oplus E,\mathbb{E})
of
\mathfrak{h}
along
E
, which induces an
L_{\infty}
-pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
. The Atiyah class
\alpha_{\mathbb{E}}
is trivial in this case. Hence, we see that the Atiyah class measures the nontriviality of the extension of
\mathfrak{h}
to
\mathfrak{g}
.

Next, we are going to see some simple situation when does Atiyah classes vanish.

Proposition 18.20
.

Let
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
be an
L_{\infty}
-pair and
(E,\mathbb{E}=d_{{\mathcal{A}}(h)}+D^{\mathfrak{h},E})
be an
\mathfrak{h}
-module, then the Atiyah class
[\alpha_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}}]\in H^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
vanishes if any of the following equivalent condition is met:

(1)

There exists a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
over
\mathfrak{g}
on
E
extending
\mathbb{E}
such that the Atyiah cocycle
\alpha_{\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{E}}
relative to
\alpha_{\mathbb{E^{\prime}}}
vanishes.

(2)

There exists a degree 1 cocycle
\phi\in{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
such that
(1\otimes\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(\phi)=(d_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathsf{dR}}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})
.

(3)

There exists an
\mathfrak{h}
-module morphism
\{\phi_{k}:\operatorname{Sym}\mathfrak{h}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\to\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E)[1]\}_{k\geq 0}
from
\mathfrak{g}
to
\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E)[1]
extending the canonical
\mathfrak{h}
module morphism
\{\phi_{k}^{\mathfrak{h},E}\}_{k\geq 0}
from
\mathfrak{h}
to
\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E)[1]
, i.e.

(\phi_{k})\circ(1\otimes j)=\phi_{k}^{\mathfrak{h},E}:\operatorname{Sym}\mathfrak{h}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\to\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E)[1]

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2): Clearly (3) implies that the Atiyah class of
E
vanishes. Hence
\delta[(d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})]=0
. We can find a degree 1 element
\tilde{\phi}\in{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
such that
[(1\otimes\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(\tilde{\phi}]=[(d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})]
. Then we can find an element
\beta\in{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
of degree 0 such that
\mathbb{E}^{\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E)}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\beta)=(1\otimes\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(\tilde{\phi})-(d^{\mathsf{dR}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})
, and therefore a
\gamma\in{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
maps to
\mathfrak{h}
, i.e.
(1\otimes\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(\gamma)=\beta
.

Now we let
\phi=\tilde{\phi}-\beta
, by an easy calculation, we have

(1\otimes\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(\phi)=(d_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathsf{dR}}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{h},E})
.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1): Given
\phi\in{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
, we can find a
D^{\mathfrak{g},E}\in{\mathcal{A}}(L,\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E)))
such that
(J\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{g},E})=\beta
and
(\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{g},E})=D^{\mathfrak{h},E}
. Now
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}=d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})}+D^{\mathfrak{g},E}
is a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection extending
\mathbb{E}
, and the associated Atiyah cocycle

\displaystyle\alpha^{E}_{\mathbb{E}^{\prime}}=

\displaystyle(J\otimes 1)(R_{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(J\otimes 1)(d_{{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{g})}D^{\mathfrak{g},E}+(D^{\mathfrak{g},E})^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}}((J\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{g},E}))+[(\iota^{\vee}\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{g},E}),(J\otimes 1)(D^{\mathfrak{g},E})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}}(\phi)+[D^{\mathfrak{h},E},\phi]=0

(2)
\Leftrightarrow
(3): Note that
\phi\in{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))
consists of a family of map
\phi_{k}:\operatorname{Sym}\mathfrak{h}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\to\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E)[1]
.

∎

18.5.
Scalar Atiyah classes and Todd classes

Let
(E,\mathbb{E})
be an
\mathfrak{h}
-module and
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
an
L_{\infty}
-pair. We define the
scalar Atiyah classes
of the
L_{\infty}
-pair to be

c_{k}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})=\frac{1}{k!}\bigg(\frac{i}{2\pi}\bigg)^{k}\operatorname{Str}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h}})\in H^{k}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp})

Let
\operatorname{Ber}:\Gamma(\underline{\operatorname{End}}(E))\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(M)
be the
Berezinian map
(superdeterminant), then we define the
Todd class
of an
L_{\infty}
-pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
to be

\operatorname{Td}_{\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h}}=\operatorname{Ber}\Bigg(\frac{\alpha_{(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})}}{1-e^{-\alpha_{(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})}}}\Bigg)\in\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}H^{k}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\perp})

Example 18.21
.

Let
X
be a compact Kahler manifold. Consider the
L_{\infty}
-pair
(T_{{\mathbb{C}}}X,T^{0,1}_{X})
, then the natural map of sheaf cohomology
\bigoplus_{k}H^{k}(X,\Omega_{X}^{k})\to\bigoplus_{k}H^{2k}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
sends the scalar Atiyah classes
c_{k}(T_{X}^{1,0})
and the Todd class
\operatorname{Td}_{T_{X}^{1,0}}
of the
L_{\infty}
-pair
(T_{{\mathbb{C}}}X,T^{0,1}_{X})
to the
k
-th Chern characters
\operatorname{ch}_{k}(X)
and the Todd class
\operatorname{Td}_{X}
of
X
respectively.

18.6.
Infinitesimal ideal system of
L_{\infty}
-algebroids

In this section, we will define infinitesimal ideal systems associated to an
L_{\infty}
-pair, and show that there is a natural infinitesimal ideal systems associated to an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid fibration. The infinitesimal ideal system structure is actually related to the vanishing to Atiyah classes.

Definition 18.22
.

Consider an
L_{\infty}
-pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
, we define an
infinitesimal ideal system
to be a triple
({\mathcal{F}}_{M},\mathfrak{h},\mathbb{E})
such that
{\mathcal{F}}_{M}\subset T_{M}
is an involutive locally free subsheaf of the tangent sheaf
T_{M}
,
\rho(\mathfrak{h})\subset{\mathcal{F}}_{M}
, and
\mathbb{E}
is a flat
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection over
{\mathcal{F}}_{M}
on
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}
which satisfies

(1)

If
g\in\mathfrak{g}
is
\mathbb{E}
-flat, then
[g,h_{1},\cdots,h_{i-1}]\in\mathfrak{h}
for all
h_{i}\in h
and all
i
-brackets for
i\geq 2
.

(2)

If
g_{1},\cdots,g_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}
are
\mathbb{E}
-flat, then
[g_{1},\cdots,g_{i}]
is also
\mathbb{E}
-flat.

(3)

If
g\in\mathfrak{g}
is
\mathbb{E}
-flat, then
\rho(g)
is
\nabla^{F_{M}}
-flat, where

\nabla^{F_{M}}
is the Bott connection on
T_{M}/{\mathcal{F}}_{M}
.

This is a direct generalization of infinitesimal ideal systems in Lie algebroids.

Proposition 18.23
.

For any
L_{\infty}
-algebroid fibration
\phi:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{h}
over a
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
-map
f:M\to N
, there exist an infinitesimal ideal system
({\mathcal{F}},\mathfrak{h},\mathbb{E})
associated to it.

Definition 18.24
.

We define the Atiyah class of an infinitesimal ideal system
({\mathcal{F}}_{M},\mathfrak{h}.\mathbb{E})
in an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid
\mathfrak{g}
to be the Atiyah class of the flat
{\mathbb{Z}}
-connection
\mathbb{E}
.

Proposition 18.25
.

Let
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
be an
L_{\infty}
-pair on
M
. If there exists an infinitesimal ideal system
({\mathcal{F}}_{M},\mathfrak{h}.\mathbb{E})
in
\mathfrak{g}
, such that the quotient
(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h})/\mathbb{E}\to M/{\mathcal{F}}_{M}
exists and is smooth, then the Atiyah class of the infinitesimal ideal system
vanishes.

Example 18.26

(Simple foliations)
.

For example, if
{\mathcal{F}}
is a simple foliation, i.e. the leaf space of
{\mathcal{F}}
is a manifold, then the Atiyah class associated to
L_{\infty}
-pair
(TM,{\mathcal{F}})
vanishes.

Part V
Singular foliations and
L_{\infty}
-algebroids

19.
Singular foliation and their homotopy theory

19.1.
Foliations

A foliation is a partition of a manifold into immersed submanifolds.

Definition 19.1

(
[
MM03
]
)
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold. A (regular)
foliation

{\mathcal{F}}
of codimension
q
on
M
can be described in the following equivalent data:

(1)

A
foliation atlas

\{\phi_{i}:U_{i}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n-q}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{q}\}
of
M
for which the change-of-coordinates diffeomorphisms
\phi_{ij}
’s are globally of the form

\phi_{ij}(x,y)=\big(g_{ij})(x,y),h_{ij}(y)\big)

with respect to the decomposition
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}={\mathbb{R}}^{n-q}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{q}
, where
n=\dim M
. Note that each leaf is partitioned into
plaques
, which are connected components of the submanifolds
\phi^{-1}_{i}({\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\times\{y\}),y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{q}
. The plaques globally glue to
leaves
, which are immersed submanifolds of
M
. We call the first
n-q
directions in the decomposition the
leaf directions
, and the last
q
directions the
transversal directions
.

(2)

An open cover
\{U_{i}\}
of
M
with submersions
s_{i}:U_{i}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{q}
such that there are diffeomorphisms

\gamma_{ij}:s_{j}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})\to s_{i}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})

with
\gamma_{ij}\circ s_{j}|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}=s_{i}|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}
, which is necessarily unique.
\gamma_{ij}
’s satisfy the cocycle condition
\gamma_{ij}\circ\gamma_{jk}=\gamma_{ik}
, which is called the
Haefliger cocycle
representing
{\mathcal{F}}
.

(3)

An integrable sub-bundle
F
of
TM
of rank
n-q
, i.e. for any
X,Y\in\Gamma(F)
,
[X,Y]\in\Gamma(F)
. We usually denote
\Gamma(F)
by
{\mathcal{F}}
and
F
by
T{\mathcal{F}}
.

(4)

A locally trivial
differential ideal

\mathcal{J}=\oplus_{k=1}^{n}\mathcal{J}^{k}
of rank
q
in the de Rham dga
\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
.

When there is a foliation on a manifold
M
, we denote by
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
a
foliated manifold
.

The first two conditions are descriptions of
{\mathcal{F}}
by local charts, which tells us that locally a foliation is decomposed into two distinction directions: the leaf direction and the transversal direction. We denote
M/{\mathcal{F}}
the
leaf space
by quotienting equivalence relations such that
x\sim y
if
x
and
y
lie on the same leaf.

Definition 19.2
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold. We define a
complex foliation
to be an involutive sub-bundle of the complexified tangent bundle
T_{{\mathbb{C}}}M=TM\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{\mathbb{C}}
.

A complex foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
is called
real
if and
\overline{T{\mathcal{F}}}=T{\mathcal{F}}
. In this case, we can define a real foliation
T{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}=T{\mathcal{F}}\cap TM
.

Definition 19.3
.

A map between two foliated manifolds
f:(M_{1},{\mathcal{F}}_{1})\to(M_{2},{\mathcal{F}}_{2})
is called
foliated
if it preserves the foliated structure, i.e.
f
maps leaves of
(M_{1},{\mathcal{F}}_{1})
into leaves of
(M_{2},{\mathcal{F}}_{2})
.

We denote
\mathsf{Mfd}^{{\operatorname{Fol}}}
the category of foliated manifolds where the morphisms are foliated maps, and
\mathsf{Mfd}^{{\operatorname{Fol}}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
the category of complex-foliated manifolds.

Example 19.4
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold. The two most simple foliation on
M
is given by (1) foliation by whole manifold, i.e.
{\mathcal{F}}=T_{M}
, and (2) foliation by points , i.e.
{\mathcal{F}}=M\times\{0\}
. For the first case, the transversal direction is trivial, i.e. 0-dimensional. For the second case, the transversal direction is the whole manifold, which is again foliated by points. Hence, we see that the transversal direction always locally admits a foliation by points.

Example 19.5

(Product foliations)
.

Given two foliations
(M_{1},{\mathcal{F}}_{1})
and
(M_{2},{\mathcal{F}}_{2})
, we can form their product
(M_{1}\times M_{2},{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{F}}_{2})
, where
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
is given by
F_{1}\times F_{2}\subset TM_{1}\times TM_{2}=T(M_{1}\times M_{2})
.

We say
f
is
transversal
to
{\mathcal{F}}
if
f
is transverse to all leaves of
{\mathcal{F}}
in the image of
f
, i.e. for any
x\in N
,

(df)_{x}(T_{x}N)+T_{f(x)}({\mathcal{F}})=T_{f(x)}M

Example 19.6

(Pullback foliations)
.

Let
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
be a manifold with foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
. Consider a smooth map
f:N\to M
transversal to
{\mathcal{F}}
. It is not hard to show that the pull-back
f^{*}{\mathcal{F}}
is a foliation on
N
. This example will be important when we consider the ’derived’ counterpart of the foliation.

Example 19.7

(Flat bundles)
.

Let
G
be a group acting freely and properly discontinuesly on a connected manifold
\tilde{M}
and
\tilde{M}/G=M
. For example, we can take
\tilde{M}
be the universal cover of
M
and
G=\pi_{1}(M,x)
for some
x\in M
. Here we let
G
be a right action on
\tilde{M}
. Suppose
G
also acts on the left on some manifold
F
, then we can form a quotient space
E=\tilde{M}\times_{G}F
from the product space
\tilde{M}\times F
by identifying
(yg,z)\sim(y,gz)
. It is easy to show that
E
is a manifold, and we have the following commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{M}\times F\par}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{pr}}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

The projection
{\operatorname{pr}}_{1}
induces a submersion
\pi
, which gives
E
a fiber bundle structure over
M
with fiber
F
.

This construction also produces a foliation
{\mathcal{F}}({\operatorname{pr}}_{2})
on
\tilde{M}\times F
, which is given by the submersion
{\operatorname{pr}}_{2}:\tilde{M}\times F\to p
. By the construction,
{\mathcal{F}}({\operatorname{pr}}_{2})
is
G
-invariant, hence we can form a quotient foliation
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{F}}({\operatorname{pr}}_{2})/G
on
E
.

We can look into more detail about leaves of
{\mathcal{F}}
. Let
x\in F
, and
G_{x}\subset G
the isotropy group of the
G
-action at
x
, then the leaf of the foliation
F
associated to
\tilde{M}\times\{z\}
is diffeomorphic
\tilde{M}/G_{x}
.

19.2.
Singular foliation

We also often see foliated structures with singularities, i.e. the dimensions of leaves are not constant.

Definition 19.8

(
[
AC09
]
,
[
LLS20
]
)
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold. A
singular foliation

{\mathcal{F}}
on
M
is a locally finitely generated subsheaf of
{\mathcal{O}}_{M}
-modules of the tangent sheaf
T_{M}
which is involutive, i.e. closed under Lie brackets.

Equivalently, we can characterize a singular foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
as a locally finitely generated
O_{M}
-submodule of
\Gamma(TM)
. Clearly, regular foliations are singular foliations, since sub-bundles of
TM
are finitely generated. By a result of Hermann
[
Her60
]
, a singular foliation on
M
induces a partition of
M
into leaves.

A
singular sub-foliation

{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
of a singular foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
is a singular foliation such that, for all open sets
U\subset M
, we have
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}(U)\subset{\mathcal{F}}(U)
.

Definition 19.9
.

Let
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
be a singular-foliated manifold and
x\in M
. The
tangent space of the leaf
at
x\in M
is the image
F_{x}
of
{\mathcal{F}}
in
T_{x}M
. The
fiber
of
{\mathcal{F}}
at
x
is
{\mathcal{F}}_{x}={\mathcal{F}}/I_{x}{\mathcal{F}}
, where
I_{x}=\{f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{M}:f(x)=0\}
.

Let
\operatorname{ev}_{x}:{\mathcal{F}}\to T_{x}M
be the evaluation map of
{\mathcal{F}}
at
x
. Clearly
\operatorname{ev}_{x}
vanishes on
I_{x}{\mathcal{F}}
, therefore it descends to a map
\tilde{\operatorname{ev}_{x}}:{\mathcal{F}}_{x}\to F_{x}\subset T_{x}M
.
\ker{\operatorname{ev}_{x}}
is a Lie subalgebra of
{\mathcal{F}}
and
I_{x}{\mathcal{F}}
is an ideal in this Lie algebra. It follows that
\ker\tilde{\operatorname{ev}_{x}}=\ker{\operatorname{ev}_{x}}/I_{x}{\mathcal{F}}
is a Lie algebra, and we call this Lie algebra the
isotropy Lie algebra
.

Below are some basic results for fibers and tangent spaces of the leaves of singular foliations:

Proposition 19.10

(
[
AS06
]
)
.

Let
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
be a singular-foliated manifold, and
x\in M
. We have

(1)

Let
X_{1},\cdots,X_{k}\in{\mathcal{F}}
whose images in
{\mathcal{F}}_{x}
form a basis of
{\mathcal{F}}_{x}
, then there is a neighborhood
U
of
x
such that
{\mathcal{F}}_{U}
is generated by
X_{1},\cdots,X_{k}
.

(2)

\dim F_{x}
is lower semi-continuous and
\dim{\mathcal{F}}_{x}
is upper semi-continuous.

(3)

The set

U=\{x\in M:\tilde{\operatorname{ev}}_{x}:{\mathcal{F}}_{x}\to F_{x}\}\text{ is an isomorphism}

is the set of continuity of
x\mapsto\dim F_{x}
.
U
is open and dense.
F|_{U}\subset TM|_{U}
is a sub-bundle, hence
{\mathcal{F}}_{U}
is a regular foliation.

Proof.

See
[
AS06
, Proposition 1.5]
.
∎

19.3.
Holonomy and monodromy

Holonomy of a regular foliation is defined as germs of local diffeomorphisms of transversals along a path on a leaf. It turns out to be one of the most import concepts related to foliations, for example, we can construct the
holonomy groupoid
of a foliation. A related notion is the
monodromy
, which describes the leafwise homotopy classes of paths.

Let
\operatorname{Diff}_{x}(M)
denote the group of diffeomorphisms of a manifold fixing
M
.

Definition 19.11

(
[
MM03
]
)
.

Let
({\mathfrak{M}},{\mathcal{F}})
be a foliated manifold. Let
x,y
be some points on some leaf
L
of
{\mathcal{F}}
. Let
S,T
be
transversal sections
(or
transversal
) at
x
and
y
, then for any path
\alpha:x\to y
we can associate a germ of a diffeomorphism

\operatorname{Hol}^{S,T}(\alpha):(S,x)\to(T,y)

which is called the
holonomy
of
\alpha
with respect to the transversal sections
S
and
T
. For details about the construction of
\operatorname{Hol}^{S,T}(\alpha)
, see
[
MM03
, Section 2.1]
.

Two easy but import properties of the holonomy are

(1)

Homotopic paths induces the same holonomy;

(2)

Holonomy is independent of the choice of transversals by identifying the holonomy of different transversals along the constant path.

Let
x\in L
and
S
be a transversal at
x
. By the above properties, we have a group homomorphism

\operatorname{Hol}:\pi_{1}(L,x)\to\operatorname{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{q})

by the independence of the choice of
T
, we get the
holonomy homomorphism

\operatorname{Hol}

\operatorname{Hol}:\pi_{1}(L,x)\to\operatorname{Diff}_{x}(T)\simeq\operatorname{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{q})

which is defined up to conjugations in
\operatorname{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{q})
, where
q=\operatorname{codim}{\mathcal{F}}
. We call the image of
\operatorname{Hol}
the
holonomy group
of
L
at
x
, which is determined up to an inner automorphism of
\operatorname{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{q})
. As a direct consequence, we have a short exact sequence

1\to K\hookrightarrow\pi_{1}(L,x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{Hol}}}{{\to}}\operatorname{Hol}(L,x)\to 1

We will look at this sequence again when we generalize to higher holonomies. Similar to the case of homotopy, we say two path
\alpha,\beta:x\to y
lying in the same leaf
L
are in the same
holonomy class
if
\operatorname{Hol}(\alpha^{-1}\beta)=\operatorname{Id}
.

Definition 19.12

(
[
MM03
]
)
.

Taking the differential at 0 gives a homomorphism
d_{0}:\operatorname{Diff}_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{q})\to\operatorname{GL}(q,{\mathbb{R}})
. We call the composition

d\operatorname{Hol}=d_{0}\circ\operatorname{Hol}:\pi_{1}(L,x)\to\operatorname{GL}(q,{\mathbb{R}})

the
linear holonomy homomorphism
of
L
at x, and we call the image the
linear holonomy group
.

An important construction in foliations is associating various groupoids to a foliation. The most important two are
holonomy groupoids
and
monodromy groupoids
.

Definition 19.13

(
[
MM03
]
)
.

Let
({\mathfrak{M}},{\mathcal{F}})
be a foliated manifold. Define the
monodromy groupoid

\operatorname{Mon}({\mathcal{F}})
of
{\mathcal{F}}
to be a groupoid over
M
whose arrows are homotopy classes of paths along leaves of
{\mathcal{F}}
. Similarly, define the
holonomy groupoid

\operatorname{Hol}({\mathcal{F}})
of
{\mathcal{F}}
to be a groupoid over
M
whose arrows are holonomy classes of paths along leaves of
{\mathcal{F}}

Both holonomy groupoids and monodromy groupoids are Lie groupoids, hence they are powerful tools in studying the geometry and topology of foliations.

Next, we move to singular foliations.

Definition 19.14
.

Let
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
be a singular-foliated manifold. A
slice

\mathcal{T}
at
x
is an embedded submanifold
\mathcal{T}\subset M
such that
x\in\mathcal{T}
and
T_{x}\mathcal{T}\oplus F_{x}=T_{x}M
.

This is similar to the definition of local transversals in regular foliations.

Consider a path
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
from
x
to
y
which lie in a single leaf
L
of
{\mathcal{F}}
. Fixed two slices
\mathcal{T}_{x}
and
\mathcal{T}_{y}
at
x
and
y
respectively. For each time
t
, we lift
\dot{\gamma}(t)
to a vector field
X_{t}
lying in
{\mathcal{F}}
, such that the flow of the time-dependent vector field
\{X_{t}\}
maps
\mathcal{T}_{x}
to
\mathcal{T}_{y}
. However, if
{\mathcal{F}}
is not a regular foliation, the map
f\in\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{T}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{y})
will dependent on the extension. Hence, we want to modify this such that we are not affected by the choice of extensions.

First, we want to make some notations. Let
\operatorname{Aut}_{{\mathcal{F}}}(M)
be the subgroup of local diffeomorphisms of
M
preserving
{\mathcal{F}}
. Let
\exp
denote the space of time-one flows of time-dependent vector fields in
{\mathcal{F}}
. Recall we have the following exact sequence

0\to\mathfrak{g}_{x}\to{\mathcal{F}}_{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{ev}_{x}}}{{\to}}F_{x}\to 0

where
\mathfrak{g}_{x}={\mathcal{F}}(x)/I_{x}{\mathcal{F}}
is the isotropy Lie algebra. We have that both
\exp(I_{x}{\mathcal{F}})
and
\exp({\mathcal{F}}(x))
are subgroups of
\operatorname{Aut}_{{\mathcal{F}}}(M)
. Denote the restriction of
{\mathcal{F}}
to the slice
\mathcal{T}_{x}
by
{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{T}_{x}}
, i.e.
{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{T}_{x}}={\mathcal{F}}|_{{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{T}_{x}}}\cap T\mathcal{T}_{x}
. Finally, we denote
\operatorname{Germ}\operatorname{Aut}_{{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathcal{T}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{y})
the space of germs of local diffeomorphisms from
(\mathcal{T}_{x},{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{T}_{x}})
to
(\mathcal{T}_{y},{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{T}_{x}})
.

Theorem 19.15
.

The class
\Gamma(-,1):\mathcal{T}_{x}\to\mathcal{T}_{y}
in the quotient

(19.1)

\frac{\operatorname{Germ}\operatorname{Aut}_{{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathcal{T}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{y})}{\exp({\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{T}_{x}})}

is independent of the choice of extension
\Gamma
.

Proof.

See
[
AZ12
, Proposition 2.3]
.
∎

This seems to be a good candidate to define the holonomy transformation. However, as pointed out in
[
AZ12
]
,
\exp({\mathcal{F}}(x))
is too large to do linearization. Hence, we replace
{\mathcal{F}}(x)
by
I_{x}{\mathcal{F}}
and get the following definition:

Definition 19.16

(
[
AZ12
]
)
.

Let
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
be a singular-foliated manifold, and
x,y\in M
lying in some leaf
L
. We fix slices
\mathcal{T}_{x}
and
\mathcal{T}_{y}
at
x
and
y
respectively. We define a
holonomy transformation
from
x
to
y
to be an element of

(19.2)

\frac{\operatorname{Germ}\operatorname{Aut}_{{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathcal{T}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{y})}{\exp(I_{x}{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{T}_{x}})}

Lemma 19.17
.

Let
x
be a point on a regular leaf, then
\exp(I_{x}{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{T}_{x}})
is trivial.

Proof.

In this case,
\mathcal{T}_{x}
is equipped with a trivial singular foliation, i.e. foliation by points. Hence, the flow is trivial.
∎

Therefore, we see that for regular foliation, the holonomy transformations reduce to the ordinary holonomy transformations
\operatorname{Germ}\operatorname{Diff}(\mathcal{T}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{y})
. It is easy to see that we can form a topological groupoid
\operatorname{HolTrans}({\mathcal{F}})
over
M
with morphisms being

\bigcup_{x,y}\frac{\operatorname{Germ}\operatorname{Aut}_{{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathcal{T}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{y})}{\exp(I_{x}{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{T}_{x}})}

There is a natural map from the holonomy groupoid in the sense of Androulidakis and Skandalis in
[
AS06
]
, which justifies the correctness of the definition of holonomy transformations. Let’s first review basics about holonomy groupoids defined by Androulidakis and Skandalis.

Definition 19.18

(
[
AS06
]
)
.

Let
(M,{\mathcal{F}}_{M}),(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})
be two singular foliated manifolds. A
bisubmersion
is a manifold
P
with two surjective submersions
s:P\to M
and
t:P\to N
such that

s^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}_{M}=t^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}_{N}=\Gamma(\ker(s)_{*})+\Gamma(\ker(t)_{*})

We have the following diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(P,{\mathcal{F}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(M,{\mathcal{F}}_{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

where
{\mathcal{F}}=s^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}_{M}=t^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}_{N}
is the pullback singular foliation on
P
.

Definition 19.19
.

A
morphism between bisubmersions

(U,s_{U},t_{U}),(V,s_{V},t_{V})
is a smooth map
f:U\to V
such that for all
u\in U
, we have
s_{V}(f(u))=s_{U}(u)
and

t_{V}(f(u))=t_{U}(u)
.

A
local morphism between bisubmersions

(U,s_{U},t_{U}),(V,s_{V},t_{V})
is a smooth map
f:U^{\prime}\to V
for some
U^{\prime}\subset U
such that for all
u\in U^{\prime}
, we have
s_{V}(f(u))=s_{U}(u)
and

t_{V}(f(u))=t_{U}(u)
.

Definition 19.20
.

Let
\mathcal{U}=(U_{i},s_{i},t_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of bisubmersions.

(1)

A bisubmersion
(U,s,t)
is said to be
adapted to
\mathcal{U}
at
u\in U
if there exists an open subset
U^{\prime}\subset U
containing
u
and a morphism of bisubmersion
U^{\prime}\to U
.

(2)

A bisubmersion
(U,s,t)
is said to be
adapted to
\mathcal{U}
if
(U,s,t)
is adapted to
\mathcal{U}
at
u\in U
for all
u\in U
.

(3)

We call the family
\mathcal{U}=(U_{i},s_{i},t_{i})_{i\in I}
an
atlas
if

(a)

\cup_{i\in I}s_{i}(U_{i})=M
.

(b)

Any elements of
\mathcal{U}
is still adapted to
\mathcal{U}
under taking inverses and compositions.

Recall that an atlas of a manifold allows us to reconstruct the manifold, similarly an atlas of a singular foliation
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
allows us to reconstruct the foliated structure by a groupoid over
M
, and this is the first step in constructing the holonomy groupoid.

Theorem 19.21

(
[
AS06
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}=(U_{i},s_{i},t_{i})_{i\in I}
be an atlas of a singular foliated manifold
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
.

(1)

Let
G=\coprod_{i\in I}U_{i}/\sim
where
\sim
is the equivalence relation generated by local morphisms, i.e.
u\in U_{i}
is equivalent to
v\in U_{j}
if there exists a local morphism from
U_{i}\to U_{j}
which takes
u
to
v
. There are maps
s,t:G\to M
such that
s\circ q_{i}=s_{i}
and
t\circ q_{i}=t_{i}
, where
Q=(q_{i})_{i\in I}:\coprod_{i\in I}\to G
is the quotient map.

(2)

For any
(U,s_{U},t_{U})
adapted to
\mathcal{U}
, there exists a map
q_{U}:U\to G
such that for every local morphism
f:U^{\prime}\subset U\to U_{i}
and every
u\in U^{\prime}
, we have
q_{U}(u)=q_{i}(f(u))
.

(3)

There exists a (topological) groupoid structure on
G
over
M
with source and target maps
s
and
t
defined before, and
q_{i}(u)q_{j}(v)=q_{U_{i}\circ U_{j}}(u,v)
.

Hence, given any atlas, we can construct a groupoid which encodes information about the singular foliation. Apparently, we can simply take all possible bisubmersions to be an atlas, which is called the
full holonomy atlas
. However, this atlas is obviously too big which will make the arrow space of the groupoid to be incredibly large and nasty. The second choice is to take all leaf-preserving bisubmersions, i.e. bisubmersions
(U,s,t)
that
s(u)
and
t(u)
lying in the same leaf for all
u\in U
. This is called the
leaf-preserving atlas
, which is much smaller than the full holonomy atlas.
[
AS06
]
constructs an atlas
\mathcal{W}
which is as minimal as possible.

Proposition 19.22

(
[
AS06
]
)
.

Let
x\in M
and
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\in{\mathcal{F}}
be vector fields whose images at
x
form a basis of
{\mathcal{F}}_{x}
(the fiber of
{\mathcal{F}}
at
x
). For
y=(y_{1},\cdots,y_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, set
\phi_{y}=\exp(\sum y_{i}X_{i})\in\exp{\mathcal{F}}
, i.e. the image of
y
under the time-1 flow of the vector field
\sum y_{i}X_{i}
. Let
W_{0}={\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times M,s_{0}(y,x)=x,s_{0}(y,x)=\phi_{y}(x)
. Then

•

There exists a neighborhood
W
of
(0,x)
in
W_{0}
such that
(W,s|_{W},t|_{W})
is a bisubmersion.

•

For any bisubmersions
(V,s_{V},t_{v})
carries the identity of
M
at some
v\in V
, then there exists a local morphism from
(V,s_{V},t_{v})
to some
(W,s_{W},t_{W})\in\mathcal{W}
at
v
which sends
v
to
(0,x)
.

Definition 19.23

(
[
AS06
]
)
.

We define the
path holonomy atlas
of a singular foliated manifold
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
to be the maximal atlas generated by a cover of
M
by
s
-connected bisubmersions of the form in Proposition
19.22
.

The corresponding groupoid of the path holonomy atlas is the smallest due to (2) in Proposition
19.22
, which implies that this atlas is adapted to any other atlas.

Definition 19.24
.

Let
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
be a singular-foliated manifold. We define the
holonomy groupoid
of
{\mathcal{F}}
to be the (topological) groupoid associated to the path holonomy atlas of
{\mathcal{F}}
. We denote the holonomy groupoid of
{\mathcal{F}}
by
\operatorname{Hol}({\mathcal{F}})
or
\operatorname{Hol}^{AS}({\mathcal{F}})
.

The topology of holonomy groupoids in this definition is usually pretty bad. For example,
[
AS06
, Example 3.7]
consider the singular foliation on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
generated by the action of
\operatorname{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
, whose holonomy groupoid has a highly non-Hausdorff arrow space, for example, for any
x
outside the origin, the sequence
\{(x/n,x/n)\}
will converge to all
(g,0)
for stabilizers
g
of
x
. Therefore, in general we don’t expect holonomy groupoids to be Lie groupoids. However, we do have the following local smoothness results.

Theorem 19.25

(
[
Deb13
]
)
.

Let
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
be a singular-foliated manifold. The
s
-fibers of
\operatorname{Hol}({\mathcal{F}})
are smooth manifolds.

Corollary 19.26

(
[
AZ11
]
)
.

The transitive groupoid
\operatorname{Hol}_{L}({\mathcal{F}})
is smooth and integrates the Lie algebroid
A_{L}=\cup_{x\in L}{\mathcal{F}}_{x}
, where
\operatorname{Hol}_{L}({\mathcal{F}})=\operatorname{Hol}({\mathcal{F}})|_{s^{-1}L}=\operatorname{Hol}({\mathcal{F}})|_{t^{-1}L}

Hence a natural question is can we find a higher categorical geometric object such that the 1-truncation equals the holonomy groupoid?

We will answer this question later, and let’s return to the holonomy transformation first.

Theorem 19.27

(
[
AZ12
]
)
.

Let
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
be a singular-foliated manifold, and
x,y\in M
lying in some leaf
L
. We fix slices
\mathcal{T}_{x}
and
\mathcal{T}_{y}
at
x
and
y
respectively. There is a natural injective map

\Phi^{y}_{x}:\operatorname{Hol}^{AS}({\mathcal{F}})^{y}_{x}\to\operatorname{HolTrans}({\mathcal{F}})^{y}_{x}=\frac{\operatorname{Germ}\operatorname{Aut}_{{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathcal{T}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{y})}{\exp(I_{x}{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{T}_{x}})}

where
\operatorname{Hol}^{AS}({\mathcal{F}})
denotes the holonomy groupoid in the sense of Androulidakis and Skandalis
[
AS06
]
. Moreover,
\Phi^{y}_{x}
assembles to a global groupoid morphism

\Phi:\operatorname{Hol}({\mathcal{F}})\to\operatorname{HolTrans}({\mathcal{F}})

Definition 19.28
.

Let
f:\mathcal{T}_{x}\to\mathcal{T}_{y}
be a holonomy transformation. Suppose
f
is also an embedding, then we call
f
a
holonomy embedding
.

19.4.
Hausdorff Morita equivalences

There are various notions of two (singular) foliations to be equivalent. Garmendia and Zambon
[
GZ19
]
proposed a notion called
Hausdorff Morita equivalence
of singular foliations, which is constructed to be compatible to Androulidakis and Skandalis’s construction of holonomy groupoids in
[
AS06
]
.

Definition 19.29
.

[
AS06
]

Definition 19.30

(
[
GZ19
]
)
.

Let
(M,{\mathcal{F}}_{M}),(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})
be two singular foliated manifolds. We say
(M,{\mathcal{F}}_{M})
and
(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})
are
Hausdorff Morita equivalent
if there exists a manifold
P
and two surjective submersions with connected fibers
\pi_{M}:P\to M
and
\pi_{N}:P\to N
such that
\pi_{M}^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}_{M}=\pi_{N}^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}_{N}
. We have the following diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(P,{\mathcal{F}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(M,{\mathcal{F}}_{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{N}}

where
{\mathcal{F}}=\pi_{M}^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}_{M}=\pi_{N}^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}_{N}
is the pullback singular foliation on
P
.

This definition is almost the same as the bisubmersion except that we do not require
\pi_{M}^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}_{M}=\pi_{N}^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}_{N}=\Gamma(\ker(\pi_{M})_{*})+\Gamma(\ker(\pi_{N})_{*})
. Hence, Hausdorff Morita equivalences are weaker notions than bisubmersions.

The following theorem gives basic properties of Hausdorff Morita equivalences.

Theorem 19.31
.

[
GZ19
]

Let two singular foliated manifolds
(M,{\mathcal{F}}_{M}),(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})
be Hausdorff Morita equivalent. We have

(1)

There is a homeomorphism between the leaf spaces of
(M,{\mathcal{F}}_{M})
and the leaf space of
(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})
, which maps the leaf though some
x\in M
to the leaf of
{\mathcal{F}}_{N}
containing
\pi_{N}(\pi^{-1}(x))
, and preserves the codimension of leaves and the property of being an embedded leaf.

(2)

Consider
x\in M,y\in N
. Pick slices
S_{x}
at
x
and
S_{y}
at
y
, then the singular foliated manifolds
(S_{x},\iota_{S_{x}}^{-1})
and
(S_{y},\iota_{S_{y}}^{-1})
are diffeomorphic.

(3)

Consider
x\in M,y\in N
. The isotropy Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{{\mathcal{F}}_{M}}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{y}^{{\mathcal{F}}_{N}}
are isomorphic.

The next theorem justifies Hausdorff Morita equivalence is the correct notion which preserves holonomy groupoids.

Theorem 19.32

(
[
GZ19
]
)
.

If two singular foliated manifolds
(M,{\mathcal{F}}_{M}),(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})
are Hausdorff Morita equivalent, then their holonomy groupoids (in the sense of Androulidakis and Skandalis in
[
AS06
]
) are Morita equivalent as open topological groupoids.

Next, let’s look at the behavior of Hausdorff Morita equivalences under pullbacks.

Proposition 19.33
.

Pullbacks of Hausdorff Morita equivalences are Hausdorff Morita equivalences.

Proof.

Let
f:(M,{\mathcal{F}}_{M})\to(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})
,
g:(L,{\mathcal{F}}_{L})\to(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})
be morphisms in
\mathsf{Mfd}^{{\operatorname{SFol}}}
. Suppose the pullback singular foliated manifold
(M\times_{N}L,{\mathcal{F}}_{M}\times_{{\mathcal{F}}_{N}}{\mathcal{F}}_{L})
exists and
f
is a Hausdorff Morita equivalence, then the induces map
f^{\prime}:(M\times_{N}L,{\mathcal{F}}_{M}\times_{{\mathcal{F}}_{N}}{\mathcal{F}}_{L})\to(L,{\mathcal{F}}_{L})
is also a Hausdorff Morita equivalence.
∎

Proof.

Let
(M,{\mathcal{F}}_{M})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{M}}}{{\leftarrow}}(P,{\mathcal{F}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{N}}}{{\rightarrow}}(N,{\mathcal{F}})
be a morphism representing the Hausdorff Morita equivalence. We have the following commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(P^{\prime},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(P,{\mathcal{F}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(M\times_{N}L,{\mathcal{F}}_{M}\times_{{\mathcal{F}}_{N}}{\mathcal{F}}_{L})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(M,{\mathcal{F}}_{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(L,{\mathcal{F}}_{L})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(N,{\mathcal{F}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{M}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{N}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Since
\pi_{M},\pi_{N}
are submersions, we see their pullbacks exist, and the left triangle in the diagram is actually a composition of pullbacks, which implies that all three squares are pullbacks. In particular.
(\pi_{M}^{\prime})^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{M}\times_{{\mathcal{F}}_{N}}{\mathcal{F}}_{L})=(\pi_{N}^{\prime})^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{L})
follows from the composition pullback. Clearly the fiber of
\pi_{M}^{\prime}
and
\pi_{N}^{\prime}
since
\pi_{M}
and
\pi_{N}
’s are.
∎

19.5.
Homotopy theory of singular foliations

Definition 19.34
.

Let
f:(M,{\mathcal{F}}_{M})\to(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})
,
g:(L,{\mathcal{F}}_{L})\to(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})
be morphisms in
\mathsf{Mfd}^{{\operatorname{SFol}}}
. We say
f
is
foliated transverse
to
g
if the natural map
(d_{f}\times d_{g})({\mathcal{F}}_{M}\times{\mathcal{F}}_{L})\to f^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{N}\times_{N}g^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{N}
is surjective. Here we use
f^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{N}\times_{N}g^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{N}
to denote the pullback of
{\mathcal{F}}_{N}
on
M\times_{N}L
.

Proposition 19.35
.

Let
f:(M,{\mathcal{F}}_{M})\to(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})
,
g:(L,{\mathcal{F}}_{L})\to(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})
be morphisms in
\mathsf{Mfd}^{{\operatorname{SFol}}}
. Suppose the
f
is foliated transverse to
g
. If the pullback
(M\times_{N}L)
exists, then the pullback
({\mathcal{F}}_{M}\times_{{\mathcal{F}}_{N}}{\mathcal{F}}_{L})
is a singular foliation on
(M\times_{N}L)
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M\times_{N}L,{\mathcal{F}}_{M}\times_{{\mathcal{F}}_{N}}{\mathcal{F}}_{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M,{\mathcal{F}}_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(L,{\mathcal{F}}_{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Proof.

First let’s look at the involutivity. Denote
{\mathcal{F}}_{M}\times_{{\mathcal{F}}_{N}}{\mathcal{F}}_{L}
by
{\mathcal{F}}
. Obviously,
g^{\prime}
is foliated, i.e.
d_{g^{\prime}}({\mathcal{F}})\subset{\mathcal{F}}_{M}
. Let
X,X^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{M}
. Write
d_{g^{\prime}}(X)=\sum f_{i}Y_{i}\circ g^{\prime}
and
d_{g^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})=\sum f_{i}^{\prime}Y_{i}^{\prime}\circ g^{\prime}
, then

d_{g^{\prime}}([X,X^{\prime}])=\sum f_{i}f_{j}^{\prime}[Y_{i},Y_{j}^{\prime}]\circ g^{\prime}+\sum X(f_{j}^{\prime})Y_{j}^{\prime}\circ g^{\prime}-\sum X^{\prime}(f_{i})Y_{i}\circ g^{\prime}

hence we see the pullback is closed under brackets.

Next, we want to show
{\mathcal{F}}
is locally finitely generated. By restricting to sufficient small open subsets of
M
,
L
and
N
, we can assume
{\mathcal{F}}_{M}
,
{\mathcal{F}}_{L}
, and
{\mathcal{F}}_{N}
are finitely generated, and tangent bundles of
M
,
L
, and
N
are trivial.

{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{F}}_{M}\times_{{\mathcal{F}}_{N}}{\mathcal{F}}_{L}={\mathcal{F}}_{M}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.04651pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[14.20764pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{f^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}}}}\times^{{\kern-64.50856pt\mathchoice{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.04651pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[14.20764pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.071pt\mathchoice{\makebox[25.11679pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.11679pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.63306pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[13.91231pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-25.22862pt{f^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}\kern 8.071pt{g^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.04651pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[14.20764pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{f^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}}}}\times^{{\kern-64.50856pt\mathchoice{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.04651pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[14.20764pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.071pt\mathchoice{\makebox[25.11679pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.11679pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.63306pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[13.91231pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-25.22862pt{f^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}\kern 8.071pt{g^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.04651pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[14.20764pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{f^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}}}}\times^{{\kern-47.11928pt\mathchoice{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.04651pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[14.20764pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.6321pt\mathchoice{\makebox[25.11679pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.11679pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.63306pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[13.91231pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-19.95085pt{f^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}\kern 5.6321pt{g^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.04651pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[14.20764pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{f^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}}}}\times^{{\kern-46.0304pt\mathchoice{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.04651pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[14.20764pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.54321pt\mathchoice{\makebox[25.11679pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.11679pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.63306pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[13.91231pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-18.86197pt{f^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}\kern 4.54321pt{g^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}}}}{\mathcal{F}}_{L}

Note that

\displaystyle f^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})=

\displaystyle f^{*}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})\times_{\Gamma(M,f^{*}(TN))}\Gamma(M,TM)

\displaystyle g^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})=

\displaystyle g^{*}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})\times_{\Gamma(L,g^{*}(TN))}\Gamma(L,TL)

By foliated transversality, we see there exists a section

s:{\mathcal{F}}_{N}\to f^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})\times g^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})\subset{\mathcal{F}}_{M}\times{\mathcal{F}}_{L}

It follows that
{\mathcal{F}}_{M}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.04651pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[14.20764pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{f^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}}}}\times^{{\kern-64.50856pt\mathchoice{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.04651pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[14.20764pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.071pt\mathchoice{\makebox[25.11679pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.11679pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.63306pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[13.91231pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-25.22862pt{f^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}\kern 8.071pt{g^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.04651pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[14.20764pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{f^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}}}}\times^{{\kern-64.50856pt\mathchoice{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.04651pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[14.20764pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.071pt\mathchoice{\makebox[25.11679pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.11679pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.63306pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[13.91231pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-25.22862pt{f^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}\kern 8.071pt{g^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.04651pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[14.20764pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{f^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}}}}\times^{{\kern-47.11928pt\mathchoice{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.04651pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[14.20764pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.6321pt\mathchoice{\makebox[25.11679pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.11679pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.63306pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[13.91231pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-19.95085pt{f^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}\kern 5.6321pt{g^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.04651pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[14.20764pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{f^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}}}}\times^{{\kern-46.0304pt\mathchoice{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.64842pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.04651pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[14.20764pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.54321pt\mathchoice{\makebox[25.11679pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.11679pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.63306pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[13.91231pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-18.86197pt{f^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}\kern 4.54321pt{g^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{N})}}}}{\mathcal{F}}_{L}
is finitely generated.
∎

Similarly, we can define foliated submersions.

Definition 19.36
.

Let
f:(M,{\mathcal{F}}_{M})\to(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})
be morphisms in
\mathsf{Mfd}^{{\operatorname{SFol}}}
. We say
f
is a
foliated submersion
if the natural map
d_{f}{\mathcal{F}}_{M}\to f^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{N}
is surjective.

By this definition, a foliated submersion is then foliated transversal to any foliated maps. Hence, a direct corollary is:

Corollary 19.37
.

Let
f:(M,{\mathcal{F}}_{M})\to(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})
,
g:(L,{\mathcal{F}}_{L})\to(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})
be morphisms in
\mathsf{Mfd}^{{\operatorname{SFol}}}
. Suppose
f
is a surjective foliated submersion. If the pullback
(M\times_{N}L)
exists, then the pullback
({\mathcal{F}}_{M}\times_{{\mathcal{F}}_{N}}{\mathcal{F}}_{L})
is a singular foliation on
(M\times_{N}L)
. Moreover, the induced map
f^{\prime}:(M\times_{N}L,{\mathcal{F}}_{M}\times_{{\mathcal{F}}_{N}}{\mathcal{F}}_{L})\to(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})
is a surjective foliated submersion.

Next, we will construct a finite dimensional model for the path object of singular foliations. Recall that, in general,
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
path spaces for finite dimensional manifolds are infinite dimensional, even if we restrict to
C^{1}
paths. One way to remedy this is to consider only those ’short paths’. We will follow the construction in
[
BLX21
, Section 2.1]
.

First, we can some connection
\nabla
on
M
. Let
\exp^{\nabla}
denote the exponential map with respect to
\nabla
. Let
I=(a,b)\supset[0,1]
.

Proposition 19.38

(
[
BLX21
]
)
.

There exists a manifold
P_{g}M
, which is called the manifold of short geodesic paths in
M
, which parametrizes a family of geodesic paths, such that
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TM}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M\times M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{P_{g}M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces const}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma(0)\times\gamma^{\prime}(0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma(0)\times\gamma(1)}

where the two lower diagonal maps are open embeddings.

Proof.

Consider
U\subset TM
an open neighborhood of the zero section, where the
\exp^{\nabla}(tv)
is defined for any
t\in I,v\in U
.
U
parametrize a family of geodesic paths with domain
I
:

\displaystyle U\times I

\displaystyle\to M

\displaystyle(x,v,t)\mapsto\gamma_{x,v}(t)

\displaystyle=\exp^{\nabla}_{x}(tv)

We can restrict to a smaller open neighborhood
V\subset V
such that the both maps
U\to TM
and
V\to M\times M
are open embeddings. Now take
P_{g}M=V
.
∎

Hence,
P_{g}M
is diffeomorphic to an open neighborhood of the zero section of
TM
. The evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}_{0}:P_{g}M\to M
is just the restriction of the projection
TM\to M
, and
\operatorname{ev}_{1}:P_{g}M\to M
is given by the exponential map

v_{x}\mapsto\exp^{\nabla}_{x}v_{x}

Lemma 19.39
.

Let
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
be a singular-foliated manifold. Then there exists a associated foliation
{\mathcal{F}}_{g}
on
P_{g}M
which projects to
{\mathcal{F}}
along the projection
P_{g}M\to M
.

Proof.

It suffices to construct
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
locally. The connection gives a splitting
TTM\simeq TM\oplus vTM
, where
vTM
denotes the vertical tangent bundle of
TM
. Hence, locally,
P_{g}M\simeq U\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
for some
U\subset M
and
n=\dim M
. Pick
X_{1},\cdots,X_{k}
to be generators of
{\mathcal{F}}|_{U}
, and
e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}
be the local coordinate sections of
vTM|_{U}
. Now we define
{\mathcal{F}}_{g}|_{U}
to be the module generated by
X_{1},\cdots,X_{k},e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}
. The involutivity follows directly from our construction. Note the projection
P_{g}M\to M
is clearly a foliated map which just kills
e_{i}
’s, which then maps
{\mathcal{F}}_{g}
to
{\mathcal{F}}
.
∎

Lemma 19.40
.

Both
\operatorname{ev}_{0}
and
\operatorname{ev}_{1}
are foliated submersions.

Proof.

Follows from the construction that

(X_{1},\cdots,X_{k},e_{1},\cdots,e_{n})\mapsto(X_{1},\cdots,X_{k})

is surjective.
∎

Proposition 19.41
.

There exists an incomplete category of fibrant objects structure on
\mathsf{Mfd}^{{\operatorname{SFol}}}
, where

(1)

Fibrations are surjective foliated submersions.

(2)

Weak equivalences are Hausdorff Morita equivalences.

(3)

Path objects are foliated short geodesic path space.

Proof.

(2) follows from Corollary
19.40
. (3) then follows from (4) and Proposition
19.33
.

For (4), clearly isomorphisms are Hausdorff Morita equivalences, let’s prove 2-out-of-3 property. By construction, the composition of Hausdorff Morita equivalences is the fiber product which is again a Hausdorff Morita equivalences, this directly implies that, if
(M,{\mathcal{F}}_{M})\simeq(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})
and
(L,{\mathcal{F}}_{L})\simeq(N,{\mathcal{F}}_{N})
, then we have
(M,{\mathcal{F}}_{M})\simeq(L,{\mathcal{F}}_{L})
. By symmetry of the Hausdorff Morita equivalences, this will generate all cases of 2-out-of-3.

For (5), clearly isomorphisms are surjective foliated submersions, and composition of foliated submersions are again foliated submersions by definition.

For (6), given a singular foliated manifold
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
, we construct its path object
(M,{\mathcal{F}})^{\Delta[1]}
to be
(P_{g}M,{\mathcal{F}}_{g})
. By Theorem
19.38
and Lemma
19.39
, we have the following factorization

(M,{\mathcal{F}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\longrightarrow}}(P_{g}M,{\mathcal{F}}_{g})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\gamma(0),\gamma(1))}}{{\longrightarrow}}(M,{\mathcal{F}})\times(M,{\mathcal{F}})

which composes to the diagonal map.
(\gamma(0),\gamma(1))
are fibrations by Lemma
19.40
. To see
\iota
is a Hausdorff Morita equivalence, notice that the
\iota
is an embedding, and
p:P_{g}M\to\iota(M)
is a submersion. By construction
p_{-1}(\iota({\mathcal{F}}))
is exactly
{\mathcal{F}}_{g}
. Hence,
(P_{g}M,{\mathcal{F}})
itself gives a Hausdorff Morita equivalence by
(\operatorname{Id},p)
.

Finally, the trivial map
(M,{\mathcal{F}})\to*
is clearly a surjective foliated submersion, hence a fibration.
∎

We denote the
\infty
-category of singular foliated manifolds presented by this iCFO by
\mathtt{Mfd}^{{\operatorname{SFol}}}=\mathsf{Mfd}^{{\operatorname{SFol}}}[W^{-1}]
. We will use this later in constructing algebraic
K
-theory of singular foliations.

19.6.
Algebraic
K
-theory of singular foliations

In this section, we will construct the
algebraic
K
theory sheaves

\mathsf{K}
on
\mathsf{Mfd}^{{\operatorname{SFol}}}
following
[
Bun18
]
for regular foliations. Then we can calculate Algebraic
K
-theory of
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
by taking homotopy groups of
\mathsf{K}(M,{\mathcal{F}})
:

K^{\bullet}(M,{\mathcal{F}})=\pi_{-\bullet}(\big(\mathsf{K}(M,{\mathcal{F}})\big)

We consider
\mathsf{Cat}
with its Cartesian symmetric monoidal structure. Let
W
denote the class of categorical equivalence, then we get a symmetric monoidal category
\mathsf{Cat}[W^{-1}]
. We denote the category of commutative algebras in
\mathsf{Cat}[W^{-1}]
by
\mathsf{CAlg}(\mathsf{Cat}[W^{-1}])
.

Construction of algebraic
K
theory sheaves

(1)

Let
M
be a manifold, we denote
\mathsf{Vect}(M)
the category of vector bundles over
M
. Any map
f:M\to N
induces a functor
f^{*}:\mathsf{Vect}(N)\to\mathsf{Vect}(M)
. Hence, we get a stack
\mathsf{Vect}
on the site
\mathsf{Mfd}
with open covering topology. Similarly, we get a stack
\mathsf{Vect}^{{{\mathbb{C}}}{\operatorname{SFol}}}
by pullback along the forgetful functor

F:\mathsf{Mfd}^{{{\mathbb{C}}}{\operatorname{SFol}}}\to\mathsf{Mfd}

We will write
\mathsf{Vect}
for
\mathsf{Vect}^{{{\mathbb{C}}}{\operatorname{SFol}}}
for simplicity.

(2)

Similarly, we can consider the category of pairs
(V,\nabla)
of a vector bundle
V\to M
. Denote the resulting symmetric monoidal stack (with the Cartesian symmetric monoidal structure) by
\mathsf{Vect}^{\nabla}
.

(3)

Let
(M,{\mathcal{F}})\in\mathsf{Mfd}^{{{\mathbb{C}}}{\operatorname{SFol}}}
. Denote
\mathsf{Vect}^{{\operatorname{flat}}}(M,{\mathcal{F}})
the category of pairs
(V,\nabla_{I})
of a vector bundle
V\to M
and a flat partial connection
\nabla_{I}
on
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
. A foliated map
f:(M,{\mathcal{F}})\to(M^{\prime},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime})
induces a functor
f^{\prime}:\mathsf{Vect}^{{\operatorname{flat}}}(M^{\prime},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{Vect}^{{\operatorname{flat}}}(M,{\mathcal{F}})
. We get a stack
\mathsf{Vect}^{{\operatorname{flat}}}
on the site
\mathsf{Mfd}^{{{\mathbb{C}}}{\operatorname{SFol}}}
.

(4)

Finally, let
(M,{\mathcal{F}})\in\mathsf{Mfd}^{{{\mathbb{C}}}{\operatorname{SFol}}}
. Denote
\mathsf{Vect}^{{\operatorname{flat}},\nabla}(M,{\mathcal{F}})
the category of pairs
(V,\nabla_{{\mathcal{F}}})
of a vector bundle
V\to M
and a flat
{\mathcal{F}}
-connection
\nabla_{{\mathcal{F}}}
on
(M,{\mathcal{F}})
. A foliated map
f:(M,{\mathcal{F}})\to(M^{\prime},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime})
induces a functor
f^{\prime}:\mathsf{Vect}^{{\operatorname{flat}},\nabla}(M^{\prime},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{Vect}^{{\operatorname{flat}},\nabla}(M,{\mathcal{F}})
. We get a symmetric monoidal stack
\mathsf{Vect}^{{\operatorname{flat}},\nabla}
on the site
\mathsf{Mfd}^{{{\mathbb{C}}}{\operatorname{SFol}}}
.

We have the following commutative diagrams of stacks by forgetful maps
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Vect}^{{\operatorname{flat}},\nabla}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Vect}^{{\operatorname{flat}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Vect}^{\nabla}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Vect}}}

in
\mathsf{Sh}_{\mathsf{CAlg}(\mathsf{Cat}(W^{-1}))}(\mathsf{Mfd}^{{{\mathbb{C}}}{\operatorname{SFol}}})
.

(5)

Now we can apply the
K-theory machine
developed in
[
BNV13
]
, we get a commutative diagram of presheaves of spectra
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{K}(\mathsf{Vect}^{{\operatorname{flat}},\nabla})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{K}(\mathsf{Vect}^{{\operatorname{flat}}})}}
